STATE OF NEW YORK 
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC SERVICE 
 
Case 23-E-0418 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Electric Service. 
 
Case 23-G-0419 - Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and 
Regulations of Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation for Gas Service. 
 
 
STAFF INITIAL BRIEF 
 
I. 
INTRODUCTION 
A.  
Overview 
 
 
On July 31, 2023, Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corporation (Central Hudson or 
the Company) filed initial testimony, including panels and individual witnesses, and exhibits in 
support of a request to increase the Company’s annual electric revenue requirement by 
approximately $139.5 million (31.6% increase in delivery revenues or 13.3% increase in total 
revenues), and annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $41.5 million (29.8% increase 
in delivery revenues or 14.2% increase in total revenues) for the twelve months ending June 30, 
2025 (Rate Year).  Although Central Hudson identified potential sources of rate moderation, it 
did not reflect use of the rate moderators in its requested increases.  On October 2, 2023, the 
Company filed updated testimony and exhibits in support of its corrected and updated request, 
prior to rate moderation, to increase Central Hudson’s annual electric revenue requirement by 
approximately $142.0 million (32.1% increase in delivery revenues or 13.5% increase in total 
revenues), and annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $42.0 million (30.2% increase 
in delivery revenues or 14.3% increase in total revenues). 
Over the past seven months, Staff of the Department of Public Service (the 
Department) has vigorously prosecuted its statutory duty to conduct a thorough analysis of 
Central Hudson’s rate request on behalf of the Company’s customers and the State of New York.  
Necessarily, Staff’s analysis was conducted against the backdrop of issues stemming from 
Central Hudson’s 2021 deployment of its customer information system (CIS) and related 
management matters, which are currently the subject of three pending proceedings outside of 


--- Page 10 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
2 
these rate cases.1  Through this extensive analysis, Staff has determined that Central Hudson’s 
rate requests are excessive.  As explained in more detail throughout this Initial Brief, the 
Company’s requests cannot be said to be just and reasonable and are not necessary to enable it to 
provide safe, adequate, and reliable service.  At the same time, while some parties advocate that 
Central Hudson should not be allowed any rate increases whatsoever based on the pending 
proceedings related to the Company’s CIS deployment, Staff based its analysis on identifying the 
appropriate level of revenue needed to enable Central Hudson to execute its statutory duty to 
provide safe and adequate service at just and reasonable rates, and to operate consistent with 
other State policies and statutory requirements such as the Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act (CLCPA).2  
Based on the foregoing considerations, Staff recommends that the New York 
State Public Service Commission (the Commission) reduce the Company’s electric and gas 
requests, as of the evidentiary hearing in these proceedings, by approximately $73.847 million 
and $19.233 million, respectively.  Staff recommends authorizing rates for Central Hudson that 
will increase its annual electric revenues, prior to the use of any sources of rate moderation, by 
approximately $65.516 million, or an increase of approximately 14.5% in delivery revenues and 
6.2% in total revenues.  Staff recommends authorizing rates for Central Hudson that will increase 
its annual gas revenues, prior to the use of any sources of rate moderation, by approximately 
$24.943 million, or an increase of approximately 18.4% in delivery revenues and 8.6% in total 
revenues.  As discussed in more detail later, Staff anticipates filing updated revenue 
requirements as part of our Reply Brief, to capture agreements between Staff and Central 
 
1 
Matter 22-00666, Staff's Investigation into Central Hudson's Customer Information System

consumer complaints regarding billing issues following the Company’s deployment of its CIS); Case 
21-M-0541, Comprehensive Management and Operations Audit of Central Hudson Gas & Electric 
Corporation, Proposed Implementation Plan (filed May 22, 2023) (Considering the Company’s 
proposed Management Audit Implementation Plan following the release of the consultant’s report in 
the comprehensive audit of the Company); and Case 22-M-0645, Central Hudson Gas & Electric 
Corporation's Development and Deployment of Modifications to its Customer Information and 
Billing System and Resulting Impacts on Billing Accuracy, Timeliness, and Errors, Order to 
Commence Proceeding and Show Cause (issued December 15, 2022) (Investigating the Company’s 
deployment of its CIS and considering enforcement and prudence actions).

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
3 
Hudson resulting from the stipulations, as well as some additional corrections and updates as 
discussed in relevant sections of this Initial Brief.  
In support of its recommendations Staff has logged numerous hours in 
quantitative and qualitative analysis of the Company’s and other parties’ filings, issued nearly 
800 discovery requests seeking responses to multifaceted points of inquiry, held multiple 
technical conferences with the Company’s and other parties’ subject matter experts, and 
participated in ten days of evidentiary hearings during which Staff Counsel conducted cross 
examination of the Company’s and other parties’ witnesses, and Staff witnesses were subject to 
extensive cross-examination regarding their positions. 
Where appropriate, Staff has supported expenditures in the public interest and has 
similarly recommended removal or adjustment of unsupported or poorly conceived expenditures 
and programs.  Staff also proposed mechanisms necessary to protect customers and ensure the 
efficient and effective management of the Company’s operations.  It is for these reasons that the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations and adjustments as discussed herein. 
B. 
Procedural Background 
Central Hudson last filed for new rates in August, 2020.3  As part of those rate 
proceedings, the parties negotiated a three-year electric and gas rate plan (2021 Rate Plan), 
which was memorialized in a Joint Proposal filed on August 24, 2021, and adopted by the 
Commission in an Order issued November 18, 2021.4  Central Hudson is currently in the final 
year of that three-year rate plan. 
Central Hudson filed tariff leaves, initial testimony, and exhibits on July 31, 2023, 
in support of its request to increase electric and gas delivery rates for the Rate Year.  On August 
11, 2023, Central Hudson filed further initial testimony and exhibits concerning the subject of 
Site Investigation and Remediation (SIR).  On August 29, 2023, Administrative Law Judges 
(ALJs) Ashley Moreno and James A. Costello presided over a procedural conference and 
technical conference in these proceedings.  Following those conferences, the ALJs issued a 
ruling providing that the Company would file updates and corrections on September 15, 2023; 
 
3  
Cases 20-E-0428 et al., Central Hudson – 2020 Electric and Gas Rates. 
4  
Cases 20-E-0428 et al., supra, Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and 
Gas Rate Plan (issued November 18, 2021) (2021 Rate Order). 


--- Page 12 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
4 
Staff and intervenors would file testimony and exhibits on November 21, 2023; all parties would 
file rebuttal testimony and exhibits on December 19, 2023; and the evidentiary hearing would 
commence on January 9, 2024. 
On September 15, 2023, Central Hudson submitted an update filing, in narrative 
form, reflecting known changes or corrections to the Company’s initial filings on July 31, 2023 
and August 11, 2023, and reflecting the impacts of accelerating the Company’s plan to 
implement monthly meter reading pursuant to the Interim Agreement filed on July 27, 2023 in 
Case 22-M-0645.5  Central Hudson’s update filing reflected total revenue requirement increases 
of $142.0 and $42.0 for electric and gas, respectively, during the Rate Year.  Thereafter, to 
correct deficiencies identified by the ALJs regarding the form of the update filing, Central 
Hudson submitted its update filing as updated testimony and exhibits of the Company’s Policy 
Panel on October 2, 2023. 
On October 31, 2023, the Public Utility Law Project of New York, Inc. (PULP) 
filed a motion seeking to deny Central Hudson’s proposed rate increases on the basis that the 
Company’s rate filing was deficient, premature, and against the public interest in light of billing 
issues surrounding the Company’s 2021 roll-out of its CIS, pending proceedings regarding the 
CIS and management matters, and concerns relating to the accuracy of the baseline data used to 
compile Central Hudson’s rate filings.  Responses in support of PULP’s motion were filed by 
Communities for Local Power (CLP) on November 1, 2023; the Office of Assemblymember 
Sarahana Shrestha (AD103) on November 2, 2023; Beacon Climate Action Now (BCAN) on 
November 7, 2023; and Alliance for a Green Economy on November 8, 2023.  Staff and Central 
Hudson both filed responses in opposition to the motion on November 8, 2023.  On December 
19, 2023, the Commission issued an Order denying PULP’s motion on the grounds that the relief 
sought is legally impermissible and otherwise unnecessary considering the rate case process 
outlined by Public Service Law (PSL) §66 and 16 NYCRR Chapter 1. 
On November 21, 2023, Staff filed initial testimony and exhibits and proposed an 
electric revenue requirement increase, before rate moderation, for Central Hudson of 
approximately $75.0 million, or an increase of approximately 17.0% in delivery revenues and 
 
5  
Case 22-M-0645, supra, Interim Agreement (filed July 27, 2023).  The Commission issued its Order 
Adopting Terms of Interim Agreement on August 18, 2023 in the same case.

7.1% in total revenues, and a gas revenue requirement increase of approximately $25.2 million, 
or an increase of approximately 18.5% in delivery revenues and 8.7% in total revenues.  The 
following intervenors also filed initial testimony and exhibits on or around November 21, 2023: 
PULP, the County of Dutchess (Dutchess County), BCAN, AD103, CLP, Multiple Intervenors 
(MI), Walmart, Inc. (Walmart), the Town of Olive, Key Capture Energy (KCE), and Utility 
Intervention Unit of the New York Department of State’s Division of Consumer Protection 
(UIU).  On December 19, 2023, Staff filed supplemental testimony and exhibits specifically 
addressing the limited issues of forecasting post-model adjustments, and the associated price out 
and revenue requirement impacts.   
On or about December 19, 2023, Staff, the Company, CLP, and PULP filed 
rebuttal testimony and exhibits.  The Company’s rebuttal filings presented updated revenue 
requirements, wherein the Company requested an electric revenue requirement increase, before 
rate moderation, of $139.363 million, or an increase of approximately 31.5% in delivery 
revenues and 13.3% in total revenues; and a gas revenue requirement increase of $44.176 
million, or an increase of approximately 32.2% in delivery revenues and 15.2% in total revenues.  
Thereafter, on December 29, 2023, Central Hudson filed rebuttal testimony and exhibits in 
response to Staff’s December 19, 2023 supplemental testimony. 
ALJs Moreno and Costello held a further procedural conference on December 21, 
2023, and issued a Ruling on Evidentiary Hearing Date and Process on December 26, 2023.  In 
that ruling, the ALJs established a procedural schedule whereby, inter alia, corrections to 
testimony and exhibits were due on January 16, 2024; motions to strike or exclude testimony or 
exhibits were due on January 19, 2024; and the evidentiary hearing was rescheduled to begin on 
January 24, 2024.  The December 26, 2023 Ruling noted that the ALJs’ decision was made, in 
part, in consideration of parties having expressed interested in engaging in some limited 
settlement to potentially narrow the scope of issues to be addressed at the hearing. 
On January 5, 2024, Central Hudson filed a Notice of Impending Settlement 
regarding discussions to potentially narrow the scope of issues to be addressed at the evidentiary 
hearing.  Following multiple rounds of discussions, four stipulations in total were executed on 
January 24, 2024 (two by Staff and the Company, and the other two by Staff, the Company, and 
UIU), each addressing discrete issues in these rate proceedings.  Each of these stipulations were 
entered into the evidentiary record, and are discussed in more detail in this Initial Brief.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
6 
On January 16, 2024, Staff provided various corrected testimonies and exhibits 
including corrected revenue requirements, while the Company and MI also filed corrected 
testimonies and exhibits.  On January 19, 2024, the Company filed various motions to strike all 
or portions of various testimonies and exhibits filed by Staff, CLP, and the Town of Olive. 
 Thereafter, the evidentiary hearing commenced on January 24 and ran through 
February 6, 2024, for a total of ten days of hearings.  Between January 25, 2024 and February 7, 
2024, both Staff and the Company filed additional corrected copies of testimonies and exhibits, 
to reflect corrections made to those testimonies and exhibits at the hearing. 
C. 
Revenue Requirement (Appendix and Updates) 
In its corrected testimony filed on January 16, 2024, Staff recommended 
authorizing rates for Central Hudson that will increase its annual electric revenue requirement by 
approximately $65.516 million, or an increase of approximately 14.5% in delivery revenues and 
6.2% in total revenues.  Staff recommended authorizing rates for Central Hudson that will 
increase its annual gas revenue requirement by approximately $24.943 million, or an increase of 
approximately 18.4% in delivery revenues and 8.6% in total revenues. 
Staff expects to update its revenue requirements with its Reply Brief, to capture 
agreed upon changes as a result of the stipulations described above and herein, which would 
include adjustments to revenue, electric minor storm restoration operations and maintenance 
(O&M) expense, and electric net plant with a tracking adjustment to depreciation expense.  
Additionally, within this Initial Brief, Staff has identified other necessary corrections or updates 
to its positions, that will be reflected in the update of revenue requirements, including Major 
Storm Reserve amortization of regulatory asset,6 savings resulting from the implementation of 
management audit recommendations,7 and Danskammer Revenues.8

revenue requirement impact of the increases in rate base are $11.7 million for electric and $5.6

call volume overflow.  One of the major differences between the Company’s revenue 
requirement requests and Staff’s recommended revenue requirements is the capital structure.   
Central Hudson proposed a 9.8% return on equity (ROE) and 50% equity ratio, while Staff 
recommended a 9.2% ROE and 48% equity ratio.  In these rate proceedings, a single basis point, 
or BP, is worth approximately $113,000 and $47,800 for electric and gas, respectively.  Staff’s 
recommended rate of return, inclusive of recommendations on ROE, equity ratio and debt rates, 
reduces the Company’s requested revenue requirements by $9.447 million and $4.044 million for 
electric and gas, respectively.  
As explained in detail in the sections below, Staff’s recommended revenue 
requirements appropriately reflect Central Hudson’s cost of providing safe, adequate, and 
reliable service in the Rate Year.  The Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations as 
they result in just and reasonable rates for customers.

D. 
Compliance with CLCPA 
The CLCPA requires total statewide greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions to be 40% 
below 1990 levels by 2030 and 85% below such levels by 2040.16  The CLCPA also requires all 
State agencies, including the Commission, to consider the impacts of their final agency actions 
on GHG emissions and disadvantaged communities.  Specifically, pursuant to section 7(2) of the 
CLCPA, State agencies are required to consider whether their administrative approvals and 
decisions “are inconsistent with or will interfere with the attainment of the statewide greenhouse 
gas emissions limits” established in Article 75 of the ECL.  Where such decisions are deemed to 
be inconsistent with or will interfere with the attainment of such emissions limits, the agency 
must “provide a detailed statement of justification as to why such limits/criteria may not be met,” 
and “identify alternatives or greenhouse gas mitigation measures to be required where such 
project is located.”  Similarly, section 7(3) of the CLCPA requires State agencies to ensure that 
their administrative approvals and decisions will not “disproportionately burden disadvantaged 
communities” as identified pursuant to ECL §75-0101(5), and to “prioritize reductions of 
greenhouse gas emissions and co-pollutants in disadvantaged communities.”  The analyses 
required by CLCPA sections 7(2) and 7(3) must also recognize and consider the Commission’s 
core statutory obligation to ensure the provision of safe and adequate service at just and 
reasonable rates.17 
Preliminarily, in assessing whether the outcome of these rate cases (i.e., a rate 
order), is in overall alignment with the CLCPA, it is important to note that generic, state-wide

Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plans with Additional Requirements (issued July 20, 
2023), p. 95 (2023 Con Edison Rate Order); see also PSL §65(1).

consistent with the GHG emission reduction targets of the CLCPA, were similarly included in 
Staff’s recommendations here.  For example, in the most recent Niagara Mohawk Power

CLCPA goals, prioritizing leak prone pipe (LPP) removal/replacement, and imposing stringent 
leak backlog metrics.22  The 2023 Con Edison Rate Order adopted similar provisions, which the 
Commission referenced in concluding that order was also consistent with attaining the CLCPA’s 
GHG emission reduction targets.23

proceedings, in the aggregate, are consistent with the attainment of the GHG emission reduction 
targets of the CLCPA.  Overall, Staff’s recommendations “appropriately balance the interests in 
reliability, public safety, and reasonable rates with emission reductions and clean energy 
objectives, without disproportionately burdening disadvantaged communities.”24  Staff’s

II. 
REVENUE ISSUES 
A. 
Revenue/Sales/Customer Forecasts 
1. 
Electric Sales Forecast 
The Company employed econometric models to predict electric customer counts 
and sales across various service classes for the upcoming Rate Year.39  These models linked 
historical customer and sales data to economic indicators such as employment, the number of 
households, and income levels.  Forecasts for electric sales volumes were calculated on a per-
customer basis for larger classes, while total class figures were used for others, including 
industrial demand, non-demand, and transmission & switching (T&S) customers.  Informed by 
regional economic predictions from Moody's Analytics and data on technology saturation and 
efficiency, the models used actual billed sales as the dependent variable.  Customers were 
segmented into electric service classes and further into rate codes, capturing variations in service 
offerings and pricing.  The Company’s forecast anticipated a moderate 1.8% growth in electric 
sales for the Rate Year, primarily due to the uptake of electric vehicles (EVs) and heat pumps.  
While residential and commercial forecasts employed end-use data, industrial and large 
transmission customer projections were based on historical trends and direct feedback.  The 
Company also employed the use of Statistically Adjusted End-Use (SAE) variables, developed 
using proprietary software algorithms that combined Company-specific data along with 
demographic and economic data from other sources to produce the variables.40   Post-forecast 
 
39  Tr. 1798. 
40  Tr. 1805-1806. 


--- Page 24 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
16 
adjustments adjusted for increased EV use, distributed solar impact, and load profile changes due 
to heat pumps.41  
Staff reviewed the Company’s electric forecasts and raised concerns with the 
Company’s use of SAE variables in its models.  The SAE variables aggregated numerous 
underlying variables, including weather, energy prices, and other economic and demographic 
data.  The use of these aggregated variables made the Company’s models non-transparent and 
prevented the evaluation of the impact of the individual variables, which is important for 
evaluating the model for reasonableness.  Staff also raised concerned that the SAE variables 
assumed fixed relationships between variables that were not directly included in the models.   
Considering these concerns, Staff developed and utilized its own economic 
models to forecast Central Hudson’s customers and sales.  Staff forecasted aggregated residential 
heat, residential non-heat, commercial demand, and commercial non-demand rate groups for 
electricity.42  Staff used updated historical data through September 2023, and directly used the 
underlying, unmodified economic data as explanatory variables.43  This increases the 
transparency of the models because each economic variable is included as a distinct explanatory 
variable, and not combined with other economic and survey saturation variables, to allow for an 
interpretable associated coefficient.  This also allows a more straight-forward and transparent 
way to evaluate the model results and forecasts. 
As explained in the testimony of the Staff Updated Forecasting and Price Out 
Panel (SUFPP), Staff further identified corrections needed to the Company’s post-model 
adjustments.  Specifically, Staff found that the Company inappropriately adjusted its model 
outputs by cumulative levels of adoptions for EVs and solar photovoltaics (PV).44  Staff further 
found that the Company’s post-model adjustment calculations for PVs, EVs, and heat pumps did 
not reflect the inclusion of the most recent nine months of available actual data through 
September of 2023.45  Staff incorporated these corrections into its post-model adjustments, and

Gas Sales Forecast

forecasting for each service class within the upcoming Rate Year, based on historical monthly 
billed data up to December 2022.  The Company separately estimated sales class-specific 
customer count and use-per-customer models and then multiplied the forecasted customer counts 
and sales per-customer projections from those models to produce overall sales forecasts for each 
service class.   These forecasting models incorporated economic drivers and integrated end-use

C. 
Revenue Tracking Mechanisms 
The Electric Revenue Decoupling Mechanism (RDM) is a revenue per class 
model applicable to Service Classification Nos. 1, 2 Non-Demand, 2 Primary Demand, 2 
Secondary Demand, 3, 5, 6, 8 13 and 14.  Actual class or sub-class delivery revenues are 
compared on a monthly basis to a delivery revenue target.  If the monthly actual delivery revenue 
exceeds or falls short of the delivery revenue target, the delivery revenue excess or shortfall is 
accrued for a refund or recovery to customers at the end of the semi-annual RDM period (six 
months ending December or six months ending June).56  The Company proposed to include 
Service Classification 10 – Buyback Service into its RDM.57  Additionally, Staff recommended 
tariff language related to the Company’s RDM during months a rate change occurs.58   The 
inclusion of Service Classification No. 10, and Staff’s modified tariff language to the RDM, are

III. 
EXPENSE ISSUES 
A. 
Operation and Maintenance 
1. 
Areas of Agreement 
Legal Service expenses include costs of non-rate case recurring legal expenses.  
These costs were forecasted by normalizing the Historic Test Year and then applying inflation to 
get to the Rate Year.  As stated in the response to DPS-414, the Company understated its 
downward normalizing adjustments.61  After Staff corrected for this understatement, the 
normalizing adjustments changed from $312,519 to $384,189 for electric and from $62,504 to 
$96,047 for gas.  This correction results in a reduction to Central Hudson’s Rate Year forecast of 
legal services of $71,670 for electric and $33,543 for gas.  The Company and Staff are in 
agreement with this correction.62 
Other issues on which Staff is in agreement with the Company are discussed in 
the relevant sections of this brief. 
2. 
Site Investigation and Remediation (SIR) 
In February of 2011, the Commission commenced a proceeding (Case 11-M-
0034) to examine, on a generic basis, the treatment of costs for utilities’ site investigation and 
remediation of hazardous waste, primarily at former manufactured gas plant (MGP) sites 
previously owned and operated by the utility companies or their predecessor companies.  SIR 
projects, including investigation and remediation of former MGP sites, are primarily overseen by 
 
60  Tr. 1870. 
61  Tr. 4112. 
62  Exhibit 239, Schedule A, p. 4; Exhibit 240, Schedule A, p. 3. 


--- Page 29 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
21 
the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (DEC).  The United States 
Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) regulates the investigation and remediation of Federal 
Superfund sites.  Central Hudson’s SIR expenses generally consist of the costs of assessment, 
monitoring, cleanup, and restoration of sites containing environmental contamination for which 
the Company has been found to be wholly or partially liable by the DEC or EPA.  The Company 
then seeks recovery of these SIR expenses through rates. 
Central Hudson is wholly or partially responsible for seven MGP sites, of which 
six have received a “No Further Action” (NFA) determination with minimal ongoing obligation; 
one maintenance/warehouse facility, also with an NFA determination; and one customer service 
office.  The one outstanding MGP site is North Water Street, which has experienced various 
practical and regulatory setbacks in recent years and for which remediation is ongoing.  The 
Company projected spending $0.8 million on this site in the Rate Year. 
Central Hudson projected Rate Year SIR costs using a historical three-year 
average of actual costs, adjusted for inflation.  The Company explained that this methodology 
has been in place since Cases 08-E-0887 and 08-G-0888 and is consistent with the 2021 Rate 
Plan.63  The Company also proposed continuation of the existing symmetrical true-up 
mechanism for SIR costs.64  In its Policy Panel’s initial testimony, the Company stated that in the 
context of a multi-year settlement, as a source of rate moderation, it would consider using a 
forecast of Rate Year spending to project SIR costs, which would reduce electric and gas revenue 
requirements by $4 million.65   
Staff recommends using the forecast of Rate Year spending instead of a historical 
three-year average, because the historical expenses include costs for work at sites which are now 
completed, as well as other costs which will not occur in the Rate Year.66  In developing that 
recommendation, Staff relied upon the Company’s projected costs in the Rate Year of $0.789 
million for electric and $0.197 million for gas, as provided in the response to DPS-291.67   
 
63  Tr. 718, 3429. 
64  Tr. 3429,3430. 
65  Tr. 3299-3300. 
66  Tr. 3496-3497. 
67  Tr. 3497. 


--- Page 30 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
22 
In rebuttal, Central Hudson disagreed with using forecasted costs, arguing that 
SIR costs fluctuate from year to year depending on projects, and that funding based on the 
historical average in this case will act as a reserve and reduce potential volatility of SIR costs in 
the future.68  Additionally, the Company stated that it originally noted using a forecasted 
methodology to calculate Rate Year costs in the context of a multi-year rate plan only.69  Staff’s 
proposed methodology, however, is a better indicator of Rate Year spending and should be 
adopted by the Commission in the context of this case.  The Company’s proposed use of a 
historical spending average would inappropriately factor prior spending that is not anticipated to 
continue into the calculation of forecasted spending in the Rate Year.  Conversely, Staff’s 
recommended methodology considers the changes in the statuses of the Company’s SIR sites, 
and therefore does not include costs for sites which have been fully remediated, or for which 
historical costs are not otherwise representative of future expenses.  Staff’s forecast is based on 
the scopes of SIR work and associated costs the Company expects to incur during the Rate Year 
and is a reasonable forecast methodology in this one-year rate case.  For the foregoing reasons, 
the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation and use the forecast of Rate Year 
spending instead of a historical three-year average to establish the Company’s SIR expense 
allowance for the Rate Year. 
Staff also assessed Central Hudson’s compliance with the Inventory of Best 
Practices, as developed in Case 11-M-0034.70  Based on Staff’s analysis, the Company is largely 
in compliance with the Inventory of Best Practices, although Staff made two recommendations 
for better adherence to items nine (“work with other utilities to share the costs of research 
evaluating remedial technologies and risks from MGP contamination and other contaminants”) 
and twelve (“only use waste disposal facilities that have been pre-approved by the utility to 
minimize the potential for creating new liabilities”).71  Regarding item nine, the Company stated 
in its response to DPS-297 that it had not identified any instances where the Company has been

waste disposal facilities currently utilized by the Company, some of which have sub-par records 
and histories of infractions.74  Given the purpose of waste disposal facilities, and the potential 
deleterious impacts that could arise if waste is handled improperly, it is critical that the Company 
takes appropriate actions to ensure that such facilities utilized by the Company as part of its SIR 
program comply with federal, state, and local standards and regulations, including but not limited 
to those promulgated by the federal Environmental Protection Agency and state departments of 
environmental conservation.  To ameliorate Staff’s concerns with the waste disposal facilities 
currently utilized by the Company, Staff recommends that the Commission direct the Company 
to undertake a cost-benefit analysis of the continued use of some of these facilities, as compared 
to the cost of switching to waste facilities with fewer liabilities.75  Such an analysis will present 
useful information regarding whether it is appropriate for the Company to continue using its 
current facilities, and that can help to “minimize the potential for creating new liabilities,” in 
accordance with item twelve of the Inventory of Best Practices.   
In addition, in its November 28, 2012 Order issued in Case 11-M-0034, the

expenses, it must indicate the results of any internal process the utility may have conducted with 
respect to review of SIR procedures, and explain how internal controls are brought to bear on the

3. 
Vegetation Management 
Central Hudson’s Vegetation Management Program is separated into two main 
components, Transmission Right of Way (ROW) Management and Distribution Line Clearance 
Program.  Both components consist of smaller individual activities.  Transmission ROW 
Management includes ROW Routine Floor, Mowing/Access Roads, Side Trimming, Danger 
Tree Removal, Hot Spot Trimming, Edge Reclamation, and Legal and Environmental 
Activities.78  Distribution Line Clearance Program includes the following activities: Trimming 
On-Road, Trimming Off-Road, Danger Trees, Flagging, Hazard Tree-Flagging, Trouble Orders, 
and Hazard Tree Program.79   
Transmission ROW Management   
The Company forecasted expenditures for Transmission ROW Management of 
approximately $3.60 million in the Rate Year.80  The Company forecasted these expenditure 
levels using historical expenditures from past rate years and factoring in contractual labor cost 
increases, inflation, and operational and environmental constraints.  The contractual labor 
increases were based on union contract agreements with the Local 1249 B Chapter and internal

associated with labor contracts, the Company’s proposed expenditures are generally within 
historical spending levels,82 with the exception of the Company’s proposed Rate Year 
expenditure of $194,287 for the Hot Spot Trimming activity, which represents a significant cost 
increase from historical levels.83  Based on its review, Staff noted that the Company continues to 
identify significant amounts of undesirable vegetation underneath transmission lines that must be 
addressed by hot spot work, which indicates that trees are likely being missed during regular 
maintenance intervals based off visual observations of the ROWs.84  Notwithstanding the 
foregoing, Staff recommends that the Commission adopt the recommended funding level of 
$194,287 for Hot Spot Trimming in the Rate Year, in order to enable the Company to continue 
performing hot spot work, which is important for the Company to maintain the transmission 
system’s reliability, and to prevent future increases of the same magnitude.85  Staff also 
recommends, and the Company agreed,86 that the Commission direct Central Hudson to continue 
its efforts related to improving the performance of contractors during regular maintenance 
operations, by which vegetation will not resprout and grow into the wire zone, which would 
enable the Company to reduce the budget for Hot Spot Trimming in future years.87   
Based on the foregoing, the Commission should adopt the Company’s forecasted 
Rate Year expenditures of approximately $3.60 million for Transmission ROW Management.

other insects and tree related diseases, as well as damage from severe weather events, continues 
to be the main sources for this impact.  The intent of the Hazard Tree Program is to focus more 
on addressing hazard/danger trees outside of our routine trimming program that could improve 
system reliability.”91  The ECOP also reasoned that since the inception of the Hazard Tree 
Program in 2018, there has been an overall SAIFI improvement of 17.2%.92  Central Hudson also 
referred to a consultant’s recommendations on how to improve its program, including increasing 
the Hazard Tree Program currently in place.

data and the Company’s current five Hazard Tree crews, Staff calculated that each crew was able 
to remove 750 trees on average per calendar year.  Thus, allowing three additional Hazard Tree 
crews, bringing the total program crewing from five to eight, would enable the Company to 
remove an additional 2,250 hazard trees annually, and would provide the Company with 
significant future hazard tree mitigation resources.94  On January 16, 2024, the Company and 
Staff executed the Stipulation Regarding Electric Capital and Operations (Electric Capital 
Stipulation), wherein Central Hudson, inter alia, agreed to Staff’s recommendation to increase 
the Company’s hazard tree crewing from five to eight crews.95  For all those reasons, the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended adjustments to the Company’s Hazard Tree 
program for the Rate Year.

the Department of Labor,97 Lineman-Tree Trimmers serving as “Flag Person” in their duties 
have consistently maintained New York State minimum wage as their prevailing wage amount.

$26.26 million in the Rate Year for the Distribution Line Clearance Program, which is in line

continue improving system reliability through the removal of hazard trees within its service 
territory.   
Reporting 
Staff recommends that Central Hudson provide additional Distribution Line 
Clearance Program reporting on a quarterly basis, to provide a clear view into the Company’s 
use of resources, progress with program goals, and to assist with analysis in future proceedings.98  
These reports should be filed with the Secretary to the Commission on a quarterly basis, no later 
than 45 days after each calendar quarter.  Reporting requirements should include trimming on-
road, trimming off-road, danger tree removal, flagging, hazard tree flagging, trouble orders, and 
hazard tree removals.  These reports should contain monthly totals of miles completed and 
expenditures in a five-year annual historical format, plus the current year’s monthly totals of 
miles completed and expenditures.  Staff also recommends that additional reporting on hazard 
tree crew activities during major and minor storm events be provided.  These reports should 
include the date of the event, duration of the event, number of full-time equivalents used, service 
divisions affected, number of trees/limbs removed, and total expenditures for the event, 
separated by labor included in contracts, and labor excluded, for example, overtime.99  Staff

Grid Mod, Network Strategy, and Interconnections.”110  The Company requested five Assistant

Staff reviewed historical and forecasted capital expenditures, historical and

forecasted capital expenditures remain flat compared to the Company’s historical spending 
levels.  The Company’s request would increase the headcount of the Assistant Engineer (Grid 
Mod) position by 166%, and the Assistant Engineer (Substation) position by 100%, compared to 
the current headcount.  Additionally, Assistant Engineer (Grid Mod) and Assistant Engineer 
(Substation) positions share common responsibilities which provides the Company the ability to 
share the workload among the two positions.111  Considering these factors, Staff recommended 
one Assistant Engineer (Grid Mod) and one Assistant Engineer (Substation) be allowed.

projects related to the NYISO interconnection queue.112  The Company stated that while the

Assistant Engineer (Hydro) 
The role of Assistant Engineer (Hydro) would oversee small capital construction 
projects within the Hydro & Gas Turbines category.  The duties of this position would include 
project scoping, bidding, material and labor procurement, quality control, financial tracking, and 
closeout documentation.113  Additionally, the position would provide engineering support for 
regulatory compliance items, provide reporting for various regulatory agencies, manage 
hydroelectric grant applications, and work with operations to provide engineering support for 
projects and emergent needs to maintain plant operation.  Central Hudson requested one 
Assistant Engineer (Hydro) FTE. 
Staff reviewed historical and forecasted capital expenditures, historical and 
current FTE headcount, and the roles and responsibilities of the position.  The forecasted five-
year capital expenditures for the Hydro & Gas Turbines category slightly decreased, on average, 
compared to the capital costs in the Joint Proposal adopted by the Commission in Central 
Hudson’s previous rate case.114  The Company’s request would also double the current 
headcount for this position.  Given these facts, Staff recommended zero additional Assistant 
Engineer (Hydro) positions be authorized.

tasks.115  These tasks would consume much of their working time.  While the Company generally 
agreed with Staff’s position that its forecasted capital expenditures have marginally decreased as

I&C Technician (Substation) and Relay Technician (Substation) 
The role of I&C Technician (Substation) supports work related to testing, 
adjusting repairing and maintenance of radio, telemetric, fiber optic, microwave, supervisory 
control and data acquisition (SCADA) equipment in substations and other facilities.  The role of 
Relay technician (Substation) supports work related to testing, adjusting, and repairing of relays 
and associated equipment in substations and other facilities.  According to the Company, the 
I&C and Relay Technicians positions did not exist prior to 2022 as they were all classified as 
“Substation Technicians” who performed the responsibilities mentioned above.  The Company 
explained that the Substation Technician role was split into I&C Technician and Relay 
Technician to allow for more specialized training.117  Central Hudson requested four I&C 
Technician (Substation) and two Relay Technician (Substation) FTEs.

which was provided in DPS-725.118  The Company currently has three I&C Technicians and 
eight Relay Technicians.  These existing technicians have the knowledge and knowhow to 
handle the work at the present, considering that the delineation of Substation Technicians into 
I&C Technicians and Relay Technicians was a recent action.  Additionally, the Company has 
been able to meet its I&C and relay needs through 2022 with its historical Substation Technician

cited to “recent turnover within the Substations Technicians [that] has reduced the complement 
below historical levels and attracting and retaining qualified individuals has proven difficult.”122   
In addition to these responsibilities, the Company further argued that Relay Technicians that are 
grandfathered Substation Technicians continue to support some of the I&C Technicians’ work, 
until the desired complement is achieved with the I&C Technicians, but the I&C work being 
supported by the Relay Technicians is too great of a burden and cannot be performed on an 
ongoing basis. 123   
As discussed earlier, the Company has been able to meet its overall workload with 
the present headcount.  Additionally, Staff’s recommendation of one I&C Technician should 
allow the Company to address any increase in workload while relieving, at least in part, the 
Relay Technicians who are supporting I&C related work.  Staff is also confident in the 
Company’s ability to manage its concerns about positional turnover, given statements by the 
Company’s Revenue Requirements Panel that “the Company has a proven history of hiring the 
employees needed to safely and effectively run the business.”124  Accordingly, Staff maintains its 
recommendation that the Commission authorize one I&C Technician (Substation) and zero 
Relay Technician (Substation) FTEs.

Distribution Operators and Emergency Service Representatives 
In its initial testimony, the Company discussed “the expansion of Central 
Dispatch operation and the buildout of Distribution System Operations” as “essential to ensure 
24x7 monitoring and control with” the Company’s Advanced Distribution Management System 
(ADMS).125  The Company identified a need for onboarding ten classified employees (eight 
Assistant Distribution Operators, two Emergency Service Representatives and one Senior 
Distribution Operator), in order to provide for three eight-hour operating shifts to provide 
adequate 24/7, 365 day coverage.126  The Company stated that “these positions will be filled over 
a three-year period to align with the deliverables of Grid Modernization and the in-service of the 
Primary Control Center in Lake Katrine.”127   
In its initial testimony, Staff recommended that four incremental Assistant 
Distribution Operators be authorized, including one already hired by the Company on June 25, 
2023, one to be hired in the Rate Year, and two in the subsequent twelve months.128  Staff noted 
that the Company already employs three Distribution Operators and one Assistant Distribution 
Operator working in its Primary Control Center, with the responsibility of operating the 
ADMS.129  Staff also noted that the purpose of the ADMS is to automate the electric system, and 
is designed to improve operator efficiencies and, therefore, requires less human intervention.130  
As such, Staff did not agree that the Company’s FTE proposal aligned with the purpose of the 
technology. 
In rebuttal, the Company argued that “Assistant Distribution Operators are 
required to support incremental workstreams associated with transitioning the distribution 
operating authority from five district offices to a single Distribution Control Center.”131  While 
the Company acknowledged that the ADMS provides operating efficiencies, Central Hudson 
maintained that centralizing the distribution operating authority would require incremental

Staff maintains that the recommended four incremental Assistant Distribution 
Operators or eight total Distribution Operators is appropriate 24x7 coverage of the Distribution 
Control Center.  While the Company argued that these FTEs are needed to operate the ADMS, 
the Company also acknowledged that the ADMS would be operated in advisory mode.  In Staff’s 
opinion, however, the Company is being overly conservative in its estimation of how many FTEs 
are needed to operate the ADMS.  The ADMS does not need to be in advisory mode indefinitely.  
As noted above, the purpose of the ADMS is to automate the electric system, improve operator 
efficiencies, and reduce the need for human intervention.  Instead, at some point, the ADMS can 
and should transition to operate in greater autonomous capacity, thereby no longer needing 
constant, full-time monitoring by Distribution Operators as the Company suggests.  Using the 
ADMS to perform automated tasks such as VVO and FLISR provides operating effectiveness, 
which will reduce control room workload.  Staff does not support ramping up the number of 
Assistant Distribution Operators just for a brief advisory mode period.

June 2025, and one FTE through June 2026, or two FTEs in total.  The primary responsibility of 
the Emergency Service Representative is to facilitate 24/7 communication between customers

and the Primary Control Center staff.135  The Company currently employs eight Emergency

which is more than adequate to support 24x7 operation, particularly when considering the 
synergies between Distribution Operators, Distribution Dispatchers, and Emergency Service 
Representatives workstreams.  Staff is confident that the Company will have adequate 
Emergency Service Representatives to meet its ongoing operational needs, and therefore 
maintains its recommendation of zero additional Emergency Services Representatives.

Gas Capital and Operations FTEs 
The Company proposed adding eight incremental FTEs related to gas operations, 
as follows: one Engineering Technician, one Natural Gas Compliance Analyst (NG Compliance 
Analyst), one Foreman/Forewoman, three Assistant Engineers, one Operation Supervisor 
dedicated to Damage Prevention and Public Awareness Plan (Operations Supervisor), and one 
Damage Prevention Patroller (Patroller).  Among the eight proposed FTEs, the Company has 
already hired one Assistant Engineer and one NG Compliance Analyst.   
Staff agrees with the Company’s proposal to hire the Engineering Technician to 
offset contractor resources, which would allow the Company to save costs on contracting 
cathodic testing and troubleshooting and help the Company build internal skills.  Staff also 
agrees with the Company’s hiring of one Assistant Engineer on June 26, 2023, and an additional 
Assistant Engineer who will be mainly managing integrity testing for the Company’s High 
Consequence Areas and Moderate Consequence Areas, as well as auditing TIMP and DIMP 
annually for program compliance in addition to other tasks.140  Additionally, Staff agrees with 
the Company’s proposal to hire one Operation Supervisor to assist in achieving Staff’s 
recommendations for damage prevention targets.141 
Staff disagrees with the Company’s proposal to hire a third Assistant Engineer 
with assigned tasks of participating in the implementation of a Pipeline Safety Management 
System (PSMS) by execution of tasks within the PSMS Road Map in development by the 
Company,142 participating in industry meetings, serving as PSMS ambassador within the 
Company, and applying PSMS principles to aspects of Utility Thermal Energy Networks 
(UTEN).143  The Company failed to provide any evidence of tangible progress in the 
development and implementation of its PSMS, as noted in Section IV.H, below.  The UTEN 
proceeding is also still in a relatively nascent stage, and it is unclear at this time how and to what 
extent the Company could apply PSMS principles to UTENs.

provide any justification for hiring two FTEs with different titles to fulfill the same tasks.146  The

programs.147  For those reasons, Staff recommended denying the Company’s proposal to hire an 
NG Compliance Analyst.

Common Capital and Operations FTEs 
Staff recommends that the following incremental FTEs proposed by the Company 
and listed in the Company’s response to DPS-260 be disallowed: a Data & Analytics 
Lead/Program Manager found on row 50, an Analytics Microsoft Data Engineer/Architect found 
on row 66, an Analytics and Data Engineer #2 on row 69, an Analytics PowerBI Developer 
found on row 70, and a Business Analyst found on row 76.160  All of these incremental FTEs

the stated purpose and reported responsibilities for these FTEs were inconsistent with the 
incremental work necessary for the Company to provide data to the IEDR.161  In any event, 
although the Company asserted in rebuttal that these FTEs are necessary to achieve “achieve the 
IEDR requirements as defined in the Company’s IEDR Phase 2 budget submittal,”162 the 
Commission has since issued an Order on January 19, 2024 in Case 20-M-0082, wherein the 
Commission specifically directed that the recovery of costs associated with Phase 2 of the IEDR 
be addressed in subsequent rate cases after Phase 2 has been completed.163  Accordingly, costs 
associated with these five incremental FTEs related to the Company’s implementation of IEDR 
Phase 2 should be disallowed.

initiatives, including monthly meter reading,171 billing,172 collections activities,173 and customer 
outreach and education.174  For monthly meter reading, the Company stated it would need to 
double the number of current meter reading FTEs to transition to monthly meter reading,175

with bi-monthly billing estimates”176 and these internal and external contracted resources would 
be hired prior to the Rate Year.  For billing, the Company stated that 11 FTEs “are required in 
order to investigate an estimated 10% of the collections calls in order to provide customers with 
greater certainty regarding the accuracy of their arrears balances.”177  The Company also 
proposed five incremental customer outreach FTEs: one director, one supervisor, and three 
outreach representatives.  
In the Staff Consumer Services Panel’s (SCSP) initial testimony, Staff disagreed 
with all of the Company’s proposed customer service positions, with the exception of three 
outreach representatives.  The SCSP expressed concerns with the proposed amount of monthly 
meter reading personnel and the Company’s lack of a plan regarding the restart of collections

Billing  
The Company asserted that additional FTEs, which would supplement the current 
Billing Department of 20 FTEs,187 are necessary to “support manual processes, testing, and 
maintenance associated with new mandated complex billing structures on an ongoing basis.”188  
However, the Company’s rebuttal offered no additional justification for the proposed positions, 
such as a list of specific job tasks, a granular breakdown of time for those job tasks, or any 
further details.  The Company also pointed to the Commission’s October 13, 2023 Order 
Establishing Updated Standby Service Rates and Implementing Optional Mass Market Demand 
Rates in Case 15-E-0751 for the premise that it establishes new complex billing structures,189 
which we note was issued and became effective after the Company’s initial testimony was filed 
in July, and requires compliance before the start of the Rate Year.190  
The Company’s argument that CDG billing requirements are likely to evolve is 
speculative at this time, and adding incremental FTEs in this rate proceeding based on the 
supposition that requirements may change is inappropriate when the Company can propose such 
positions in a future rate proceeding to address actual needs as they arise.  Approving additional 
billing FTEs when the Company already has a Customer Billing Department of 20 FTEs, as well 
as an upgraded CIS that should seamlessly be able to process complex billing, is not appropriate 
at this time.  Therefore, we recommend the Commission adopt Staff’s position to reject all of 
these incremental billing FTEs in this rate proceeding.

As described in Section X.E, Staff is concerned with the Company’s proposed

if the Company relies on a manual process for residential service terminations, which could

b. Vacancy Rate 
In the Workforce, Compensation and Benefits Panel’s initial testimony, the 
Company stated its rate of attrition, or vacancy rate, had increased from 5% to 8.1% and 8.9% in 
2021 and 2022, respectively.214  The Workforce, Compensation and Benefits Panel noted that the 
increase is significant and impactful, and that, although the Company made 100 external hires 
during 2021 and 150 in 2022, total headcount only increased by 12 in 2021 and 52 in 2022.215  
The Company, however, did not make a specific attrition adjustment to its Rate Year forecast of 
labor expense.  
Staff’s testimony explained that although Central Hudson’s projection of labor is 
based on annualized salaries and wages of its employees, projecting based on annual salaries at 
the end of the Historic Test Year does not account for periods of time during the year when a 
position was vacant, particularly when the Company pointed out that its vacancy rate had 
increased in recent years.  Therefore, an attrition adjustment was necessary to reflect normal 
operations during the Rate Year.216  Staff recommended adjusting Central Hudson’s labor 
projection to include a vacancy rate of 3.5%.  This rate represents a five-year average of attrition, 
excluding retirements, from 2019 to September of 2023.217   
In rebuttal, Central Hudson disagreed with applying a vacancy rate for multiple 
reasons.  First, the Company disagreed with Staff’s rate and stated the rate should be calculated 
using actual headcount compared to the headcount allowed in the prior rate case, so that it 
represents the amounts funded by ratepayers.218  Using this method, the Company determined 
that no vacancy rate should be applied, since its headcount was higher than the level included in 
rates.219  Additionally, the Company argued that labor is a component of the productivity 
adjustment, and applying a vacancy rate in addition to the productivity adjustment further 
reduces labor expense and is not appropriate.220  The Company further asserted that applying the 
 
214  Tr. 324. 
215  Tr. 324. 
216  Tr. 4058-4059. 
217  Exhibit 299 (DPS-380); Exhibit 489 (DPS-764). 
218  Tr. 777-778.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
52 
vacancy rate to incremental employees is unwarranted because the Company staggered its hiring 
of new employees to account for the employees not being present for the entire Rate Year, and 
therefore, applying a vacancy rate on this would be a double count of the timing difference.221  
Furthermore, the Company stated that a vacancy rate would be redundant to Staff’s proposed 
labor reconciliation.222  
Staff disagrees with Central Hudson’s rebuttal arguments and maintains that a 
vacancy rate is a necessary reduction to forecast Rate Year labor expense.  Central Hudson’s 
proposal of using headcount compared to the amount allowed in rates is not appropriate for 
projecting future rates.  Although the Company had more FTEs on site than the number allowed 
in rates during the Historic Test Year, that does not mean there was no level of vacancy at the 
Company.  A vacancy rate adjustment is necessary to account for the routine time lapses as FTEs 
change positions or leave the Company.  Additionally, productivity and vacancy do not account 
for the same things.  Productivity is meant to capture unidentified or unquantifiable productivity 
gains, efficiencies, and cost savings that could be realized during a utility’s Rate Year, but is not 
necessarily tied to labor, and does not capture the portion of positions vacant during the Rate 
Year.223  Further, the vacancy rate applies to total labor expense and should apply to new hires as 
well because, like all FTEs, they can also leave employment at any time.  Since Central Hudson 
staggered the hiring of incremental employees, the vacancy rate fairly applies to only the portion 
of salary included in the Rate Year labor expense.  In addition, Staff’s recommended downward-
only labor reconciliation is meant to recover ratepayer funds for positions that do not get filled in 
the Rate Year, while the vacancy rate reduces total labor expense to account for positions that are 
vacant for a portion of the year, and therefore, the application of the reconciliation does not 
offset the need for a vacancy adjustment.   
Staff’s methodology of using a historic average of the Company’s actual non-
retirement attrition rates to calculate our recommended vacancy rate of 3.5% is reasonable 
because the Company’s historic rates capture routine vacancy at the Company from all

employees on the payroll.  For those reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommendations regarding vacancy rate. 
 
c. Labor Distribution Rate (% of labor charged to capital) 
Central Hudson’s distribution of Rate Year labor costs to capital, expense, and 
other was based on the normalized distribution of payroll costs at the end of the Historic Test 
Year.  Part of the Company’s normalizations made to the Historic Test Year were to reflect the 
expected capital and expense ratios for its requested incremental FTEs.224  
In initial testimony, Staff recommended adjusting the distribution of labor to an 
average of Central Hudson’s historic rates.  The average included actual rates from 2020 to 2022 
with no normalizations.225  Staff specifically excluded the normalization for expected new hires 
because of Central Hudson’s request to hire a significant amount of incremental FTEs, and based 
on the fact that the specific positions that would ultimately be hired, and the actual distributions 
for those incremental FTEs, is unknown at this time. 
In rebuttal testimony, Central Hudson stated its disagreement with Staff’s 
adjustment, arguing that over a 30-year span, the normalized Historic Test Year has been used by 
Central Hudson to forecast Rate Year labor distribution, and that Staff has accepted this method 
in those cases.226  Additionally, Central Hudson argued that using a historic three-year average 
does not reflect allocation of the current workforce and ignores the projected effect of 
incremental employees on the distribution.227  Central Hudson also stated that operating results 
for the rate period should be predicated on historical test year with normalizing adjustments, and 
cited the Commission’s 1977 policy statement in Case 26821 on test periods in a major rate 
proceeding.228  The Company did agree that the incremental headcount that will be ultimately 
approved and included in the Rate Year is uncertain, but asserted that Staff had the information 
needed to adjust the normalizations based on Staff’s proposed level of incremental FTEs.  The 
Company also stated Staff had previously recommended a normalization for incremental FTEs in

d. Wage Increases 
i. 
Union Employees 
In its Rate Year labor projection, Central Hudson applied wage increases of 
2.25% in 2024 and 3% in 2025 for its International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers (IBEW) 
Union employees.  For Systems Operations Union employees, a 4.5% increase was applied.234  
The union contract covering wage increases for the Systems Operations Union employees 
expires March 31, 2024.235  Central Hudson explained it applied a 4.5% increase, consistent with 
its executive and non-union management employees, based on a recommendation from its 
consultant, Mercer, who recommended a wage increase of between 4% and 5%.236  Additionally, 
historic actual wage increases of 2.5% were given each year from 2021 to 2023 for system 
operations employees.237   
In its testimony, Staff recommended wage increases of 2.25% in 2024 and 3% in 
2025 for the Systems Operation Union employees.238  Staff’s recommended 2.25% and 3% wage 
increases are consistent with the IBEW Union increases, and better align with the actual rates 
received historically for systems operations employees.

e. Labor Reconciliation Mechanism 
Staff recommends a new downward-only reconciliation mechanism for labor 
expense, due to the significant number of incremental FTEs being allowed in Staff’s 
testimony.251  If incremental positions are not filled, the Company would be required to defer the 
labor expense underspending, for future disposition by the Commission.252  
The Company’s rebuttal testimony noted that it has a proven history of hiring the 
employees needed to safely and effectively run its business.253  The Company believes this 
mechanism is unnecessary in a one-year rate case and states that if a mechanism is adopted, it 
should not be adopted as proposed by Staff because Staff’s recommended calculation is flawed.  
Specifically, the Company explained that Staff’s calculation does not consider the timing of 
when employees are actually employed by the Company, and in the event an FTE is employed 
during the year but is absent at the end of the year, the mechanism would assume that position 
was vacant the entire year.254  Additionally, the Company stated that the mechanism “disregards 
the distribution of labor expense assumed in the development of rates.”255

A downward-only labor reconciliation is appropriate in the Rate Year.  
Considering the significant number of requested FTEs in this case, and the large amount of FTEs 
that Staff recommends, a downward-only reconciliation mechanism would protect ratepayers 
from funding vacant positions in the event that the Company is unable to fill each incremental 
position.  If the Company has no issues with its hiring as it claims, and fills the positions allowed 
in this case, then the mechanism would not cause any deferral of funds.  Staff also does not agree 
with Central Hudson’s concerns regarding the calculation of this reconciliation.  Given the 
substantial amount of incremental FTEs recommended, the Company’s headcount should trend 
upwards, and unless the Company also anticipates a significant turnover in its existing workforce 
during the Rate Year, Staff does not believe there would be any issue with timing, or with a 
significant number of employees being assumed vacant for the entire year.  The calculation 
should be modified to allocate the deferral based on the final distribution of labor established for 
the Rate Year.  The reconciliation will also need to be updated to reflect the Commission’s final 
decisions for labor expense, headcount, the distribution of labor, and medical benefits. 
f. Variable Compensation 
The Commission has stated that, in order to receive recovery of the costs of an 
incentive pay program, a utility must demonstrate that its overall compensation, inclusive of 
incentive pay, is reasonable relative to peer companies.256  Utilities can demonstrate the 
reasonableness of their incentive pay plans by providing a compensation study of similarly 
situated companies.  However, even if the utility produces a study showing that its compensation 
is reasonably in line with that of its peers, the Commission stated that an incentive pay plan will 
not be deemed reasonable if it includes performance targets that adversely affect ratepayer 
interests or are inconsistent with Commission policies.257 
 
256  Case 10-E-0362, Proceeding on the Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules, and 
Regulations of Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. for Electric Service, Order Establishing Rates for 
Electric Service (issued June 17, 2011).

Staff reviewed the Company’s benchmarking study methodology and determined

In addition to the compensation study review, Staff considered Central Hudson’s 
management practices, disturbances from the billing issues, and immense rate pressures in 
evaluating the Company’s executive compensation levels.  In the most recent comprehensive 
management and operations audit of Central Hudson in Case 21-M-0541 (2021 Management 
Audit), the consultant retained by the Commission to conduct the audit criticized the Company’s 
executive management incentive compensation program.  Among its critical findings, the 
consultant observed that the Company’s performance against the team goal targets underlying its 
incentive compensation program had been mixed, and the targets did not demonstrate an 
expectation of continuous improvement, and that the Company’s 2021 targets were lower than 
2017 targets.267  Based on those findings, Staff recommended the disallowance of the Company’s 
executive incentive compensation request.  
In its rebuttal testimony, Central Hudson disagreed with Staff’s recommendation.  
The Company noted that its billing issues are being addressed in other proceedings and general 
rate pressures should not support the disallowance of executive incentive pay.  Central Hudson 
also asserted that Staff’s recommendation would negatively impact the Company’s ability to 
attract and retain highly skilled executives.  Regarding the critical findings from the 2021 
Management Audit, the Company noted that performance program modifications are 
forthcoming with its implementation efforts in the that case.268 
The purpose of an incentive compensation program is to motivate employee 
performance to help an organization achieve its goals.  As the Commission has stated in its 
 
265  Tr. 4071. 
266  Tr. 4073.

268  Tr. 404.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
62 
requirements, the targets comprising an incentive compensation program must align with 
ratepayer interests in order for ratepayers to fund an incentive compensation program.269  Central 
Hudson’s practice of setting performance targets at minimum thresholds protects ratepayers from 
unacceptably poor service, but ratepayers are better served when utility performance exceeds 
those minimums. Incentive compensation programs should strive for continuous improvement in 
all areas where improvement can benefit ratepayers.  However, based on its structure, Central 
Hudson’s proposed executive incentive compensation program would not benefit ratepayers.  
Accordingly, Staff recommends that the Commission deny cost recovery for the executive 
incentive compensation program. 
g. Employee Benefits 
i.  
Medical Benefit Inflation Rate 
Central Hudson stated that it used its consultant, Mercer, to project medical 
benefit costs in its Rate Year forecast due to escalated claims over the last several years, historic 
cost increases due to the Company’s growing workforce, more high-cost claims, and unit cost 
inflation of services provided.270  The Company later acknowledged that it inadvertently double 
counted the incremental FTEs in its medical benefits projection, explaining in its response to 
DPS-509 that Mercer’s projection included costs for incremental FTEs, and the Company’s 
workpaper also added additional costs to that projection for the same incremental FTEs.271  
Staff disagrees with the Company projection of medical benefits.  It is the 
Commission’s long-standing practice to project medical costs using the Gross Domestic Product 
(GDP) Price Index (GDP-PI) inflation rate, as health care costs constitute a significant portion of 
costs included in the GDP-PI.272  Staff projected the medical costs using historic costs and GDP-
PI inflation, with additional costs for incremental employees.273  Since Staff excluded Mercer’s 
projection, there is no double count of costs for incremental FTEs.

In its rebuttal, the Company disagreed with using GDP inflation, stating that it 
does not consider the trend in increased health care costs that the Company is experiencing, and 
that it is likely that health care costs will increase at a level greater than items in the general 
inflation pool.274  The Company also argued that the study conducted by Mercer shows costs are 
projected to exceed GDP, and GDP is a broad measure while their study used the Company’s 
claims experience and specific trend factors.275  
The Commission should adopt Staff’s projection of medical benefit costs in this 
case.  The historic costs used in Staff’s projection already include the increased subscribers, 
high-cost claims, and costs of services that the Company has been experiencing.  The historic 
base is increased by GDP-PI inflation rate and adds costs for projected incremental employees.  
The GDP-PI rate reflects health care cost increases, as those costs make up a significant portion 
of the costs included in the inflation rate.

5. 
Productivity 
Central Hudson proposed to maintain the productivity imputation of 1%, as 
currently reflected in its 2021 Rate Plan.290  The Company stated that the productivity base it 
proposed remains unchanged from prior rate cases and includes labor, employee benefits, 
pension, OPEBs, payroll taxes and variable compensation.   
In its testimony, Staff explained that the Commission has a long-standing policy 
of imputing a productivity adjustment intended to capture unidentified or unquantifiable 
productivity gains, efficiencies and cost savings that could be realized by a utility.291  Staff 
recommended three changes to the Company’s productivity forecast.292  First, Staff 
recommended increasing the productivity imputation from 1% to 2%, to capture the unquantified 
savings associated with implementation of the recommendations from the 2021 Management 
Audit, which were not reflected in Rate Year revenue requirements, and to reflect expected 
savings from the significant FTE increases recommended by Staff in this case, which should 
allow the Company to realize increased efficiencies.293  Second, Staff recommended removing

the negative pension and OPEBs expense forecasts from the productivity calculation.294  In 
Staff’s view, although these costs have been reflected in the calculations in prior Staff testimony 
and joint proposals in Central Hudson rate cases, reflecting a negative expense is counterintuitive 
to the intention of the Commission’s productivity adjustment, and would not be appropriate in 
this rate case.  Third, Staff recommended updating all components in the productivity base, in 
order to accurately reflect Staff’s Rate Year cost recommendations.   
In its rebuttal testimony, the Company disagreed with the 2% productivity 
imputation, stating that the FTEs projected to be hired in the Rate Year are performing 
incremental work and no efficiencies would be realized.295  Second, the Company disagreed with 
the exclusion of pension and OPEBs from the productivity base because the Company believes 
the time horizon of negative pensions and OPEBs, which was about a ten-year timeframe 
spanning three rate cases, is temporary.  Third, the Company disagreed with increasing the 
productivity rate to 2% based on unquantified cost savings associated with management audit 
recommendations, stating that audit recommendations labeled as “To Be Determined” are 
expected to occur in the Rate Year, and any identified savings would follow in a subsequent 
period. 296 
Staff disagrees with the Company’s arguments in its rebuttal testimony.  First, the 
argument that newly hired FTEs would only be performing incremental work and therefore will 
not lead to productivity gains at the Company is speculative.  Business needs evolve over time, 
and it is entirely possible that newly hired employees initially perform incremental work but later 
also expand their duties to non-incremental work.  Second, including the negative pension and 
OPEBs costs within the productivity proxy calculation is contradictory to the intention of the 
Commission’s long-standing productivity imputation calculation, because a negative amount 
implies no productivity gains would be realized and actually reduces the proxy of productivity 
gains.  If the amounts are instead reflected as zero within the calculation, ratepayers at least do 
not lose proxy benefits from the other factors in the calculation.  Third, Staff does not agree that 
all savings related to the implementation of management audit recommendations would be

reflected in subsequent periods after the Rate Year.  While some savings may take time to be 
fully reflected, there could be savings that begin during the implementation process or 
immediately following implementation.  Staff’s recommended productivity adjustment is 
intended to capture these unquantifiable savings for ratepayers’ benefit.  
For these reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s adjustments to the 
productivity imputation.

9. 
Regulatory Commission Assessments 
The Regulatory Commission Expense is projected based upon the Department’s 
General Assessment charged to each utility in New York State to fund its operations.  There are 
three letters associated with the Regulatory Commission Assessment sent by the Department to 
utilities.315  First, an initial statement is mailed in February, prior to the start of the state fiscal 
year to which the assessment applies.  Second, a revised statement is mailed in August after the 
utilities have filed their annual reports.  Third, a final statement of assessment is mailed in 
October of the following year, after the close of the state fiscal year.  
The Company forecasted this expense in the Rate Year by applying a three-year 
historic average growth rate of 10% to the initial assessment dated February 10, 2023, in the 
amount of $3.427 million, allocated $3.017 million to electric and $847,520 to gas.316  Staff 
disagreed with the Company’s use of a 10% three-year growth average to develop its forecast 
because Central Hudson’s calculation reflected revised statements of assessment and an initial

concerns over fluctuations between revised and final statements is valid, as demonstrated by the 
latest known update that resulted in a significant, $897,752 reduction from the revised statement

Regulatory Commission Expense using the latest known final statement plus inflation is 
reasonable and appropriate in the context of a one-year rate filing.  For these reasons, the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s adjustment.
