11. 
Major Storm Reserve 
Central Hudson’s current rate plan includes $4.674 million in funding for its 
Major Storm Reserve for each rate year.328  Currently, once a storm has been determined to fall 
within the Commission’s definition of a major storm, incremental restoration costs incurred as a 
result of the adverse weather event must reach a level of at least $500,000 in order for expenses 
related to the event to be chargeable to the Major Storm Reserve.329  Storm costs charged to the 
Major Storm Reserve are reviewed by Staff to verify the costs are accurate, and in compliance 
with the Order which allows the company to use reserve accounting for major storms.330  
Additionally, pursuant to Appendix U of the Joint Proposal adopted in the 2020 rate case, the 
Company is allowed to record certain pre-staging event costs to the Major Storm Reserve: pre-
staging events with incremental costs between $100,000 and $1.75 million can be charged to the 
Major Storm Reserve, and events with incremental restoration costs greater than $1.75 million 
are allowed to be charged 85% to the Major Storm Reserve and 15% to expense, with Central 
Hudson allowed to file a deferral petition requesting recovery of the 15% if certain criteria are 
met. 
The Company proposed three changes related to the operation of its Major Storm 
Reserve.  First, the Company proposed an increase of $10.15 million to the annual O&M 
expense rate allowance for the Major Storm Reserve, bringing the Rate Year allowance to $14.82 
million per year, 331 based on a 10-year average of the Company’s major storm history.332  
 
328  Tr. 2602. 
329  Tr. 2602. 
330  Tr. 2601-2602. 
331  Tr. 722. 
332  Tr. 2079. 


--- Page 83 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
75 
Second, the Company proposed a change to the prestaging event thresholds from $1.75 million 
to $3.60 million.  Third, Central Hudson proposed to modify and expand the definition of a 
“major storm” for purposes of determining costs that can be charged to the Major Storm Reserve.   
Regarding the Company proposal to increase the allowance for the Major Storm 
Reserve to $14.82 million in the Rate Year, Staff agrees in part to the Company’s Rate Year 
forecast methodology.  Specifically, Staff agrees that a normalized 10-year average should be 
employed, with the exception that two superstorms costing $14.7 million and $24.2 million 
should be excluded from the normalized 10-year average.  These superstorms were considered 
outliers based on the fact that their restoration costs were significantly greater than the average 
storm restoration cost for the past 10 years.  The Major Storm Reserve is intended to fund 
“recurring” major storm events, exclusive of superstorm events, which occur less 
frequently.  Based on this adjustment, Staff recommends a Major Storm Reserve allowance of 
$10.7 million for the Rate Year. 
The Company argues against excluding superstorms from the Rate Year forecast, 
stating that removing superstorms would underfund the reserve, contributing to a larger 
regulatory asset balance.333  However, the Major Storm Reserve is not intended to be a blanket 
for all major storm events.  Moreover, even after removing these two superstorm events, Staff’s 
recommendation would increase the Major Storm Reserve allowance by approximately $6.1 
million from the last rate case proceeding, which established a rate allowance of $4.7 
million.  Staff’s recommended major storm rate allowance of $10.7 million provides an adequate 
Major Storm Reserve allowance after taking into consideration the normalized 10-year average.  
For  those reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended Major Storm Reserve 
allowance of $10.7 million for the Rate Year.  
Second, regarding the proposed change to pre-staging event thresholds, Staff’s 
analysis of the Company’s workpapers found that over the last 10 years, only one pre-staging 
and mobilization event exceeded the current $1.75 million threshold.  Increasing the threshold to 
$3.6 million based on this single outlier event would not be consistent with Central Hudson’s 
historical spending on such events and could in fact encourage the Company to spend more on 
pre-staging and mobilization even if an impending storm does not warrant such spending.  As

As to the definition of major storms, a “major storm” is currently defined in 
section 97.1 of the Commission’s rules and regulations as “a period of adverse weather during 
which service interruptions affect at least 10 percent of the customers in an operating area and/or 
result in customers being without electric service for durations of at least 24 hours.”336  Central 
Hudson proposed adding the following to the Commission’s “major storm” definition: “an 
incident resulting from a catastrophic event beyond the control of the Company; or an incident 
where problems beyond the Company’s control involving generation or the bulk transmission 
system is a key factor in the outage.”337  As noted above, a “major storm” is already clearly 
defined in Section 97.1 of the Commission’s rules and regulations.  Moreover, there already is a 
process available whereby the Company may petition the Commission directly if it believes 
extraordinary circumstances, such as those referenced by the Company, exist which warrant 
excluding a particular outage from reliability statistics; the Commission affirmed this process in 
its Order issued October 12, 2004, in Case 02-E-1240.338  In any event, as part of the Electric 
Capital Stipulation executed on January 16, 2024, Central Hudson agreed to certain Major Storm 
Reserve mechanisms for the Rate Year, including that a major storm event will be defined as 
specified in 16 NYCRR §97.1.339   
Adopting the pre-staging and mobilization provisions and the definition of major 
storm discussed above will permit Central Hudson to continue to defer incremental restoration

Although the Company’s intent to mitigate the revenue requirement increase by 
maintaining the currently approved depreciation parameters is admirable, it is appropriate to 
update the depreciation parameters, with Staff’s recommended adjustments as discussed below.  
Depreciation in a rate-making context is the equitable distribution of the recovery of plant from 
ratepayers, and using outdated depreciation parameters would result in an inequitable amount of 
depreciation expense being collected from ratepayers.  In addition, updating the Company’s 
depreciation parameters would both ensure that the Company is collecting an appropriate amount 
of depreciation expense, and that the Company’s accumulated depreciation reserve will not be 
smaller than it should be and consequently that the rate base will be at the appropriate level.  
Staff further notes that, in the Rebuttal Testimony of the Accounting and Tax Panel, the 
Company agrees in theory that it is appropriate to use updated depreciation rates.395   
2. 
Average Service Lives 
In the initial testimony of the Accounting and Tax Panel, the Company proposed 
to maintain the current depreciation parameters such as average service lives and salvage in order 
to mitigate the revenue requirement increase.396   As noted above, Staff recommends instead that 
the Commission adopt the depreciation parameters set forth in the 2022 Depreciation Study 
(including average service lives), except for the survivor curves and average service lives for 
eight accounts specified in the initial testimony of Staff witness Mingdi Huang, for which Staff 
recommends different depreciation parameters.397  Staff relied on mortality data as well as the 
original and smooth survivor curves provided in the 2022 Depreciation Study to select the 
appropriate curves and average service lives; in a number of instances, Staff found that a 
different smooth survivor curve than that proposed in the 2022 Depreciation Study better fit the 
 
394  Tr. 1493.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
87 
actual historic data points.398  Staff also limited recommended movements to 10 years from the 
average service lives currently approved, following the principle of gradualism to avoid 
significant impacts to revenue requirement.  Staff’s recommended depreciation parameters 
would result in increases in electric depreciation expense, from $58.5 million to $59.7 million, 
and increases in gas depreciation expense, from $18.7 million to $20.2 million, based on the 
Company’s plant in service levels on June 30, 2022.399   
In the rebuttal testimony of Company Witness John Spanos, the Company 
disagreed with Staff’s recommended average service lives, arguing that the selection of curves 
and average lives should not be based solely on a visual or mathematical fit, but should also 
consider what witness Spanos characterized as “informed judgment.”400  For average service 
lives, the Company also argued that limiting adjustments to 10 years places too much emphasis 
on depreciation parameters that were developed through the settlement process.401  Finally, the 
Company disagreed with Staff’s treatment of three common plant accounts where Staff 
recommended a different average service life, stating that as the life or amortization period has 
changed, the depreciable plant balance should change as well.402  
The Commission should reject the arguments raised by witness Spanos and adopt 
Staff’s recommended depreciation parameters as discussed herein.  First, the Company claimed 
that depreciation curves and average service lives should not be based solely on a mathematical 
fit.  However, Company witness Spanos states in the 2022 Depreciation Study that “the 
information external to the statistics led to little to no significant departure from the indicated 
survivor curves” for many of the curves where the Company and Staff disagree.403  Thus, it is 
unclear whether the “informed judgment” parameters that witness Spanos asserts that Staff failed 
to consider actually would have impacted the selection of curves and average lives based only on 
visual or mathematical fits.  
 
398  Tr. 2275-2276. 
399  Tr. 2265-2266. 
400  Tr. 280. 
401  Tr. 280. 
402  Tr. 310-311. 
403  Exhibit 151, Page III-2. 


--- Page 96 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
88 
Second, Staff witness Huang’s application of the principle of gradualism is 
appropriate for setting depreciation rates.  The selection of depreciation parameters is not precise 
and necessarily contains an element of discretion, for example, to address concerns of rate shock.  
As an extreme example, it is possible that some plant account may be declared outdated and have 
its retirement accelerated, thereby shortening its average service life significantly, from a 
hypothetical 80 years to 20 years.  It may then be decided that a retirement period of 20 years is 
too fast, and that it should instead be 40 years.  Consequently, significant rate shock is possible 
were depreciation rates to attempt to follow what analysis indicates they should be too closely, 
which can be avoided if gradualism was applied.  While the Company argued that Staff’s 
application of gradualism here places too much emphasis on depreciation parameters that were 
approved during a settlement process, those depreciation parameters are still currently in effect 
and impacting customer rates, and therefore represent an appropriate baseline from which 
changes to depreciation parameters can be measured.   
Third, regarding the Company’s argument about the appropriate depreciable plant 
balance, the Company appears to incorrectly suggest that whether plant has been fully amortized 
in the past depends on the amortization period going forward.  For example, the current 
amortization period for Common Plant Account 391.11, EDP Equipment – System and Main 
Frame, is eight years.404  By changing the amortization period to five years, the Company is 
suggesting that everything in the account older than five years should be considered fully 
amortized, even though plant installed six years ago would not be fully amortized under an eight-
year amortization period.   
For all the foregoing reasons, Staff’s recommended average service lives are 
appropriate to use for establishing depreciation rates, and the Company’s arguments to the 
contrary are without merit.  Accordingly, the Commission should approve the average service 
lives recommended by Staff. 
3. 
Net Negative Salvage Values 
Similarly to average service lives, the Company initially proposed to maintain its 
current net salvage factors in order to mitigate revenue requirement increases.405  Staff 
 
404  Exhibit 82, Schedule D, p. 1. 
405  Tr. 1493. 


--- Page 97 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
89 
recommends instead that the Commission approve the net salvage factors proposed in the 2022 
Depreciation Study, subject to adjustments for fourteen electric accounts, six gas accounts, and 
four common accounts as specified in the initial testimony of Staff witness Huang.406  Staff’s 
recommended changes to net salvage factors are based on a review of the salvage statistics 
provided in the 2022 Depreciation Study, with a look for trends in the annual and multiple yearly 
averages of the historic net salvage data, as well as the percentages of the net salvage to the 
original cost of plant retired each year.407  For accounts with apparent trends that indicate costs 
of removal are increasing, and thus net salvage is becoming more negative, Staff recommends a 
more negative net salvage factor for those accounts.  For accounts where there are no apparent 
trends, Staff recommends that the net salvage factor remain the same as approved in present 
rates. For accounts where the cost of removal is decreasing, Staff recommends a less negative net 
salvage rate.  Similar to average service lives, Staff also limited its recommended movements to 
10% from currently approved net salvage factors, following the principle of gradualism. 
Similar to its arguments regarding average service lives, the Company also 
disagreed with Staff’s application of gradualism for net salvage factors, claiming that limiting 
adjustments to 10 percentage points places too much emphasis on depreciation parameters that 
were developed through the settlement process.408  As previously noted, the principle of 
gradualism is appropriately applied in this case, as the selection of depreciation parameters has 
an element of discretion and trying to fully realize changes in depreciation rates could lead to 
rate shock.  The Company also disagreed with Staff’s net salvage parameters on the basis that 
Staff’s recommendations have not fully recognized trends to more negative net salvage.409  To 
the contrary, Staff’s recommendations do account for trends to more negative net salvage, but are 
limited by Staff’s application of the principle of gradualism, as already discussed. 
Therefore, Staff’s recommended net salvage factors, which follow the same trends 
as the depreciation study’s net salvage factors but are limited due to gradualism, are appropriate 
and should be approved by the Commission.

4. 
Amortization of Reserve Deficiency 
Generally, when a depreciation study is performed, the current book reserve is 
compared to the proposed theoretical reserve (incorporating any proposed changes to the 
survivor curves, average service lives, and net salvage factors).410  Typically, if the difference 
between the book reserve and the theoretical reserve is within a margin of 10%, no adjustment is 
made to amortize the over- or under-accruals.  However, if the difference is greater than 10%, an 
adjustment can be made to amortize some or all of the difference between the theoretical and 
book reserves,411 generally over a period of 20 years.412  Conversely, if the book reserve is larger 
than the theoretical reserve, some or all of the difference can be used for other ratemaking 
treatments, rather than a reduction to depreciation expense.413    
In its initial testimony, the Company proposed to begin recovery of its 
depreciation reserve, as follows: (1) recovering the electric and common reserve deficiencies to 
10% of the theoretical reserve, over a period of 10 years, and (2) recovering the entirety of the 
gas reserve deficiency, over a period of 10 years.414  The Company’s proposal would result in an 
annual reserve amortization of $42.7 million for electric, $33.0 million for gas and $345,000 for 
common.415  Staff recommends instead that the Commission should direct that the Company’s 
reserve deficiencies be recovered to 10% of the theoretical reserve for electric, gas and common, 
respectively, over a period of 20 years. 416  Based on the depreciation rates and parameters 
recommended by Staff, as discussed above, the Company’s theoretical reserves would differ 
from book reserves by less than 10% of each of electric, gas, and common, and therefore, no 
amortization of reserve deficiency would be needed.417 
The Commission should reject the Company’s proposal for the following reasons.  
First, reserve deficiencies are derived from theoretical reserves that are based on depreciation

Depreciation Study, but did not otherwise propose that the Commission adopt the depreciation 
rates in that study.  Second, the Company’s proposal to recover deficiencies over a period of 10 
years is inconsistent with the typical 20-year period over which such deficiencies are usually 
recovered.  While the Company asserted in rebuttal that a 10-year recovery period “provides a 
more reasonable timeframe to recover this current deficit under the principle of cost causation, 
provides credit supportive cash recovery that is needed, while also taking into consideration and 
offering some moderation to the bill impact,” the Company did not otherwise provide any 
evidence supporting those general statements.419  Third, regarding the Company’s proposal to

differently than a reserve excess.422  Preliminarily, witness Spanos did not elaborate on his

would provide additional details on his statement.  However, Central Hudson’s Accounting and

C. 
Property Taxes 
Central Hudson projected its property taxes by applying a three-year average 
historic growth rate based on actual historical taxes, applied to the most recent actual property 
tax expenses.424 The Company then added incremental expense resulting from reduced Economic 
Obsolescence, or EO, awards in the Rate Year.  Lastly, the Company added incremental tax 
expense for plant additions in the Rate Year, based on the capital expenditure plan.425  The 
Company proposed that property taxes be updated later in proceedings to use latest known 
taxes.426   
Staff recommended changes to the EO awards the Company used in 2024 and 
2025 for electric.  EO awards are a percentage reduction to the assessed value of a utility’s 
properties, which results in a lower property tax expense.427  In its workpapers, the Company 
projected declining EO awards of 3% in 2024 and 2% in 2025.428  In recent years, the 
Company’s EO award has fluctuated between 5% and 3.5%.429  Staff proposed a 4% EO award 
in 2024 and 2025, which is equal to the 2023 EO award that the Company received.  Staff also

cost and the Rate Year base upon which sales tax is applied, exclusive of call volume

is less and total sales and use tax will decrease.  Using the Company’s proposed methodology, 
when removing call volume overflow from its line items subject to sales and use tax, would 
result in a greater amount subject to the 8% sales tax, which skews the Rate Year projection.  For 
these reasons, the Commission should reject Central Hudson’s arguments, and adopt Staff’s 
adjustment.

3. 
Existing Variable Rate Debt 
Central Hudson requested continuation of its existing variable rate debt deferral

debt obligations, and Central Hudson is essentially a price-taker with respect to the benchmark 
rates upon which these securities are priced.  There is no disagreement between the Company 
and Staff on the continuation of the reconciliation for existing short-term variable rate debt for 
the Company.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation. 
4. 
New Fixed and Variable Rate Debt 
Central Hudson requested continuation of its existing fixed rate debt deferral and 
new fixed and variable rate debt allowed in its 2021 Rate Plan.454  Staff does not support 
continuation of the existing fixed rate debt deferral, or a deferral for new fixed and variable rate 
debt for Central Hudson.455  As explained in its Statement in Support, one reason why Staff 
supported the fixed rate deferral and new debt deferral in the Joint Proposal underlying the 2021 
Rate Plan was because the Company agreed to reduce the common equity component of its 
overall capitalization from 50% in rate year 1 to 48% in rate year 3.456  This necessitated the 
Company issuing more debt throughout the time frame covered by the 2021 Rate Plan than was 
previously forecast.  Those circumstances no longer exist as Staff is now recommending the 
maintenance of a 48% equity ratio.  In addition, the Company largely has control over when and 
how often it goes to the market, and has the ability to structure its fixed rate debt efficiently.

expenses.  Additionally, Central Hudson can file a deferral petition with the Commission seeking

Central Hudson requested that it receive deferral treatment of incremental costs

its testimony that the Commission deny deferral treatment of any costs connected to that 
proceeding at this time.482  The Company has since filed its long-term plan which is now under 
review.  Completion of that review, and an ultimate decision from the Commission on that filing, 
will likely not occur until after these rate proceedings have concluded.  Any decision regarding 
deferral of costs associated with that proceeding is best left for the Commission to decide within 
that specific proceeding, on a generic basis, instead of in a rate case involving a single utility.  
Based on those considerations, Staff recommends that the Commission deny the requested 
deferral treatment.

10. 
Roadway Excavation Quality Assurance Act 
In the October 2, 2023 Update Filing of its Policy Panel, Central Hudson 
proposed a new deferral of incremental costs associated with the implementation of the Roadway 
Excavation Quality Assurance Act, and explained that it had not had time to evaluate the 
potential impacts on the gas capital programs.483  In its testimony, Staff disagreed with this 
proposed deferral because the Company did not any provide justification for it, and 
recommended that the deferral be denied until Central Hudson had evaluated the impacts and 
presented a more thorough proposal.484   
  In rebuttal, the Company indicated that although it had put forth preliminary 
estimates of the impact on certain aspects, the full and final impact was not quantifiable at that 
time.  The Company reiterated that given the uncertainty in amount that may be required under 
this new law, a deferral is appropriate and should be allowed.485   
Staff maintains its testimonial position that this deferral should not be allowed.  
There is insufficient information at this time with which to evaluate the potential impacts of this 
Act on the gas capital program.  Moreover, as already stated, the risk to the Company is limited 
in a one-year rate case as the Company may seek new rates in the short term.  Also, the 
Company can petition the Commission for deferral authority if the Roadway Excavation Quality 
Assurance Act is not otherwise addressed by the Commission in a generic proceeding.

H. 
Rate Adjustment Mechanism 
Central Hudson proposed to continue its RAM, but with an adjusted threshold, 
from 2.5% of total operating revenues to 2.4% of total revenues, inclusive of surcharges and 
commodity costs.486  In testimony, Staff disagreed with allowing the RAM to continue during the 
Rate Year.  Central Hudson reflected ten-year amortizations of major storm and Energy 
Efficiency/Heat Pump costs, its two largest RAM-eligible regulatory asset balances, in its Rate 
Year revenue requirement.  However, since these items will have a recovery method, Staff did 
not support offering a second recovery method through a RAM.487  
In rebuttal, Central Hudson argued that Staff did not address carrying charges and 
how the RAM allows for more timely collection or return of carrying charges to customers.488 
The Company also indicated that the RAM would not be implemented if there were insufficient 
deferral balances to warrant collection and the RAM would continue to be capped at a maximum 
threshold to mitigate bill volatility.489   
Staff maintains its initial testimonial position that a RAM is not necessary during 
the Rate Year of a one-year rate case.  If a significant balance of regulatory assets or liabilities is 
built up between July 1, 2024, and June 30, 2025, recovery or refund of that balance could be 
pursued in the Company’s next rate case.  For those reasons, the Commission should reject the 
Company’s arguments and adopt Staff’s recommendation.

IV. 
RATE BASE ISSUES 
A. 
Electric Capital 
1. 
Electric Capital Projects 
The Company proposed an electric capital budget for its traditional Electric 
Capital Expenditure Categories totaling approximately $754 million across the five-year 
construction horizon from 2024 through 2028.  The initial testimony and exhibits of the ECOP 
indicated that the key drivers behind the Company’s electric capital forecasts are attributed to 
investments which are necessary to meet minimum standards of service or compliance, maintain 
their current level of service reliability and safety, and address aging asset conditions and 
condition-based infrastructure replacements that are needed throughout their service territory and 
across a host of various electric infrastructure.490  The Company’s electric capital budget also 
included twenty one projects classified as CLCPA Phase 1 projects that, according to Central 
Hudson, “satisfy Reliability, Safety, and Compliance purposes but that can also address 
bottlenecks or constraints that limit renewable energy delivery within a utility’s system or 
include the added benefit of increasing the capacity to host additional DERs.”491  The Company 
explained that its five-year capital plan includes projects that will increase headroom for 
renewables by up to 547 MW by the end of the five-year period.492 
Preliminarily, Staff identified issues with the Company’s presentation of capital 
data in these rate proceedings, which hindered Staff’s review of the Company’s proposed 
projects and programs in the context of the Company’s historical programs and spending.493  
These capital data issues are discussed in more detail in Section IV.G, infra.  Notwithstanding 
such issues, following their resolution, Staff reviewed the Company’s electric capital 
expenditures and generally agreed with the Company’s proposal, with two modifications, as 
reasonable and appropriate.  The Company’s electric capital expenditure request is largely 
business-as-usual spending, plus costs associated with projects identified as CLCPA Phase 1 
projects that will advance the sustainability initiatives identifies by and in support of the

CLCPA.494  If the capital expenditure impact associated with CLCPA Phase 1 projects are 
filtered out from the Company’s proposal, its remaining capital expenditure forecast would be 
well below the Company’s historic actual spending levels, and the Commission-approved total 
electric capital spending levels approved by the Commission in the 2021 Rate Order.495   
Regarding Staff’s recommended adjustments to the Company’s electric capital 
budget, first, the Company’s proposal included a new capital cost category to track non-
discretionary capital spending related to storm restoration efforts.  Staff recommended the 
removal of this new category because storm response related capital costs are still being tracked 
through various other cost categories.496  Considering that the Company has not re-categorized 
and budgeted all its future storm response related capital costs into the Storms Category, this 
may result in double counting of expenditures, and could create additional confusion in the 
future with respect to the Company’s tracking of capital expenditures, which is an ongoing issue 
with the Company as previously noted.497  Second, Staff recommended the removal of the 
Company’s Electric Transmission Structure Coating (ETSC) Program from its electric capital 
expenditure proposal.  Staff agreed with the conceptual need for the program.  However, in light 
of the large capital costs associated with the implementation of the suite of CLCPA Phase 1 
projects, and based on the fact that the Company had been managing its electric capital portfolio 
safely and reliably without this program (as demonstrated by the Company’s performance over 
the last few years with respect to its reliability metrics), Staff found that the proposed ETSC 
Program is not immediately necessary at this time.498  The cost impact associated with Staff’s 
recommendation to remove its Storms Category and ETSC Program proposals yielded a 
reduction of approximately $8.75 million and $8.25 million over the five-year construction 
period for each proposal, respectively.499

reasonable and appropriate.503  Accordingly, Staff recommends no adjustments to this category.

amounts to less than one million dollars annually, even though the Company asserts that it has 
“experienced an unprecedented increase in applications for attachment to distribution poles,” 
including the single project referenced above that is estimated to cost nearly $10 million in 
combined capital costs and associated expense.508  Based on the Company’s assertions, at a 
minimum the Company appears to be aware of, and expects, some level of increased spending 
for this program in the Rate Year.  To the extent that such costs are known and expected, the 
Company should be able to reasonably forecast the costs for this program during the Rate Year, 
and should have incorporated such incremental costs into its capital forecast.  Conversely, the 
Commission should not permit the Company to now forecast spending for this program at 
historical levels even when it claims to expect additional future costs, and simultaneously

4. 
PSL 119-d (Maintenance of Utility Poles) Reconciliation Mechanism 
In its rebuttal testimony, the Company’s ECOP identified concerns with PSL 
§119-d, which provides that, on or before April 1, 2024, the Commission will promulgate rules 
and regulations to combat vine growth on utility poles that could negatively affect service.509  
The ECOP explained that the Company’s current practice regarding vine trimming is handled 
through dispatch calls from customer inquiries as well as pole inspections.510  The ECOP 
asserted that, if PSL §119-d and the Commission’s implementing regulations result in additional 
vine clearing activity, incremental funding would be required.511  Accordingly, the Company 
proposed a new deferral mechanism be implemented for such vine clearing costs. 
The Company acknowledges that it is not aware of any regulations promulgated 
by the Commission to implement PSL §119-d.512  Absent such regulations, it is unclear what 
additional vine clearing activity (if any) would be required beyond the activities already 
conducted by the Company pursuant to its existing vine removal procedures.  Similarly, absent 
Commission regulations, it would be impossible at this time to determine the incremental costs 
(if any) that this section of the PSL would have on utility vine trimming activities.  For those 
reasons, Staff recommends that the Commission deny the Company’s proposed deferral 
mechanism  as premature at this time.  Instead, once the Commission has promulgated new 
guidelines and regulations pursuant to PSL §119-d, Staff can and will coordinate with the 
utilities, including Central Hudson, to monitor the impact of this provision of the PSL on ROW 
clearance activities.

B. 
Gas Capital 
1. 
Gas Transmission Projects 
The Company proposed several capital projects under its gas Transmission 
category, with capital expenditure levels of $4.24 million in 2024 and $6.65 million in 2025.513  
The majority of the capital improvements in this category are related to the continued 
replacement and installation of transmission line valves with those that can accommodate remote 
operators, replacements for the aging Receival Mahopac-Poughkeepsie/Tuxedo-Poughkeepsie 
interconnect transmission station, and partial replacement of gas transmission lines pursuant to 
the Pipeline Mega Rule promulgated by the Pipeline and Hazardous materials Safety 
Administration in an Order dated October 1, 2019, and set forth in 49 CFR §192.624.514  
In his initial testimony, Staff witness Andrew Riebel identified the Receival 
Mahopac-Poughkeepsie/Tuxedo-Poughkeepsie as important for gas system reliability in the 
Poughkeepsie region, as it would provide the Company the ability to supply the Poughkeepsie 
regulator station from multiple sources.515  Notwithstanding specific identification of that project 
as reliability-related, Staff recommended a capital budget for the gas transmission projects of 
$1.92 million in 2024 and $3.78 million in 2025.516  As part of its recommendation Staff did not 
propose an adjustment for a Pipeline Mega Rule project because this is a new project, and this 
project is in compliance with 49 CFR §192.624.  For the rest of the projects in this category Staff 
utilized a historical three-year average, because three years covers the most recent actual 
spending but also takes into account some historical data. 517  
In its rebuttal testimony Central Hudson disagreed with Staff’s adjustments, 
arguing that three-year average historical spending is not an adequate method of setting future 
expenditures levels for the Transmission category because it fails to consider the Company’s 
specific project justifications and the need for funding based on reliability and safety aspects of

purposes.550  For the same reasons as described with respect to the Transmission and Regulatory

projects does not negate the reasonableness of Staff’s overall recommended capital budget for 
the Distribution Improvements category.  Instead, it would be up to the Company to decide what 
projects need to be prioritized within the recommended budget during the Rate Year.

adopt Staff’s adjustments to the Company’s Distribution Improvements capital forecasts.

5. 
Meters 
The Company proposed a capital budget of $2.9 million in 2024, and $3.0 million 
in 2025, for its Meters category.551  The Company justified this forecasted budget based on the 
assertion that the cost of meters has risen by 56% from 2020 to 2023.552  In its testimony, Staff 
recommended a capital budget of $2.3 million in 2024, and $2.4 million in 2025.  Staff noted 
that it received conflicting answers from the Company for the forecasted number of meters to be 
replaced annually.553  As such, Staff calculated its recommended budget based the historical 
three-year average, from calendar years 2020 to 2022, adjusted by inflation, consistent with 
Staff’s adjustments for the other categories of gas capital spending. 
In rebuttal, the Company acknowledged the discrepancy in the discovery 
responses provided to Staff, and provided additional information in Exhibit__(GCOP-5R) which 
purportedly ties to the Company’s proposed budget for the Meters category.554  Staff notes, 
however, that the Company has historically overbudgeted for the Meters category.  Thus, Staff’s 
usage of a three-year historical average, which would take into consideration the growth in 
material costs during the recent past and would reflect the Company’s actual spending in this 
category, is correct and appropriate.  Accordingly, the Commission should adopt Staff’s 
recommended capital budget for the Meters category. 
C. 
Common Capital 
1. 
Land and Building 
For the Company’s Lands and Buildings category, Central Hudson proposed 
many projects including, but not limited to, Daily Operations, Architectural/Engineering Design 
Program, Paving, Solar, Training Academy-Annex, Training Academy-Academy, Transportation 
Building, Butler Building Rebuild, and Ellenville Office Renovation.   For the Lands and 
Buildings budget, the Company proposed capital expenditures of $17.479 million in 2024 and

costs, Staff recommends that Central Hudson should receive some funding for this budget item.

Paving 
Central Hudson’s Paving budget is comprised of necessary paving projects at

and then applied inflation to determine the total capital costs of $355,305 in 2024, and $342,679 
in 2025.570  Based on those calculations, Staff recommended a downward adjustment of 
$362,267 in 2024, and $193,017 in 2025.571  In rebuttal, Central Hudson disagreed with Staff’s 
methodology of using the last three years of historic actuals, arguing that they did not take into 
account paving needs that were postponed from 2021-2023 due to a reprioritization of 
expenditures.572   
Staff maintains its testimonial position.  Reprioritizing capital expenditures was 
Central Hudson’s choice, and it is unclear if all paving work would have been completed if the 
Company did not reprioritize its allocation of funding.  Further, the Company is not providing a 
guarantee that it would not reprioritize paving funds to other capital projects again in the future.  
Therefore, using a historical three-year average is a reasonable and appropriate method to 
calculate the amount of forecasted spending that the Company could achieve in the Rate Year as 
it accounts for actual spending levels and the current costs of paving.  For those reasons, Staff 
recommends that the Commission reject the Company’s arguments, and adopt Staff’s 
adjustments for the Paving category.

123 
The Company argued in rebuttal that Staff incorrectly applied contingency 
factors.585  Based on Staff’s review of the Central Hudson Capital and Operations Panel’s 
Exhibits, however, the “Raw Cost” with vendor estimates already appear to include a 
contingency factor.  Applying two contingency factors to the “Raw Cost” component for these 
projects would be inappropriate and were therefore excluded.  In other words, Staff applied the 
8% contingency factor utilized by the Company to all other cost components for these projects, 
with the exception of the Raw Cost.   
In Central Hudson’s rebuttal, the Company further disagreed with Staff’s use of 
an 8% contingency factor for the Transportation Building - EC, Butler Building Rebuild, and 
Ellenville Renovation Projects, instead of the 20% contingency factor used by the Company.586  
Central Hudson also asserted that its internal guidelines provide for an 8% contingency factor for 
projects at a definitive estimate phase, and a 20% contingency factor for projects at a conceptual 
estimate phase.587  However, when the Company is presenting Staff with projects in the design 
phase, it should already have a fairly accurate idea of the costs associated with those projects.  
This is particularly true with respect to the three projects referenced by the Company, all of 
which have capital expenditures forecasted for 2025, and should be in a relatively mature state of 
development.  Moreover, it is unclear at what point a project would transition from a 
“conceptual” estimate phase to a “definitive” estimate phase, and whether the contingency factor 
for that project is reduced from 20% to 8% at that time.  Absent this consideration, there is a risk 
that the Company’s total capital budget could be inflated to account for those future unknowns 
that should become known at some point.  Conversely, maintaining a consistent contingency 
across projects to be implemented in the near future, as recommended by Staff, is an appropriate 
approach for this one year rate case.  To the extent the Company believes a 20% contingency 
should still apply to projects in the later years of its Five-Year Capital Plan that may still be in a 
conceptual estimate phase, those projects would fall outside of the Rate Year in the current one-
year litigated rate case.

adjustment of $0.173 million in 2024, and $0.057 million in 2025, based on adjustments to the 
Company’s calculation of workstations and office chair replacements, which are discussed in 
detail below.  Central Hudson, in its rebuttal, disagreed with Staff’s use of the industry average 
when calculating workstation and chair replacement costs, the Company’s specific arguments to 
each are outlined in detail below.590   
Staff maintains its testimonial position.  The Company did not provide 
documentation in support of its calculated value for the cost per workstation.  Specifically, in its 
response to DPS-511, which requested all cost estimates, methodologies, assumptions, and other 
supporting workpapers to justify the Company’s calculations, the Company only responded that 
“a per workstation average cost was generated by using actual data from the recent building 808 
project which is $5,938,” and did not otherwise provide documentation supporting that 
statement.591  The Company also failed to define what was captured in its “per workstation cost” 
calculation.  Due to the lack of information provided by the Company to support its budget, Staff 
instead used the industry average cost for a workstation cost which, based on industry standard, 
cost between $1,400 and $3,100.592  While the Company argued in rebuttal that its estimate

proposal, and instead utilize Staff’s methodology to forecast office chair costs.

capital category are reasonable and appropriate in these rate proceedings.

projects.  Although funding for Emergent projects was granted in the previous rate case, the 2021 
Rate Order spanned three years.  Given the shorter one-year span in this case, Staff does not 
consider it necessary to grant funding for unplanned projects without first seeing justification for 
specific projects.  If the need to fund an unplanned project arises during the Rate Year, the 
Company may request a deferral or provide specific request for projects in a future rate case 
filing since no funding for Emergent projects is requested for 2024.  The deferral process or 
review in a future rate case filing would allow the Commission to exercise appropriate oversight, 
which would otherwise be absent if the Company received funding for unplanned projects 
without showing proof of a need for those specific projects over a short-term.  In addition, the

discretionary funding in Case 20-M-0082.  Staff did not learn that the Company’s workpapers 
had melded the IEDR Fin/Ops Data Analytics project into the IEDR Phase I and II projects until 
the Company responded to DPS-668.  Because these project costs were not broken out in the 
Company’s initial proposal, the Company did not adequately justify this discretionary 
spending.626  The Company argued in its rebuttal testimony that implementation of the Phase I 
and II projects without the concurrent implementation of the IEDR Fin/Ops Data Analytics 
project could lead to inefficiency.627  Staff, however, contends that this potential pitfall does not 
justify including funding for a project that was not approved in Case 20-M-0082.  The 
Commission has not determined that such costs are needed, and although the Company stated 
that it would be forced to continue maintaining a separate legacy data warehouse, it has not 
identified in rebuttal testimony where such cost savings are accounted for in future years with the 
funding of the IEDR Fin/Ops Data Analytics project.  Staff accordingly recommends that the 
Commission not approve cost recovery for this project.

5. 
Communications 
Central Hudson proposed spending $9.56 million in 2024 and $8.66 million in 
2025 for the Communications category in its five-year capital plan.664  Staff recommended a 
12.87% annual reduction to Central Hudson’s capital forecast for the Communications category, 
for a total funding level of $8.33 million in 2024 and $7.55 million in 2025.665  This 12.87% 
reduction was determined by calculating the Company’s annual actual average IT spend for 2020 
through 2022 and applying projected inflation rates from the latest forecasts of the GDP-PI.666  
The Staff Common Panel did not review the Communications category on a project-by-project 
basis. 
In its rebuttal testimony, Central Hudson once again disagreed with Staff’s use of 
the GDP-PI inflation rates.  Additionally, Central Hudson argued that the “Land Mobile Radio 
Replacement with Digital Mobile Radios (DMR),” and “Router Replacement Program” projects 
in the Communications category were not captured by reviewing historical averages because 
these projects were incremental additions.667  
As previously explained, Staff maintains that the GDP-PI inflation rate is 
appropriate, as it captures inflation rate for 2023, accounts for communication purchases for 
nonresidential purposes, and is consistent with other Staff capital adjustment.  Additionally, the 
Company’s historic annual average IT spend includes projects novel to that three-year period, so 
the presence of incremental additions in 2024 and 2025 does not invalidate Staff’s methodology.  
Staff therefore recommends that the Commission adopt its proposed 12.87% reduction for this 
category.

G. 
Capital Reporting Requirements 
As noted in Section IV.A.1, above, throughout this rate case process, Staff 
identified multiple issues with the Company’s presentation of capital data that hindered Staff’s 
review of the Company’s proposed projects and programs in the context of the Company’s 
historical programs and spending.   Specifically, as part of its responses to Staff’s pre-filed 
discovery, the Company provided historical actual and budgeted expenditures for its programs 
and projects in an unorganized fashion of over a thousand lines of project cost data, with capital 
cost information presented in some cases across several line items for individual programs and

ongoing programs.  They also required Staff to engage in multiple rounds of technical 
conference calls with Company subject matter experts, and issue multiple rounds of discovery 
requests, before the Company’s capital data was presented in a manner that could facilitate 
Staff’s review. 
The core mission of the Department is to ensure affordable, safe, secure, and 
reliable access to electric, gas, steam, telecommunications, and water services for New York 
State’s residential and business consumers, at just and reasonable rates, while protecting the 
natural environment.692  In that context, Staff reviews a utility’s proposed capital spending to 
ensure that forecasted projects are in the best interest of customers, such that they are necessary 
from an electric safety, reliability, and resiliency perspective, while also taking into 
consideration the costs associated with such projects and programs and their respective impacts 
on customers.  Utility rate plans also include forecasted capital costs for specific, utility-
identified programs, upon which customer rates will be derived; if a utility’s capital project 
forecasts are not transparent in the context of its historical programs, it creates unnecessary 
difficulty in understanding and comparing historic actual versus future forecasted expenditures 
and, therefore, determining whether future capital spending is just and reasonable.   
For all of these reasons, in order to alleviate inconsistencies and tracking 
inaccuracies with Central Hudson’s capital spending and help streamline Staff’s analysis of such

V. 
CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND SUSTAINABILITY INITIATIVES 
A. 
Disadvantaged Communities 
As discussed in more detail in Section I.D, Staff’s recommendations, as a whole, 
provide emissions-reduction benefits without disproportionately burdening disadvantaged 
communities.  As such, the Commission should find that adopting a rate plan reflecting Staff’s 
recommendations complies with the requirements of CLCPA §7(3) regarding disadvantaged 
communities.

B. 
Gas Initiatives 
1. 
Responsibly Sourced Gas 
Central Hudson sought authorization to procure RSG during the supply year of 
April through March, at a level which does not exceed $200,000 in incremental costs.697  
According to Central Hudson, this would allow greater utilization of RSG in serving system gas 
loads, leading to further reduction of fugitive methane emissions.698  The Company’s Climate 
Leadership and Sustainability Panel further explained how, based on the measurement and 
reporting of operational and fugitive methane emissions at upstream natural gas facilities, the 
Company’s RSG proposal would align with the state’s emission reductions goals.699   The 
Company further noted that other utilities in New York are pursuing RSG pilot programs at this 
time.700   
Staff recommends that the Commission authorize the Company to pursue RSG as 
proposed.  Based on the fact that exposure to customers would be no greater than $200,000, and 
RSG could be a potential means to decrease GHG emissions, Staff supports the Company’s 
proposal to the extent that incremental costs associated with RSG do not exceed $200,000.701  
The Commission should further direct Central Hudson to file, on a monthly basis during the Rate 
Yea, the details of the purchases of RSG going forward, including the name of the certifier of the 
RSG, the source of gas that is being certified, the volume, and the cost per unit.702  These 
informational filings will enable Staff to track and monitor such purchases, and will provide 
useful information on RSG-related efforts.  New York State currently has had little information 
to date on the details of RSG verification, and the reporting requirements will help provide such 
needed details as the State looks at ways of meeting its climate goals.

CLP opposed the Company’s proposed RSG procurement, arguing inter alia, that

certification could be based on biased data collection.704   As noted above, Staff’s proposed 
reporting requirements, which include numerous details of the RSG to be procured by Central 
Hudson, will provide relevant information to aid in the determination of whether and/or to what 
extent RSG may be help the state meet its GHG emissions reductions goals.  Staff further notes 
that it recommends authorizing a limited procurement of RSG (i.e., to a maximum of $200,000) 
to develop a better understanding of this gas supply, and Staff does not otherwise recommend 
allowing the Company unlimited ability to procure RSG.  For all the foregoing reasons, the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation.  
2. 
Clean Hydrogen Feasibility Study 
In its initial testimony, Central Hudson’s Climate Leadership and Sustainability 
panel outlined a proposed process for conducting a Clean Hydrogen Feasibility Study to identify 
portions of its distribution system where hydrogen blending activities could be successful, and

VI. 
RATE OF RETURN / FINANCIAL ISSUES 
Staff recommends an overall after-tax return of 6.74% for Central Hudson and the 
Company proposes a 7.10% return.  The difference between Staff’s recommended rate of return 
and Central Hudson’s proposed rate of return is attributable to Staff’s recommended reduction in 
the cost of equity from 9.8% to 9.2%, Staff’s recommended reduction in the common equity ratio 
from 50% to 48%, and Staff’s recommended increase in the cost of long-term debt from 4.39% 
to 4.46%.   
In terms of revenue requirement, a 1% change in the common equity ratio is 
worth approximately $1,982,000, and a 10-basis point change in ROE is worth approximately 
$1,607,000.  Staff’s recommended 6.74% is a fair rate of return, meaning that it will assure the 
Company of continued support in the capital markets.  In Staff’s view, this rate of return will be 
commensurate with the risks that Central Hudson investors will bear.  
Staff also recommends that the Commission update the rate of return and long-
term debt calculations, where applicable, to reflect the most current financial information 
available at the time of its decision.  The various inputs to rate of return and the methods of 
determining them are discussed in more detail in the sections below.   
A. 
Capital Structure 
In the direct testimony of its Finance Panel, the Company proposed a 50.0% 
common equity ratio.739  The Company offered three arguments in support of its proposed capital 
structure.  First, the Company argued that this capital structure is needed to help mitigate the 
impact of certain credit metrics, particularly those concerning cash flow.740  Second, Central 
 
737  Chapter 45 of the Laws of 2022 (effective March 22, 2022); see also Case 22-E-0222, Electric Utility 
Climate Vulnerability Studies and Plans, Order Initiating Proceeding (issued June 16, 2022), p. 3. 
738  Exhibit 160. 
739  Tr. 969. 
740  Tr. 959. 


--- Page 162 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
154 
Hudson argued that it has implemented strong ring-fencing measures that have been recognized 
by the major rating agencies.  Third, the Company claimed that utility operating subsidiaries of 
their proxy group have, between 2018 and 2021, maintained average common equity ratios of 
more than 6.0 percentage points above the 48.0% equity ratio that the Commission approved for 
Rate Year Three in the Company’s current Rate Plan.   
Staff agreed that the rating agencies view Central Hudson as adequately ring-
fenced and thus rate the Company’s credit quality on a substantially stand-alone basis and apart 
from its holding company, Fortis.  Staff did not agree, however, with the Company’s use of the 
operating subsidiaries’ historical equity ratios to support its proposed 50% common equity ratio.  
It is Staff’s opinion that a subsidiary’s common equity balance is not always financed by 
common equity at the holding company level.  Rather, some of the subsidiary’s common equity 
balance may instead be proceeds from debt issued at the holding company level that was then 
classified on the utility subsidiary's books as common equity at the time they were invested in 
the utility subsidiary.741  Therefore, the appropriate and relevant equity ratio analysis should be 
based on the utility holding companies.742   
In both its rebuttal testimony and in testimony elicited at the hearing, Central 
Hudson suggested that the Company is facing unique risks that other New York and U.S. utilities 
do not currently face.  The Company further suggested that the nature of these risks justifies an 
equity ratio that is generally higher than those allowed for other New York utilities because it 
would help offset investors’ concerns and maintain Central Hudson’s ability to attract capital at 
reasonable rates. 
Staff disagrees.  Although the Company may attempt to point to individual risk 
elements that are higher for it than for other utilities in New York or within the proxy groups 
used by Staff or Company witness Joshua Nowak, Central Hudson’s credit ratings reflect a 
company that is viewed by the rating agencies as having an overall risk profile that is comparable 
to other New York utilities and comparable to the companies in the parties’ respective proxy 
groups.   
 
741  Tr. 2365-2368. 
742  Tr. 2361. 


--- Page 163 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
155 
Staff also rejects the Company’s argument that Staff fails to acknowledge that the 
Company faces incremental risks associated with New York’s clean energy requirements and the 
associated capital spending.743  As Moody’s has observed, these risks have been noted and 
incorporated into its analysis.744  There is accordingly no need to afford Central Hudson special 
consideration on this basis as these risks have been reflected in Moody’s in its analysis.                  
Furthermore, Staff’s independent analysis using the holding company capital 
structures indicates that, from 2019 to 2022, the holding companies in Company witness 
Nowak’s Combined Proxy Group financed their asset base with an average common equity ratio 
of 44.10%.  The operating subsidiaries of his Combined Proxy Group, however, relied on an 
average ratio of 54.13% between 2018 and 2021.745  Additionally, Staff’s analysis of its Proxy 
Group indicates that, from 2019 to 2022, its holding companies financed their asset base with an 
average common equity ratio of 44.88%.  Therefore, when measured at the holding company 
level, Staff’s common equity ratio and Mr. Nowak’s ratio are very close in value.  And neither 
one is near either the 54.13% measured by Mr. Nowak at the operating company level or the 
50% requested by Central Hudson.746  
Also, Mr. Nowak’s analysis is at odds with the practical financial practices of 
corporate capitalization.  Typically, investors require less equity capitalization for a utility 
operating company than they would for a utility holding company.  This is because a utility 
operating company receives the vast majority of its revenue from regulated activities that are 
perceived by investors as more predictable and therefore lower risk.  On the other hand, a utility 
holding company, while still likely receiving a majority of its revenue from its subsidiaries’ 
regulated activities, may also receive a substantial amount of less-predictable revenue from non-
regulated activities.  Investors generally perceive that balance as riskier and thus require more 
equity capitalization to compensate for the added risk.  Therefore, it is expected that a proxy 
group of utility holding companies would have a higher common equity ratio than the collective 
utility operating companies within that group of holding companies.  This is the opposite of what 
 
743  Tr. 258. 
744  Exhibit 266, p. 4. 
745  Tr. 206-207. 
746   Exhibit 271, p.2. 


--- Page 164 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
156 
a comparison of the average common equity ratios of the Company Proxy Group holding 
companies and the Company Proxy Group operating companies appears to demonstrate.       
For these reasons, Staff continues to support a 48% common equity ratio and 
recommends that its recommended ROE of 9.2% be adopted by the Commission. 
Credit Metrics 
The Company also argued that a 50.0% common equity ratio is needed because a 
higher equity ratio will improve the Company’s cash flow and therefore reduce the risk of 
downgrades.  Cash flow is a component in a number of credit metrics utilized by the rating 
agencies to help ascertain the comparative financial risk of utility companies.   While some of 
the Company’s credit metrics tied to cash flow are below the agencies’ targets for “Baa1” 
(Moody’s) and “BBB+” (S&P) ratings, Staff projects that others will be above the agencies’ 
respective targets under Staff’s recommended 48% equity ratio and 9.2% ROE.  These include 
the Debt to Capital metric for Moody’s (“A3”), as well as the FFO to Debt and Debt to EBITDA 
metrics (both “A-”) for S&P.  Based on the credit rating methodology guidelines from Moody’s 
and Standard and Poor’s, Staff concludes that our 48% common equity ratio and 9.2% ROE, 
when combined with other quantitative metrics and qualitative factors, produce credit metrics 
that are generally consistent with the performance required by Moody’s and S&P to maintain the 
Company’s current ratings of “Baa1” and “BBB+” respectively.747  
In addition to these financial metrics, Staff relied on the rating agencies’ business 
risk profiles for Central Hudson.  Standard and Poor’s assigns the Company a business risk 
profile of “Excellent.”  Moody’s business risk profile analysis, which accounts for 60% of its 
overall indicative grid rating, incorporates such factors as: (1) Legislative and Judicial 
Underpinnings of the Regulatory Framework; (2) Timeliness of Recovery of Operating and 
Capital Costs; (3) Consistency and Predictability of Regulation; (4) Sufficiency of Rates and 
Returns; (5) Market Position; and (6) Generation and Fuel Diversity.  Each of the six business 
risk factors are ranked as “Aaa”, “Aa”, “A”, “Baa”, “Ba”, “B”, and “Caa”.  Given that the 
Company is rated by Moody’s, Staff used Moody’s qualitative scores for the Company averaging 
“Baa1.”748  And since these qualitative scores account for 60% of Moody’s credit score for 
 
747  Exhibit 272, p. 1. 
748  Exhibit 266, p. 8. 


--- Page 165 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
157 
Central Hudson, their strength gives Moody’s the flexibility to maintain the Company’s current 
rating even when some other components of the overall credit score are below Moody’s target 
ranges.     
The Company also argued that Staff's capital structure would leave it unable to 
improve its CFO pre-WC/Debt metric, which puts the Company at greater risk of a 
downgrade.749  Staff, however, reviewed Moody’s guidance on when to pursue a downgrade and 
found that pursuing a downgrade based on the CFO pre-WC/Debt metric requires the metric to 
fall below 14% for a sustained period.750  While Central Hudson’s underperformance on this 
metric is of potential concern, Staff notes that Moody’s will often discount performance in the 
short run, particularly if it is related to cash flow impacts that may not be durable.  Central 
Hudson’s most recent value for that metric, according to Moody’s was 7.8% as of March 
2023.751   
Notably, in recent credit reports for two other New York utilities, Moody’s 
maintained the ratings and outlook of both New York State Electric & Gas Corporation 
(NYSEG) and Rochester Gas & Electric Corporation (RG&E).  These utilities, like Central 
Hudson, are rated “Baa1” with a Stable outlook.  Moody’s maintained those ratings despite the 
fact that the CFO pre-WC/Debt metric for both entities were significantly lower than the current 
or forecasted level for Central Hudson.  Specifically, as of September 2023, NYSEG’s CFO pre-
WC/Debt metric was -2.2%, while RG&E’s CFO pre-WC/Debt metric was 4.0%.752  Therefore, 
Staff recommends that the Commission find the Company’s CFO pre-WC/Debt metric argument 
unpersuasive.  
Ultimately, the reason Central Hudson desires to at least maintain its current 
credit ratings is to help the Company access capital at reasonable rates.  But the Company has 
demonstrated that debt investors maintain a favorable view of Central Hudson and its ability to 
repay its debt obligations.  In a December 12, 2023 compliance filing made in conjunction with 
its most recent debt issuance, the Company reported that it achieved a spread of 130 basis points 
 
749  Tr. 962.

751  Exhibit 266, p. 2..

158 
above the benchmark 5-year U.S. Treasury rate at the time.  This result was consistent with other 
comparable utilities with similar debt ratings who issued debt in that time frame.753  This 130-
basis-point spread shows that the Company, notwithstanding its recent issues and current credit 
metrics, remains poised to continue accessing capital at reasonable rates. 
B. 
Long-Term Debt 
In its direct testimony, Central Hudson requested a Rate Year cost of debt of 
4.39%.754  Central Hudson’s 4.39% cost rate reflects the weighted average cost of its outstanding 
long-term debt, as well as interest rates projected at 5.60% and 5.70%, for a planned total 
issuance of $142 million of 20-year senior unsecured debt obligation near the end of the Rate 
Year.755  
Central Hudson determined the 5.60% and 5.70% interests rate for new debt 
securities by adding an average 185 basis point spread associated forecasted 10-year and 30-year 
U.S Treasury rates for the stub period and rate year.756  Staff disagrees with the Company’s use 
of forward yield curves to estimate the interest rate associated with Central Hudson’s planned 
debt issuance.  Consistent with the Commission’s approach, Staff recommends using the most 
recent (e.g., January 2024) average of the 10-year Treasury yield and 30-year Treasury yield, or 
4.16%, in combination with the Company’s 130-basis-points spread, to estimate an interest rate 
of 5.46% for Central Hudson’s proposed new debt issuance.  This would result in an embedded 
cost of debt of 4.39%, which is what the Company requested in its original testimony.757  The 
Company and Staff agree any chosen interest rate should be updated based on the most recent 
interest rates and yield spreads available when the Commission ultimately issues its decision.758

In its initial testimony, the Company had forecasted an interest rate on its “Series

C. 
Cost of Common Equity 
While Staff and the Company applied similar discounted cash flow (DCF) and 
capital asset pricing model (CAPM) financial models to estimate the Company’s cost of equity, 
Staff disagrees with the Company on many critical details.763  Specifically, Staff disagreed with 
the Company on: (1) composition of appropriate proxy group; (2) appropriate growth rates in 
DCF analyses; (3) key inputs employed in the CAPM analyses; (4) the relative weighting of the 
CAPM and DCF results; and (5) the appropriate version of the Microsoft internal rate of return 
Excel function to use when discounting dividend cash flow in the DCF model.     
In its rebuttal testimony, the Company contended that “Staff’s approach 
mechanically develops the assumptions used in their ROE formula without considering the effect 
of market conditions on the models, or the current financial profile of the Company.”764

Consistent application of regulatory principles and practices, however, lends predictability to the

1.  
ROE Methodology 
The Company developed three multi-stage DCF models utilizing the low, mean, 
and high earnings growth rate estimates published by Yahoo Finance, Zack’s, and Value Line 
Investment Survey (Value Line).766  Company witness Nowak’s DCF calculations results in a 
low, mean, and high ROE estimate for each company in his proxy group.  Mr. Nowak utilizes 
both the traditional CAPM and the zero-beta767 CAPM and applied both the Bloomberg and the 
Value Line betas to his CAPM calculations.  He also applied different DCF and CAPM 
weightings which, resulted in a range of 12 ROE recommendations for his proxy group.  Mr. 
Nowak then concluded that an appropriate ROE for the Company ranges between 9.80% and 
10.80%.  In these proceedings, the Company proposed a 9.80% ROE for the Rate Year.768  
Consistent with the Commission’s well-established practice, Staff applied the standard 
weightings of two-thirds to its two-stage DCF results and one-third to its CAPM results, which 
results in a ROE of 9.20%.769    
2.  
Proxy Group 
The use of a proxy group analysis is a widely accepted standard that the 
Commission has consistently applied to estimate the cost of equity for a utility.  The use of a 
proxy group is necessary because analysts’ estimates regarding an individual company are, by 
their nature, inaccurate and sometimes biased.770   
 
765  Exhibit 264, p. 4.  
766  Tr. 186.  
767  In a CAPM analysis, beta is the standard measure of systematic risk.  It gauges the tendency of a 
security’s return to track that of the stock market as a whole. 
768  Tr. 145.   
769  Tr. 2342.  
770  Tr. 2407.  


--- Page 169 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
161 
In general, a proxy group analysis begins by establishing selection criteria with 
which to construct a sufficiently large group of companies whose risk characteristics align as 
closely as possible to the specific utility or utilities being analyzed.  By employing a proxy group 
with a sufficiently large number of similarly situated companies, the effects of inaccurate 
estimates may be reduced.771   
Once the proxy group is constructed, the average cost of equity for the group can 
be estimated.  Some of the differences between Staff’s and the Company’s cost of equity initially 
stem from the difference in each other’s approach to determining the proxy group for Central 
Hudson.  As discussed below, Staff recommends that the Commission employ its proxy group. 
a.  
Company Proxy Group 
The Company’s proxy group consisted of 14 companies classified by Value Line 
as “electric” companies and five companies classified by Value Line as “natural gas” companies.  
Notably, many of the companies classified as “electric” by Value Line do have significant 
natural gas transmission and distribution operations as well.  The five companies categorized as 
“natural gas” do not have any electric transmission and distribution operations.772 
To be included in Mr. Nowak’s proxy group, companies must satisfy six criteria: 
(1) investment-grade credit ratings; (2) coverage by at least two utility industry equity analysts; 
3) dividend payments or positive earnings growth projections; (3) at least 70% of total operating 
income derived from regulated operations; (5) generation comprising less than 60% of the 
candidate’s MWh sales; and (6) no involvement in mergers, acquisitions, or other 
transformational transactions.773  For the natural gas-only companies, Mr. Nowak requires one 
additional criterion: 50% of the candidate’s total operating income must be from regulated 
natural gas operation.   
Mr. Nowak applies these seven selection criteria to a universe of 10 companies 
classified as natural gas distribution companies by Value Line to determine the set of companies 
included the Company Proxy Group.774  Staff raised concerns regarding the nature of Mr.

Nowak’s proxy group, specifically the inclusion of the natural gas-only companies, the selection 
criteria relating to net operating income, and generation MWH sales.775  
Staff objects to Mr. Nowak’s inclusion of five companies that can be 
characterized as “gas only.”  As mentioned earlier, the majority of holding companies classified 
as “electric” in the Value Line universe are combination utilities with both electric and gas 
operations, just like Central Hudson.  The inclusion of companies with “gas only” operations has 
the potential to shift the proxy group’s overall risk profile since pure gas companies may not 
have the same risk characteristics of Central Hudson.  Moreover, Fortis, Inc., the ultimate parent 
company through which equity investors can indirectly invest in Central Hudson, is classified by 
Value Line as an “electric” utility holding company.776  Therefore, any cost of equity analysis 
should include companies considered electric holding companies. 
Staff also points out flaws in the Company’s selection criteria for its proxy group.  
Specifically, Staff disagrees with the criteria requiring that the holding companies have at least 
70% of their total operating income come from regulated operations and that more than 50% of 
the candidate’s total regulated operating income come from regulated natural gas distribution 
operations.777  These two requirements are not appropriate and are the primary reasons why Mr. 
Nowak’s proxy group is notably smaller than Staff’s larger group.778   
These selection criteria eliminate multiple companies whose risk characteristics 
are sufficiently comparable to the risks faced by the Company.  Differences in accounting policy 
choices, such as a difference in inventory valuation policy options and a difference in 
depreciation choices across companies, could have a significant impact on reported operating 
income versus reported revenues.779  These policy differences may cause operating income to be 
more volatile than revenues.  Regulated revenues therefore remain a superior criterion for 
ensuring that a utility proxy group properly reflects the risk profile of the utility being analyzed.

b. 
Staff Proxy Group 
Staff constructed a proxy group of 26 investment grade rated electric and electric 
combination utility holding companies selected from a universe of 38 holding companies deemed 
by Value Line to be “electric utilities.”781 
Staff’s first and second criteria require that selected companies be tracked by 
Value Line and must have investment grade credit ratings of at least BBB-/Baa3 from both S&P 
and Moody’s.  Staff’s remaining criteria require that the selected companies currently pay 
dividends and derive at least 70% of their revenues from regulated utility operations per each 
company’s 2022 annual report, otherwise known as its SEC 10-K report.782 
 
Staff began constructing its proxy group by identifying 38 holding companies 
deemed by Value Line to be “electric utilities” because electric and gas holding companies’ 
operating risk characteristics are fundamentally similar.  From these 38 companies, Staff 
eliminated two: DTE Energy and Otter Tail Corporation, because their regulated revenues 
constituted less than 70% of total revenue.  From the remaining 36 companies, Staff eliminated 
Fortis Inc. because it is a Canadian-based company, which, although traded on both the Toronto 
and New York stock exchanges, has its financial reports and Value Line projections based on 
Canadian currency.  Exchange rate variations could therefore possibly affect the DCF 
calculation.  Three additional companies, Avangrid, PNM Resources, and Dominion Energy 
were eliminated because they were at that time involved in mergers or other transformative

physical networks that connect the customer to the source of supply.  They both also sell their 
products and services at regulated rates to customers.789  Therefore, Staff submits that the 
members of its proxy group are sufficiently similar to Central Hudson.

3.  
Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) 
a. 
Company’s DCF Methodologies 
The Company used a multi-stage DCF analysis based on a three-month average 
stock price and a range of near-term growth rate assumptions that produced a mean ROE of 
9.77% for its proxy group.  Specifically, the Company used a three-stage DCF analysis that relies 
on equity analysts’ earnings growth forecasts as published by Value Line, Zacks, and Yahoo 
Finance.791    
In testimony, Staff objected to the Company’s DCF approach.  Most importantly, 
Staff found the third stage growth rate to be excessive and contended that Mr. Nowak applied an 
inappropriate internal rate of return (IRR) tool for calculating the ROE estimate.  To a lesser 
extent, Staff also found excessive growth rates used in the first stage of Mr. Nowak’s analysis.  
Mr. Nowak’s use of an excessive third stage growth rate is the principal reason that the 9.77% 
DCF-based ROE recommendation is flawed.792   
Mr. Nowak’s DCF model uses earnings growth rates to determine short-term 
dividend growth rates, ignoring available dividend growth rates or forecasts.793  This is in direct 
contrast to the basic premise of the DCF methodology, which measures the present value of 
future dividends.  Utilizing earnings growth assumes that dividend growth will match earnings 
growth, and Mr. Nowak’s testimony fails to present evidence to support such an expectation.794   
Staff also objected to Mr. Nowak’s use of a historically derived Gross Domestic 
Product rate in conjunction with a forecasted inflation rate as the long-term growth rate of his 
 
790  Case 20-G-0101, Corning – Gas Rates, Order Establishing Rates and Rate Plan (issued May 19, 
2021), pp. 40-42 (2021 Corning Rate Order). 
791  Tr. 186. 
792  Tr. 2464-2467. 
793  Tr. 186-187. 
794  Tr. 2459-2463.  


--- Page 174 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
166 
proxy group is flawed.795  Staff contended that a historical average of actual or real GDP growth 
may not be reflective of the growth prospects of the proxy group holding companies.  The use of 
a historical GDP growth rate along with expected inflation as a long-term future growth rate for 
utility stocks also ignores changes that might be occurring with each proxy group members’ 
common stock.796  Simply put, Staff did not consider Mr. Nowak’s use of the historically derived 
actual or real GDP as the long-term growth rate to be appropriate because historical growth rates 
measure time periods that encompass far different circumstances relative to current economic 
conditions.   
In contrast, Staff advocated the use of a 4.43% long-term growth rate, which is 
closer to the forward-looking GDP estimate by the Blue Chip Economic Indicators (BCEI) 
publication.  The BCEI publication reflects the forward-looking consensus estimate of nearly 50 
of the financial community’s prominent economists.797  Mr. Nowak’s 5.45% long-term growth 
rate, by contrast, is approximately 36% higher than the BCEI forecasted nominal GDP growth 
rate.   
The excessiveness of Mr. Nowaks’s long-term GDP growth estimate, and 
specifically the use of the historically derived real GDP as the long-term growth rate, is 
inappropriate because historical growth rates are necessarily measured over a subjective time 
period.  In this instance, that time period involves far different circumstances than exist today. 
Historical average growth rates by their very nature ignore economic conditions today.798   
  While Staff utilizes the IRR function in its DCF analysis, Mr. Nowak employed 
the XIRR function.  In his rebuttal, Mr. Nowak made three arguments to justify his use of the 
XIRR.  First, he argued that Staff’s DCF analysis inappropriately relies on the regular version of 
Excel’s IRR formula, which “makes the simplifying assumption that cash flows are available at a 
set time in each period.  In contrast, the XIRR function allows additional inputs as to the timing 
of cash flows.”799  Staff rejects this argument, however, because Mr. Nowak ’s own DCF Excel
