worksheet calculations contradict this assertion.800  Staff found that Mr. Nowak’s Excel 
worksheet mismatched his DCF dividend cash flow projections, resulting in overstated DCF 
results.  Using ALLETE as an example, Mr. Nowak’s estimated annualized dividend per share as 
of May 31, 2023 was $2.71; his estimated average annual growth rate for the first stage was 
7.47%; and the forecasted dividend one year from May 31, 2023 is $2.91 (i.e., 
$2.71*(1+7.47%)).801  Surprisingly, Mr. Nowak’s Excel worksheet shows that the timing for the 
$2.91 dividend cash flow is dated November 30, 2023 which is approximately six months later 
than May 31, 2023.  This demonstrates a mismatch of cash flow and timing.   
Staff believes that the correct date for the timing of the $2.91 dividend payment 
should be May 31, 2024, not November 30, 2023.  Were Mr. Nowak to properly account for the 
timing of the dividend cash flows, his DCF results would have produced results more consistent 
with the results of the regular IRR.  Overall, Staff believes Mr. Nowak’s use of the XIRR 
overstates his mean DCF result of 9.77% for his proxy group by approximately 19 BPs.   
On cross-examination, the Company implied that the use of the XIRR was more 
accurate by asking the Staff witness if all companies in the Staff and Company proxy groups 
paid dividends on a quarterly basis.802  The implication would seem to be that XIRR would be a 
more accurate indicator of investors’ expectations regarding when they receive and presumably 
reinvest their dividend payments.  However, a rational investor would be indifferent to this 
because a company that pays dividends on a quarterly basis has less use of its earnings to create 
growth, thereby reducing the company’s sustainable growth.  This could. in turn, result in 
reducing an investor’s required return. 
This is not the first time that utility ROE witnesses have argued that Staff annual 
dividend DCF model ignores time value of money and the Commission has rejected such 
argument in the past.  In Case 08-E-0539, a Con Edison electric rate case, the Commission 
stated: 
[t]he Company replies that insistence on use of an annual dividend 
would have us improperly ignore the time value of money and bond 
math… [w]e are not adopting the judges’ recommendation that our 
 
800  Exhibit 134, p. 1.  
801  Exhibit 134, p. 1. 
802  Tr. 2506-2508.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419

DCF estimates reflect the payment of dividends quarterly rather than 
annually at the end of each year. Any extra return to be achieved on 
account of quarterly dividend reinvestment will be achieved by 
those who actually reinvest all their dividends in the Company’s 
stock. Any additional allowance would be duplicative for those who 
actually reinvest dividends and unnecessarily generous to those who 
do not.  Accordingly, the Company's proposed 20-basis point 
upward adjustment to our DCF results is rejected.803 
 
Acknowledging that both Staff and the Commission have consistently relied on 
the regular IRR in DCF-based ROE calculations in both litigated and negotiated cases, Staff 
recommends that the Commission reject Mr. Nowak’s use of the irregular internal rate of return 
and adopt Staff’s DCF methodology. 
b. 
Staff’s DCF Methodologies 
Staff’s DCF model, which has its roots in the Recommended Decision (RD) of the 
Generic Finance Proceeding and Commission precedent, adopts a two-stage model.  This model 
explicitly recognizes that short-term growth expectations do not necessarily equal long-term 
growth expectations.804  The first stage of Staff’s model uses Value Line near-term dividend 
estimates to derive a short-term dividend growth rate.  The second stage is based on a sustainable 
growth rate calculation.  This calculation is derived from both internal and external growth rates 
using Value Line three-to-five year estimates of earnings per share, dividend per share, book 
value per share, and amount of common stock outstanding for each of the proxy group 
companies. 805  This data is used to estimate the dividends that can be expected for each company 
in the future.  The results are then used to calculate the discount rate (return) required to turn the 
stream of expected dividend payments into the current stock price.806  Given the prominence of 
the DCF methodology in cost of equity determinations in general, the most critical element is the 
assumed long-term dividend growth rate.

The Company argued that Staff’s DCF model has shortcomings.  The Company

sustainable growth rate projects sustainable growth for 195 years.807 
In these cases, Staff employs the same DCF analysis that the Commission has 
accepted in all fully litigated rate proceedings over the past 25 years, including cases involving 
Con Edison, National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation, and Corning Natural Gas 
Corporation.808  This analysis is predicated on the fundamentally sound and internally consistent

reasonableness of the sustainable growth rate, Staff compared its 4.43% sustainable growth rate

growth rate estimated by the Company.

Staff’s sustainable growth calculation relies on a prediction of expected future earned rates of 
return on common equity for a proxy group composed of mainly holding companies owning both 
regulated and unregulated business.  It is therefore expected that the holding companies in the 
proxy group will gain additional earnings from these unregulated operations.  Moreover, given

4. 
Capital Assset Pricing Model (CAPM) 
a. 
Company’s CAPM Methodologies 
Mr. Nowak’s CAPM results range between 10.80% to 11.14%, with a mean of 
10.97%.  Mr. Nowak provided a total of 12 CAPM-derived ROE estimates for his proxy group 
using the same CAPM methodologies that Staff employed.  Mr. Nowak calculated 6 ROEs using 
the traditional CAPM methodology and 6 ROEs using the zero-beta CAPM methodology.  His 
calculations used two different beta sources in combination with three different risk-free rate 
estimates for each version of the CAPM.812  Staff has two principal concerns regarding Mr. 
Nowak’s CAPM analysis: (1) his derivation of a 12.12% market return; and (2) his risk-free 
rate.813    
 
811  2009 Con Edison Rate Order, p. 126. 
812  Tr. 144.  
813  Tr. 2475-2476.       


--- Page 179 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
171 
Mr. Nowak averages two inputs, specifically the Merrill Lynch Quantitative 
Profile (MLQP) and an expected return on the S&P 500, to calculate his market return estimate.  
While Mr. Nowak’s MLQP input is slightly different from Staff’s MLQP input due to the 
timeframe of the measurement, the resulting market returns were within 5 basis points of each 
other.  Staff found Mr. Nowak’s S&P 500 input, however, to be flawed.  That derivation relied in 
part upon a constant growth DCF analysis of the S&P 500.  This resulted in a much higher 
market return estimate of 13.46%.   
Staff also contests the Company’s risk-free rate methodology, as it does not 
adequately reflect investors’ investment horizon.  Although in this instance the risk-free rate 
calculated by the Company is lower than Staff’s due to a significant increase in interest rates 
between when Mr. Nowak performed his analysis and when Staff performed its analysis, Staff 
still believes its approach is more consistent with the CAPM’s intent.   
Mr. Nowak calculated the risk-free rate using three different inputs.  The first is a 
three-month average of the 30-year treasury yields for a recent three-month period.  That figure 
was 3.77%, based on the average of March 2023 to May 2023 yields.  The second is a near-term 
projected 30-year treasury yield for the five forward-looking quarters, namely the third quarter of 
2023 through the third quarter of 2024.  This average was 3.78%.  The third is the long-term 
projected 30-year treasury yield for the period of 2025 through 2029.  That yield was 3.80%.814   
The expectation that all utility equity investors have a 30-year investment time 
horizon is inappropriate because it fails to recognize that investors have both intermediate and 
long-term horizons.  Thus, the Company’s claims are not aligned with the Commission’s 
preference for an average of 10-year and 30-year treasury yields.815  Moreover, one needs to look 
no further than the Company’s debt portfolio, which is largely comprised of 10-year and 30-year 
long-term debt obligations, for further evidence that investors have different time horizons.816  
Therefore, Staff believes that averaging the 10-year and 30-year current treasury yields is 
appropriate and consistent with the Commission’s preferred approach.

b. 
Staff’s CAPM Methodologies 
Staff employed both the “traditional” and “zero-beta” CAPM methods, albeit with 
different inputs than the Company.  Staff’s resulting risk-free rate is the average of 10-year and 
30-year treasury bond yields.  For the three months ended September 30, 2023, the average rate 
was 4.19%.817   
Staff utilized only Value Line betas, which are superior to those used by Mr. 
Nowak.  Value Line betas are based upon a reasonable estimation period of five years, as 
compared to the 10-year estimation period used by Mr. Nowak for Bloomberg’s betas.818  
Although a 10-year estimation period contains more data points, Staff believes that a beta 
estimated over a longer estimation period is more likely to be biased.  A 10-year estimation 
contains more, possibly irrelevant historical observations, including results related to dissimilar 
circumstances.  These may include events related to corporate restructuring or industry 
transformation that may not represent the level of earnings volatility expected in the future.819  
Thus, a shorter estimation period is more reasonable.  The average Value Line beta of the Staff 
proxy group was 0.91.820   
Staff uses the market return estimate provided by Merrill Lynch, as published in 
its monthly Quantitative Profiles.  This estimate is reliable, publicly available, and a highly 
respected source.821  The Merrill Lynch report uses a multi-stage dividend discount model 
(“Implied market return”) and a CAPM (“Required market return”) to calculate an expected 
return for the S&P 500 each month.  The risk-free rate was then subtracted from the market 
return estimate to arrive at the market risk premium (MRP).   
The three-month average market return estimate as of September 2023 is 10.83%.  
Subtracting our 4.19% risk-free rate results in a MRP of 6.64%.822  This method of calculating an 
MRP has been used by the Commission in several litigated rate cases, most recently in Case 20-

ROE used to calculate the Company’s overall cost of capital.  As discussed earlier, Staff’s inputs 
for the MRP, the risk-free rate, and beta source are reasonable.  Additionally, Staff’s 
methodology has been upheld by the Commission in Case 16-G-0257, and the Commission’s 
adoption of Staff’s methodology in that case was and is an explicit rejection of Mr. Nowak’s 
CAPM approach.825  As stated in its Order in those proceedings:  
[T]he Commission has repeatedly affirmed certain key elements of 
the methodology we use in determining the appropriate cost of 
equity to be included in rates.  Those elements consist of the 
application of Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) and Capital Asset 
Pricing Model (CAPM) analyses to a representative proxy group of 
utility companies; the use of a two-stage DCF computation with 
inputs derived from Value Line; the basing of CAPM results on an 
average of the outcome from standard and zero-beta models with a 
risk-free rate based on Treasury bonds, market risk premium 
provided by Merrill Lynch’s Quantitative Profiles, and betas taken 
from Value Line….”826 
 
Staff submits that the Commission should continue its policy of employed the 
methodology that Staff has used here. 
c. 
DCF/CAPM Weighting 
Staff recommends that the Commission adopt its relative weighting of the CAPM 
and DCF.  The Company argued that “the Commission’s 25 years of experience with the CAPM 
since [the Generic Finance RD] now provides a sound basis for altering the weighting of the two 
ROE methodologies and raising the CAPM ‘to Parity with the DCF analysis’”827  The Company

Mr. Nowak’s claim that other utility regulatory bodies like the Illinois Commerce

Another regulatory body’s opinion should not trump the Commission’s own long-standing 
practices for New York State utilities.  Staff recommends that the Commission adhere to its long-
standing approach.  
Concerning Mr. Nowak’s point that interest rates are expected to further increase 
to combat inflation, this assumption is simply not supported by facts.  While the Federal Reserve 
has certainly raised rates in the last 24 months in an effort to reduce inflation, indications are that

rates as inflation fears subside.  Therefore, it is unlikely based on the current economy that 
interest rates will increase during the Rate Year.830  Also, Staff contends that the best estimate of 
future long-term interest rates is a no-change forecast and that bond investors have incorporated 
all relevant information when making their investment decisions.831  
Regarding Mr. Nowak’s contention that the DCF and the CAPM should be 
accorded equal weight because the DCF method is currently producing ROE results that are 
significantly lower than the results of the CAPM, Staff notes that it has demonstrated that Mr. 
Nowak’s DCF results are not understated.  Had Mr. Nowak employed more reasonable 
assumptions in his CAPM analysis, its results would not have been artificially inflated.  It is 
therefore not surprising that a significant gap exists between his DCF analysis and his CAPM

evidence that would compel the Commission to reject its long-standing policy of a two-third and 
one-third weighting of the DCF and CAPM.  The Commission should reject Mr. Nowak’s 
approach as it has done numerous times before.

5. 
ROE Recommendation 
a. 
Company’s ROE Request 
In these proceedings, the Company proposed a 9.8% ROE for the Rate Year.  The 
Company’s ROE request of 9.8% was selected from the low end of a range of six cost of equity 
estimates that were generated from DCF and CAPM methodologies utilizing a combined utility 
proxy group of companies classified as “electric” and “gas” by Value Line.834   
The principal flaws in Mr. Nowak’s methodologies have already been addressed 
above.  These include the inappropriate market risk premium used in his CAPM approach; the 
selection criteria relating to net operating income, analyst coverage, and generation MWH sales; 
the inappropriate use of the extended version of the Internal Rate of Return (XIRR) in his DCF 
analyses; the use of an excessive growth rate in his long run growth rate; and the excessive 
growth rates used in the first stage of his DCF analysis.  Because of these flaws, Staff submits 
that the Company’s request for a 9.8% ROE is not supported by the record.   
b.  
Staff’s ROE Results 
Staff instead recommends a 9.2% ROE based on a two-thirds weighting of its 
DCF result and a one-third weighting of its CAPM result.  While Staff’s ROE of 9.2% is below 
the national average return on equity authorizations, it is important to consider that, as described 
by Mr. Nowak, the authorized ROE should afford the Company an opportunity to earn an ROE 
commensurate with the return on equity for investments in enterprises having comparable 
risks.835  To that end, Staff asserted that its methodology, which relies on a sound, time-tested 
financial analysis of a proxy group of electric utility holding companies whose risks are 
comparable to the Companies, produces just such a result.   
First, Staff’s ROE is consistent with investor’s expectations of ROEs in New 
York.836 Second, it is sufficient to balance the interests of both ratepayers and the Company’s 
investors by having the lowest cost of capital for an acceptable level of risk without sacrificing 
 
834  Tr. 175-176. 
835  Tr. 148.    
836  Tr. 2446.

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
177 
service quality.837  Finally, it provides the Company the ability to access the credit market at 
reasonably priced interest rates.838  These three points will be discussed in turn.  
With respect to investor ROE expectations, it is reasonable to assume that equity 
investors derive their expectations for utility stocks, in part, from the relevant state commission’s 
customary approach to setting ROEs.  Accordingly, a rational New York investor would not 
expect the Commission to authorize an ROE of 9.80% for Central Hudson.839  Rather, rational 
investors, who are presumably aware of the Commission’s preference for a formulaic approach 
to determining the cost of common equity would expect an ROE within a range between the 
Company’s current authorized 9.0% ROE and the 9.20% to 9.25% ROEs authorized under recent 
settlements involving Con Edison, NYSEG, and RG&E.840 
In addition, the Commission’s policy for utility regulation incorporates many 
critical elements that bolster a utility’s ability to achieve its authorized return on equity.  For 
instance, the Commission relies on a fully forecasted test year to determine revenue requirement, 
which recognizes changes in revenues and expenses expected through the end of the rate year.  
The Commission generally allows for a high level of expense reconciliation for material cost 
items such as environmental remediation, pensions, and OPEB expenses.  The Commission has 
also implemented a purchase power adjustment clause that allows full and timely recovery of this 
large and volatile cost element.   
Many other jurisdictions do not incorporate these mechanisms into their utility 
rate plans.  For overall blanket-type protections, New York State, unlike most other states, allows 
utilities to request deferral accounting for material items actually incurred, but not provided for, 
in the base forecasts.  In addition, New York State allows utilities to revise their rate filings 
during the suspension period for known and material changes.841  Finally, New York utilities are

In its rebuttal, the Company downplayed the risk-reducing mechanisms in New

Regarding additional risks derived from holding the Company to certain

jurisdictions.”844  This is a mischaracterization of the performance adjustments that Staff 
recommends.  They are not penalties.  They are negative adjustments for missed metrics, just as 
there are positive adjustments available to Central Hudson if it achieves certain goals with 
positive metrics. 
Staff’s ROE and capital structure recommendations balance the interests of both 
ratepayers and the Company’s investors by having the lowest cost of capital for an acceptable 
level of risk without jeopardizing service quality.845  Adoption of Staff’s resulting overall cost of 
capital recommendation will save Central Hudson ratepayers $13.6 million on a pre-tax basis.846  
For these reasons, Staff recommends the Commission determine an ROE of 9.2%.

D. 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
Staff has reviewed PULP’s Earnings Sharing Mechanism (ESM)847 and does not 
support it.  Initially, Staff notes that ESMs are typically a component of multi-year settlement 
agreements.  They are not generally incorporated into one-year litigated rate orders.  Moreover, 
this specific proposal is flawed.  Although sharing begins right away once the authorized return 
level has been reached, the sharing would be split “evenly” with the Company.848  Under such a 
scenario, it is possible that the Company could receive more excess earnings than it would under 
more typical earnings sharing plans that have been adopted by the Commission.   
In a typical multi-year agreement, excess earnings are kept by the Company for a 
range above the allowed ROE, known as the “deadband.”  They are shared 50/50 between the 
Company and ratepayers for a certain range, but the sharing is then reduced to 75/25 and 
eventually 90/10 in favor of ratepayers as the amount of excess earnings above the allowed ROE 
increases.  Additionally, the inclusion of a “deadband” is beneficial to ratepayers because it 
provides the Company with a financial incentive to control its costs while simultaneously 
ensuring customers an opportunity to share in those efficiency gains in future rate proceedings.  
PULP’s proposed elimination of the “deadband” would eliminate this financial incentive.  For 
these reasons, Staff recommends that the Commission reject PULP’s proposal.         
VII. 
NEGATIVE REVENUE ADJUSTMENTS 
A. 
Applicability in a One-Year Case 
The Commission should adopt Staff’s recommended NRAs, notwithstanding that 
this case is proceeding on a litigated track that is likely to result in a one-year rate plan.  The 
Commission possesses the authority to do so, having previously rejected arguments to the 
contrary.849  And although incentives like NRAs first “arose as useful regulatory practices at a 
time when the many benefits of multi-year rate plans began to be recognized in the late 1980’s 
and early 1990’s,”850 the Commission has nevertheless repeatedly employed them in one-year

Staff notes that the Commission’s 2017 NFGD Order is not to the contrary.  That

Under these circumstances, there is ample reason to believe that Central Hudson’s 
ratepayers would benefit from even stronger incentives for the Company to improve its 
reliability, safety, and customer service performance.  To the extent those incentives prove 
effective, they can be promptly revisited given the projected short duration of its forthcoming 
rate plan.  NRAs are warranted notwithstanding that this is a litigated proceeding.

VIII. 
ELECTRIC RELIABILITY 
A. 
CAIDI and SAIFI Targets and Associated NRA 
Reliability Performance Mechanisms (RPMs) are company-wide performance 
targets established by the Commission in rate orders; companies are subjected to negative 
revenue adjustments for failing to meet their reliability targets.  The Commission relies on two 
primary metrics to measure Central Hudson’s current reliability performance: the System 
Average Interruption Frequency Index (SAIFI or frequency), and the Customer Average 
Interruption Duration Index (CAIDI or duration).  Each metric is used to monitor the Company’s 
performance in a calendar year.  By compiling the interruption data provided by the individual 
utilities, the average frequency and duration of interruptions can be reviewed to assess the 
overall reliability of electric service statewide.  For 2023, Central Hudson is subject to a pre-tax,

30 BP potential electric NRA for failure to achieve an annual SAIFI target of 1.30, and a pre-tax, 
30 BP potential electric NRA for failure to achieve an annual CAIDI of 2.50 hours.858   
In its initial filings, Central Hudson proposed to modify the current SAIFI 
negative revenue adjustment to a two-step structure, whereby measurement of annual 
performance would include an NRA of 10 BPs for exceeding the minimum threshold of 1.30 and 
an additional NRA of 20 BPs for exceeding a maximum threshold of 1.34, which maintains the 
existing maximum NRA of 30 BPs.859  According to the Company’s Policy Panel, this two-step 
penalty level for SAIFI would provide the Company with a financial incentive to improve 
reliability performance while maintaining aggressive target levels that are aligned with historical 
results.860  The Company also stated that the tier one target of 1.30 is aggressive based on its 
average SAIFI performance from 2018 through 2022, and that the tier two target of 1.34 aligns 
with the Company’s average performance from 2018 through 2022, which the Company believes

target for SAIFI, and instead maintain Central Hudson’s current SAIFI target of 1.30 for the Rate

been able to meet its SAIFI metrics in all but one year out of the last three.862  The Company 
only missed the target in 2021 due to a greater number of minor storm events.  In addition, the 
Company has the potential to show consistent improvement in SAIFI, based on over fifteen years 
of improved vegetation management practices and its targeted capital improvement projects.863  
Conversely, Central Hudson’s proposal to establish a two-tier SAIFI target, where the second-
tier threshold is significantly lower than the Company’s current metrics, would not encourage the 
Company to continue to improve its performance related to outages.

B. 
CAIDI and SAIFI Exclusions 
Central Hudson currently is allowed to exclude major storm outages, incidents 
resulting from a catastrophic event beyond the control of the Company, and any incident where 
problems beyond the Company's control involving generation or the bulk transmission system is 
the key factor in the outage, from SAIFI and CAIDI calculations.865  The Company proposes to 
expand the list of incidents that it deems are factors beyond its control that can be excluded from 
SAIFI and CAIDI calculations.  Central Hudson classifies these as “Non-Utility Control 
Outages.” According to the page 75 of the ECOP’s testimony, Non-Utility Control Outages 
would include, but are not limited to, outages due to vandalism; unexpected deforestation, for 
example, due to the Emerald Ash Borer; foreign utility supply; motor vehicle accidents; weather; 
strategic pole hits; and disruptions in neighboring utility systems.  The Company cites to the 
Commission’s November 19, 2020 Order in Cases 19-E-0378 et al., involving electric and gas

rates for NYSEG and RG&E, as precedent for the type of exclusions that Central Hudson seeks 
in these rate proceedings.866   
Preliminarily, the 2020 NYSEG/RG&E Rate Order approved a three-year rate 
plan that resulted from a negotiated settlement, and the inclusion of certain provisions within that 
negotiated rate plan does not otherwise demonstrate why Central Hudson’s proposed SAIFI and 
CAIDI exclusions are reasonable in the context of these litigated rate proceedings, or why they 
should be approved here.  Regarding exclusions from the RPM calculations, Staff notes that a 
waiver process is already available to the Company, where a utility may petition the Commission 
directly if it believes extraordinary circumstances exist that warrant excluding an event from 
being counted towards the utility’s reliability metrics.867  The Commission explained this case-
by-case approach in its Order issued on October 12, 2004 in Case 02-E-1240.  Moreover, 
reliability performance targets are set at levels that provide a utility some flexibility in meeting 
the targets while still maintaining adequate reliability standards, and it would  not be appropriate 
to maintain the Company’s current SAIFI and CAIDI targets if these exclusions were universally 
granted without a case-by-case review.  For all of those reasons, the Commission should reject 
Central Hudson’s proposed expansion of the types of events that would be excluded from SAIFI 
and CAIDI calculations.

prolonged periods of colder temperatures could cause more leaks.879  However, the opposite is 
also true: in periods of warm weather, the Company could potentially benefit from fewer 
leaks.880  
The Company also took issue with Staff’s recommended target of 49 leaks, 
despite the fact that other gas utilities in the state had higher numbers of leaks compared to 
Central Hudson.881  However, different operating companies have different operating

characteristics, and service territories vary from a region to another and from a system to

purposes of establishing a particular utility’s metrics, would therefore be an “apples to oranges” 
comparison.883  In any event, Staff’s recommendations herein were based specifically on Central 
Hudson’s gas system and performance. 
Regarding Staff’s recommendation to discontinue the existing PRA associated 
with this metric, the Company argued that PRAs should continue because “[a]n appropriate level 
of PRAs coupled with reasonable ‘reach’ targets motivate improved performance” and “PRAs 
will drive increased levels of performance because attainment requires increased creativity, 
effort, and resources.”  Staff notes, however, that the purpose of a PRA is to incentivize the 
Company to reduce its leak backlog to a level that is more manageable on annual basis and to 
reduce methane emissions from leaks that would have otherwise gone unrepaired.884  Given the 
recent Company’s annual level leak backlogs, Staff is confident that providing an incentive to 
reduce the backlog and methane emissions is no longer needed.  Additionally, PRAs are intended 
to reward superior performance, and the Company should not be rewarded for simply complying 
with the requirements of 16 NYCRR Part 255 related to leak management.885  The Company 
incurred no NRAs associated with this metric for the years 2019 through 2021.  However, the 
Company earned PRA BPs as follow: zero BPs in 2019, four BPs in 2020 valued at $117,200, 
and four BPs in 2021 valued at $136,400.886 
Based on the foregoing, Staff’s recommended Leak Management targets and 
associated revenue adjustments represent a reasonable and appropriate framework that provides 
the Company with achievable targets, while ensuring that the Company continues to work to 
maintain and improve safety on its gas system.  For those reasons, the Commission should adopt 
Staff’s recommended targets and revenue adjustments.

Staff’s recommended metrics and associated revenue adjustments are based on the

Based on information provided by the Company, Staff recognizes that the 
Company’s 2022 performance at a rate of 1.28 per 1,000 one-call tickets is better than the 
statewide average level.894  Staff’s recommendation is based on the Company’s performance in 
the past five years with an average of approximately 1.40 per 1,000 one-call tickets.  Staff 
believes that the Company can be a lead in the state based on its performance in both leak 
management and damage prevention.895  Staff also took in consideration the fact that as the 
Company replaces older LPP, damages due to mismarks should decrease because, generally, the 
older the pipe, the greater the chance that the Company has incomplete records, including 
inaccurate location information.  When older pipes are replaced with a newer pipeline for which 
the Company knows the exact locations, including direction of run and depth and GPS 
coordinates, this allows the Company to use updated and accurate mapping during the mark-out 
process, greatly reducing the chances for error.896  Finally, Staff notes that the Company earned 
10 BPs in 2019 valued at $257,500; 10 BPs in 2020 valued at $293,000; and 10 BPs in 2021 
valued at $341,000.897  This performance indicates that the Company is able to meet and exceed 
its currently-established targets, and adjusting those targets will ensure that the Company 
continues to improve its performance related to damage prevention. 
Based on the foregoing, Staff’s recommended metrics and associated revenue 
adjustments discussed herein represent reasonable and appropriate targets based on the 
Company’s historical performance, and will help ensure that the Company continues to improve 
its performance while maintaining its position as a leader in the state with respect to damage 
prevention.  For those reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendations.

Under the current structure, the cumulative maximum exposure for record and 
field violations is 75 BPs in each calendar year.  Record violations are currently capped at ten 
violations for each code requirement.  If the Company incurs more than ten violations for a 
single code section, the Company must submit a remediation plan.901

and another specific section of 16 NYCRR Part 255 or 261.902

Staff recommends more stringent targets to emphasize the importance of 
compliance with pipeline safety regulations, and to incentivize the Company to improve its 
performance year after year.  The Company should strive to reduce its number of violations to 
zero.  Staff also does not recommend any associated PRAs for this performance measure because 
these audits and investigations are focused on compliance with the minimum pipeline safety 
requirements, and therefore the Company should not be compensated for satisfying the 
obligation to comply with minimum pipeline safety requirements.  Staff further recommends 
capping, at ten, the violations for the total record audit of the code sections identified in 
Exhibit__(SPSP-3) that may result in an NRA.904  Staff recommends no cap to field audit and 
investigation violations, as these are typically discovered when Pipeline Safety Staff witnesses 
the Company performing an action or a task.  Notwithstanding Pipeline Safety Staff’s presence, 
violations of the minimum pipeline safety regulations or of Company’s procedures still occur.  
 
904  Tr. 2696-2697; Exhibit 336. 


--- Page 202 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
194 
Therefore, any violation discovered during field audit or investigation could indicate a lack of 
competency in performing the action or task or lack of safety culture within the company.905  
Additionally, Staff considers more than 10 record audit violations of a single code section to be 
gross non-compliance with regulatory requirements, which may necessitate additional action.  If 
the Company incurs more than 10 record audit violations of a single code section, Staff 
recommends that the Commission require the Company to file with the Secretary to the 
Commission in this proceeding, within 90 days of Pipeline Safety Staff’s audit letter, a 
remediation plan explaining how it will address and resolve the compliance issues.  This plan 
would include dates by which all cited violations will be brought into compliance or, where 
appropriate, when remedial actions will be taken to mitigate recurrence.906   
The Commission should reject the Company’s proposal that, for record audits, 
only the documentation required to be performed during the calendar year prior to the calendar 
year in which the record is conducted may constitute a non-compliance under the safety 
regulations measure.  Staff conducts High-risk records audits annually, and Other-risk record 
audits at various intervals, which may exceed one year.  Therefore, Staff’s record audit should 
not be limited to the documentation from the prior calendar year.  Staff also conducts records 
audits of projects that extend over several years, and any non-compliance found is to be 
considered as a violation.  Any violation that is not captured under this measure remains subject 
to a potential penalty action under PSL §25-a.907   
Central Hudson took issue with Staff’s proposed safety targets as “not in 
alignment with other New York State gas utilities.”908  Staff notes, however, that the new targets 
for this safety metric discussed above will be recommended by Staff in an any future rate case 
for all gas utility companies.909  Staff has already recommended the same stringent targets in the

In its initial testimony, the Company stated that, as directed in the Order Adopting

the beginning of this program: (1) residential natural gas customers through the Company’s 
“CenHub” store, by receiving a one-time credit of 50% savings off the retail price of the 
detector; and (2) natural gas customers who received Home Energy Assistance Program 
(HEAP).921  The Company proposed to continue the existing program during the Rate Year, 
while re-evaluating and researching enhancements to the program in accordance with newly 
developed National Fire Prevention Association guidelines.  The Company anticipates that an 
enhanced program would be established and rolled out during a future Rate Year.922  In its 
response to DPS-574, the Company stated that it replaced 14 methane detector units in 2021, 
nine units in 2022, and zero units in 2023.923  The Company also confirmed that the RMD 
program is now restricted to providing replacement units to customers that have a methane 
detector that has operated/alarmed or returned for a false positive.  The Company further added 
that it intends to review the program in 2024 and will have an approved plan in place by 
December 31, 2024, with a planned execution targeted for 2025.  In its response to DPS-729, the 
Company confirmed that it did not purchase any new RMD units during years 2021, 2022, and 
2023.924   
Staff agrees that the Company should continue providing RMD units to its 
customers.  However, Staff recommends that part of the $100,000 currently funded through the 
Company’s O&M approved by the 2021 Rate Order be applied towards researching and 
developing an enhanced RMD program, with connected methane detector devices that make use 
of Long-Range Wide Area Network technology with long device battery life, similar to RMD

H. 
Pipeline Safety Management System 
A Pipeline Safety Management System, or PSMS, is the American Petroleum 
Institute’s recommended practice (API RP 1173) that establishes an end-to-end safety framework 
for pipeline operators under jurisdiction of the US Department of Transportation.926  The goal of 
PSMS is to result in zero safety accidents or incidents.  In its initial testimony, the Company 
reported that, in the past three years, it has taken various steps towards implementing PSMS.927  
The Company stated that it is continuing to pursue PSMS as a focus by hiring a new employee 
on May 30, 2023 dedicated to lead the Company’s PSMS implementation.928  The Company 
further noted that it will continue to develop its PSMS program and anticipates a completed 
Road Map in 2023, with findings and executing opportunities identified in the Road Map to 
begin in 2024 and continue through subsequent rate years.929  The Company also stated that it 
will continue to participate in the Northeast Gas Association PSMS Committee to implement 
technical and tactical guides, communicate what PSMS is and its benefits, and to enhance the 
resource sharing on-line portal.  
In the 2021 Rate Order, the Commission approved the Company’s plan to develop 
a PSMS program and authorized $549,000 over a three-year plan for this effort.930  In these rate 
proceedings, the Company proposed $250,000 in funding for the Rate Year and for two 
additional rate years, totaling $765,605 of incremental expense, primarily for consultants, to 
implement its PSMS program.  To support its Rate Year request, the Company provided only a 
one-page document with no meaningful descriptions of actual services to be performed, and no

23-G-0083.  However, such issues are better suited to be addressed by the Commission in Case 
23-G-0083.945  Accordingly, Staff recommends that the Commission take no action in these rate 
proceedings regarding Permalock Tapping Tee Assemblies.

X. 
CUSTOMER SERVICE 
As we noted at the outset of this Initial Brief, these rate proceedings have 
unfolded against the backdrop of issues stemming from Central Hudson’s 2021 deployment of its 
CIS and related management matters.  The Company has erroneously blamed its poor customer 
service record and increased arrears levels on high commodity costs,946 certain communications 
from outside organizations and elected officials,947 and the default of Columbia Utilities.948  
Central Hudson’s testimonies and discovery responses, however, indicate that the Company’s 
own actions and lack of customer communication have led to customer “confusion, and 
anxiety,”949 poor customer service, and high customer arrears.  Those actions include, among 
others, the failed launch of the Company’s customer billing system and its delayed 
implementation of automated collections within SAP.   
A. 
Customer Service Performance Metrics 
Customer service performance indicators, or CSPIs, are data points used by the 
Department’s Office of Consumer Services (OCS) to measure the utilities’ levels of customer 
service so that the Commission can determine whether the utilities are providing adequate 
service to their customers.  These CSPI mechanisms help align shareholder and ratepayer 
interests by tying earnings consequences to a utility’s customer service record.950 
Central Hudson currently has NRAs associated with the following three CSPI 
metrics: PSC Complaint Rate, Customer Satisfaction Survey, and Percent of Calls Answered 
Within 30 Seconds.  Per the Commission’s 2017 Order in Case 15-M-0566, the Company also 
reports on Estimated Bills and Appointments Kept on its monthly CSPI reports, but there are no 
NRAs associated with these two metrics.951

951  Case 15-M-0566, In the Matter of Revisions to Customer Service Performance Indicators Applicable 
to Gas and Electric Corporations, Order Adopting Revisions to Customer Service Reporting Metrics 
(issued August 4, 2017) (CSPI Order).

The Company proposed no changes to its current CSPI metric targets and to the

as outlined in the following sections. 
As detailed in the following sections, the SCSP recommended that the 
Commission continue the use of the three existing metrics: Call Answer Rate, Customer 
Satisfaction Survey, and PSC Complaint Rate.  The SCSP also recommended adding a fourth 
metric for Estimated Bills.  For the Call Answer Rate, the Panel recommended to maintain the 
existing target range of 67% to 55.8% and to increase the maximum NRA from 15 to 18 basis 
points.  For the Customer Satisfaction Survey, the SCSP agreed with the Company’s proposal to 
switch to the Statewide Pilot Survey format and to split the recipients into two categories based 
on the interaction types.  The SCSP proposed an increase in the total NRA for the Survey from 
15 to 18 basis points, splitting it evenly between the High Priority Interactions and the General 
Account and Billing Interactions.  The SCSP proposed no change to the target of 89%.  For the 
PSC Complaint Rate, the SCSP proposed maintaining the existing target rate of one and to 
increase the total NRA from 15 to 18 basis points at risk.  For the Estimated Bills metric, the 
SCSP proposed a target range of 1% to 10% and a total of 18 basis points at risk. 
1. 
PSC Complaint Rate 
On page 27 of its Customer Experience Panel’s initial testimony, the Company 
proposed to exclude complaints related to commodity prices from the PSC Complaint Rate 
metric while maintaining the existing target of 1.0.  The Company asserted that these complaints 
“are outside of the Company’s control,”952 and therefore “do not present a just cause for charging 
a complaint against the Company and do not show or point to any deficiency in the Company’s 
service to customers.”953  The Company also claimed that the exclusion would increase 
alignment across the state since Con Edison has a similar exclusion. 
On page 37 of the SCSP’s direct testimony, Staff recommended inclusion of 
commodity price-related complaints.  In response to DPS-355, the Company provided a root

Staff disagrees with the proposed exclusion of commodity price-related 
complaints for several reasons.  First, although commodity prices are beyond the Company’s 
control, escalated complaints are not.  Complaints are received by the Department call center 
after a customer is unable to resolve their issue with the Company.  The Quick Response System 
(QRS) utilized by the Department gives the Company an additional chance to resolve the 
customer’s issue before the complaint is escalated and reclassified as a Standard Resolution 
System (SRS) complaint that is counted in the PSC Complaint Rate metric.  Utilities also have 
an opportunity, on a case-by-case basis, to request that an SRS case be downgraded back to a 
QRS if they believe that a case was escalated without cause.  The Company thus has more than 
one opportunity to resolve a customer’s concern before it becomes an escalated complaint that is 
included in the PSC Complaint Rate metric. 
Second, Staff believes that excluding commodity price-related complaints would 
eliminate an incentive that encourages the Company to perform important customer outreach.  
The Company acknowledged as much when it stated that its requested “increase in Consumer 
Outreach staff and their work is aimed not only at resolving PSC complaints, but also at 
conducting important outreach activities in the communities we serve in order to answer 
questions before they become complaints.”964  Notably, Staff is recommending that the 
Commission approve the Company’s proposed Consumer Outreach staffing increase. 
The Company could also implement additional customer outreach measures when 
commodity prices are expected to increase.  These could include training Company 
representatives who handle customer calls to explain how commodity prices affect bills, which 
may reduce the number of initial QRS complaints, as well as the number of QRS complaints that

Complaint Rate metrics based only on the Company’s determinations as to which complaints fall 
into the commodity price-related category.  If the SAP CIS system is rendering inaccurate bills,

Staff and does not include data provided by a utility.  Allowing complaints to be excluded at the 
Company’s discretion, especially without independent validation, could inappropriately, and thus 
inaccurately, skew the PSC Complaint Rate metric.  This is particularly true when one considers

3. 
Call Answer Rate 
The Company stated in its initial testimony that it had met the target for Percent 
of Calls Answered by a Representative within 30 Seconds in 2021 and 2022.994 It further stated 
that it was not proposing any changes to the Percent of Calls Answered by a Representative 
within 30 Seconds metric in these rate cases.995  
Staff subsequently found that the Company had miscalculated its Percent of Calls 
Answered by a Representative within 30 Seconds, or Call Answer Rate, for at least the past five 
years.  On page 5 of the Appendix to the CSPI Order, the Commission defines “[t]he percent of 
calls answered by a representative” as “the percentage of total incoming calls requesting a 
 
991  Tr. 3128-3129.

representative that were answered by a representative within 30 seconds.”  The CSPI Order 
stipulates that the response rate is calculated by dividing the number of total calls answered 
within 30 seconds by the number of calls requesting a representative, less all calls abandoned 
within 30 seconds, and then multiplying by 100.  Page 60 of the Joint Proposal adopted by the 
Commission in the Company’s 2021 Rate Order defines the Call Answer Rate as the “percentage 
of calls answered by a Company representative within 30 seconds of the customer’s request to 
speak to a representative between the hours of 8:00 p.m. and 4:30 p.m. Monday through Friday 
(excluding holidays).”  The Joint Proposal also states that the “performance rate is the sum of the 
system-wide number of calls answered by a representative within 30 seconds divided by the sum 
of the system-wide number of calls where a customer requests to speak with a representative.” 
Furthermore, as stated by the SCSP, the Company’s Metrics Manual, which provides the 
calculations for each metric and the associated processes, aligns with these definitions: BEGIN 
CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION<< 
 
 
 
 
 
 >>END CONFIDENTIAL INFORMATION.996 
The Call Answer Rate reported by Central Hudson over the last five years, 
however, was calculated using a method that is inconsistent with the CSPI Order, the 2021 Rate 
Order, and even the Company’s own metrics instruction manual.997  Specifically, the Company’s 
response to DPS-718 explained that the Company’s callback queues have a targeted time frame 
of two hours for the customer to receive a call back, and “adherence to the two hour target [was] 
counted as if the call was answered within 30 seconds and included within the Percent of Calls 
Answered by a Representative Within 30 Seconds metric.”998  Also in its response to DPS-718, 
the Company disclosed that customers who are placed on virtual hold, which is the option to 
hang up, reserve a spot in the queue, and receive a call as soon as the next rep is available, are 
 
996  Tr. 4359. 
997  Tr. 4359. 
998  Exhibit 353 ( DPS-718).

CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
214 
considered answered once the option is accepted by the customer.999  But neither calls placed on 
virtual hold, nor return calls from a callback queue within two hours, constitute calls answered 
within 30 seconds in the Call Answer Rate metric per the parameters provided in the CSPI Order 
and the 2021 Rate Order. 
Based on the corrected Call Answer Rate calculations performed by Staff,1000 the 
Company should have incurred an NRA of 10 basis points, or approximately $1.27 million for 
2022, $600,000 for 2021, and $150,000 for 2020.  Staff recommended in its SCSP initial 
testimony that the Commission require the Company to recalculate its Call Answer Rate metric 
results for each month of 2020 through 2023 utilizing the correct formula and re-file the monthly 
results with the Secretary to the Commission under Case 15-M-0566.1001 
The Company has maintained that customer callbacks should be included within 
the numerator and denominator of the calculation and that this inclusion aligns with the intent 
and purpose of the Call Answer Rate metric.1002  The Company has further proposed a reduced 
target range of 63% to 52%, which is based on its performance during calendar years 2015 
through 2019 with callbacks included.1003  The Company confirmed that calls on virtual hold that 
are successfully reconnected with the customer who accepted the virtual hold transfer are part of 
the Company’s “service level”1004 or Call Answer Rate.  
Staff disagrees that the inclusion of callbacks within a two-hour time frame aligns 
with the intent and purpose of the Call Answer Rate metric and recommends that the 
Commission deny the Company’s request as inconsistent with the plain language in the CSPI 
Order and the 2021 Joint Proposal.  Staff’s position is that the intent and purpose of the Call 
Answer Rate metric is to measure the percentage of calls requesting to speak with representative 
that actually reach a live representative who is ready to address the customer’s concern when the 
call is answered.

The Company stated that offering “a callback option can reduce the number of

customer will have to wait for assistance.  Including callbacks and virtual hold interactions as 
answered within 30 seconds serves no purpose other than to allow the Company to artificially 
manipulate the metric as it does not increase the number of customers who are actually assisted 
by a representative within 30 seconds.   
 Also, the Company refers to the percent of calls answered by a representative 
within 30 seconds metric as its “Call Service Level.”1006 The Company confirmed that the calls 
on virtual hold have been part of the Service Level,1007 which Staff argues also conflicts with the 
CSPI Order.   
Staff maintains its recommendation that the Commission require the Company to 
recalculate its Call Answer Rate metric results for each month of 2020 through 2023 utilizing the 
correct formula as defined in the CSPI Order and to re-file the monthly results with the Secretary 
to the Commission under Case 15-M-0566.  Staff also recommends that the Commission deny 
the Company’s proposal in its rebuttal testimony to reduce the target range from 67% through 
55.8%, to 63% through 52%, to align with the Company’s performance in 2015-2019 with 
callbacks included.  This reduction would enable the Company to backslide without incurring an 
associated NRA, which is inconsistent with the purpose of NRAs. 
4. 
Estimated Bills 
In the SCSP’s initial testimony, Staff recommended that the Commission impose 
an Estimated Bill metric with an associated NRA that would begin at the start of the Rate Year to 
further incentivize the Company to provide actual meter reads as soon as possible.  Staff’s 
proposal included a four-tiered target structure with an associated basis point NRA for each tier.  
The first tier would apply if the Company rendered an average of 1% or less of its bills with an 
estimated meter read.  The second tier would apply if the Company has issued greater than 1% to 
less than or equal to 4.5% of its bills with an estimated meter read.  The third tier would apply if

the Company has issued more than 4.5% of estimated bill up to 10%.  Finally, the fourth tier

for determining these tiers is described in detail in its response to CH-028.1008  The total risk 
would be 18 BPs for the highest tier, and this metric would be applicable only to scheduled meter 
reads in a given month.1009 
UIU proposed that Percent of Estimated Bills be added to the Company’s current 
list of CSPIs.  UIU proposed the following targets and NRAs.  If the percentage of estimated 
bills exceeds 12.3%, then the Company would be subject to an NRA of 5 basis points.  If the 
percentage exceeds 13.3%, then the associated NRA would be 10 basis points.  And if the 
percentage exceeds 14.3%, then the NRA would equal 15 basis points.  UIU states that the 
addition of this metric with an associated NRA “will provide a safeguard for Central Hudson 
customers who experienced delayed and inaccurate invoices, confusion, and anxiety during the 
launch of the SAP CIS and who are now being asked to bear the costs for the Company to roll 
out monthly meter reading.”1010 
In its Customer Experience Panel’s rebuttal testimony, Central Hudson did not 
disagree with Staff’s proposal to add an Estimated Bill metric with an associated NRA.  The 
Company disagreed, however, with the targets and NRAs proposed by Staff.  The Company 
proposed a higher set of target tiers and recommended that its existing NRA exposure of 42 basis 
points be spread equally among the four metrics.  The Company proposed applying two standard 
deviations to its 2015-2019 to set the targets, which yields tiers of 3.5%, 4.9%, and 6.3%.1011

usage because the customer and/or other person who controls access to the meter refuses to 
provide access; and (3) estimated meter reads due to abnormal operating conditions that are 
outside of the Company’s control.  The Company stated that abnormal operating conditions are 
deemed to occur during any period of emergency, catastrophe, strike, natural disaster, major

B. 
Negative Revenue Adjustments 
In the initial testimony of its Customer Experience Panel, the Company proposed 
no changes to the negative revenue adjustments associated with the Company’s current CSPI 
Incentive Mechanism, which includes three metrics.  The current maximum total is 42 basis 
points, which was set forth in the 2021 Rate Order.1020 
The Staff Consumer Services Panel proposed in initial testimony to increase the 
maximum total NRAs in the Rate Year to 72 basis points, an increase of 30 basis points,1021 
across four metrics.  Staff proposed this increase to “effectively deter poor customer service 
going forward”1022 as the current NRA level has not motivated the Company to improve on its 
current customer service metrics.  This is evidenced by the fact that the Company has missed all 
of its metric targets for two consecutive years, namely calendar years 2022 and 2023.1023,1024,1025  
UIU proposed that each metric have tiered amounts of 5, 10, and a maximum of 
15 basis points at risk, yielding a maximum total of 60 BPs the four metrics, collectively.1026  
The Company stated in rebuttal that “any new or modified CSPIs adopted in these 
proceedings should not start until January 1, 2025, because any start date earlier than then may

not allow “for the Company to have knowledge of the available resources and the target that it is

Staff rejects this argument because the Company is aware of its existing targets

On page 64 of the 2017 NFGD Rate Order, the Commission stated, “In the 
Commission’s experience, appropriate incentives or rewards for effective performance have 
proven to be sound regulatory practices for which both utility companies and consumers are 
well-served.”1028  The Commission further stated, “The best performance incentives financially 
affect utilities in response to specific actions they take or do not take, or in response to the 
achievement or non-achievement of specific goals.”1029  For National Fuel Gas Distribution 
Corporation in the specific context of the 2017 Rate Order, the Commission found that the 
Company had demonstrated throughout the case that its customer service incentive mechanism 
and associated NRAs were not necessary, at that time, due to sustained high levels of customer 
service demonstrated by that utility’s performance on its applicable customer service metrics. 
Staff contends the opposite is true for Central Hudson, and, therefore, Staff 
strongly recommends the Commission establish Staff’s proposal for increased NRAs. The 
current NRA levels do not appear to be motivating the Company to improve on the current 
customer service metrics, as the Company has missed all of its metric targets for two consecutive 
years, calendar years 2022 and 2023.  As illustrated in Table 11, it was less expensive for the 
Company to incur the maximum NRA for missing the Call Answer Rate than it would have been 
to maintain a level of contractor resources to properly answer customer calls.  Furthermore, 
negative revenue adjustments in this one-year case are appropriate given Central Hudson’s 
inability to meet its current metrics due to its customer billing system transition and poor 
resource planning.

Additionally, to expeditiously address the Company’s performance, Staff

customer service situation, as described in previous sections, to be dire.  Applying sufficient 
leverage to incent the Company to improve its customer service should be a high priority for the 
Commission. 
1. 
Maximum BP Per Metric 
Currently, the maximum basis point value for the Company’s customer service 
performance indicator metrics are between 12 and 15 basis points depending on the metric.  The 
Company proposed no changes to the current maximum in its initial testimony.1030  It also 
proposed dividing its recommended 42 basis points evenly across the four metrics, meaning that 
each metric would have a total of 10.5 basis points at risk.  
The Staff Consumer Services Panel proposed a maximum of 18 basis points for 
each of the four metrics.1031  The maximum of 18 basis points for the Survey recipient categories 
would be split evenly to a maximum of 9 basis points for each survey group. 
The Company stated in the Customer Experience Panel’s rebuttal testimony that it 
has significant concerns with the SCSP’s proposal regarding negative revenue adjustments for 
several reasons.  The Company stated it believes the proposed maximum NRA level is 
“excessive and overly punitive;”1032 could result in financial uncertainty for the Company and its 
debt and equity investors; the metrics are “strongly correlated with one another;”1033 and the 
metrics are “strongly correlated with commodity price fluctuations.”1034  
Staff remains concerned that the Company’s recent performance has 
demonstrated that the current maximum basis point for the Company’s metrics is not an effective 
incentive to the Company to meet its current minimum targets for customer service.  This is

instead.

that the Company has missed several of its targets for its customer service metrics, the SCSP

quadrupling provisions to provide the Company with a strong financial incentive to improve its 
customer service performance and its communications with customers as soon as possible. 
Additionally, if the Company achieves acceptable performance levels by missing no metric 
targets for two consecutive years, Staff recommends that the Commission discontinue the 
proposed tripling and quadrupling provisions. Staff’s proposal is reasonable because it would 
effectively deter poor customer service at the Company in the Rate Year and thereafter.

C. 
Customer Bill Credits 
In response to an influx in customer complaints following the Company’s 
customer information system transition, the Staff Consumer Services Panel proposed several 
customer bill credits, at shareholders’ expense, as detailed below.  Staff recommended these 
credits as a means of improving the Company’s customer service performance and showing good 
will toward customers while the Company rebuilds customer trust. 
1. 
Estimated Bill Credit 
The Staff Consumer Services Panel recommended to the Commission that the 
Company provide a bill credit of $20 to each customer that received an estimated bill beginning 
in the Rate Year.1040  The SCSP stated these credits should be borne by shareholders.  However,  
Staff recommends that until monthly meter reading is implemented, the Company would not be 
required to provide the estimated bill credit for a bill based on a regularly scheduled estimated 
meter reading, or if the Company must rely on estimated usage because the customer and/or 
other person who controls access to the meter refuses to provide access.  
The Company objected to the proposal of Estimated Bill Credits and Adjusted 
Bill Credits, asserting: (1) some level of estimated bills and adjusted bills are normal, required, 
and expected within every billing system, and the practice of issuing an estimated or adjusted bill 
is contemplated and allowed under the PSL and the Company’s tariffs; establishment of a bill 
credit for every single estimated and adjusted bill will result in an inappropriate financial impact 
to the Company for routine billing estimates and corrections; (2) the establishment of an 
estimated bill credit is redundant to the Estimated Bill CSPI proposed by SCSP and results in an 
overly punitive mechanism especially during periods when the Company meets the Estimated 
Bill CSPI performance target; (3) establishment of an adjusted bill credit and a CDG adjusted 
bill credit is by definition redundant and overly punitive; (4) the amount of $20 was arbitrarily 
based on the existing Missed Appointment bill credit; and (5) the development of a fixed dollar 
amount bill credit is inappropriate and unfairly penalizes smaller utilities relative to larger 
 
1040 Tr. 4370-4371.  


--- Page 233 ---
CASES 23-E-0418 and 23-G-0419 
 
 
225 
utilities.1041  The Company argued that “adjusted bills and estimated bills are driven by a large 
number of factors many of which are not within the control of the Company.”1042 
Staff rejects the Company’s argument that the Estimated Bill Credit would result 
in an inappropriate financial impact.  The financial impact is appropriate if the Company fails to 
provide actual and accurate bills.  Moreover, while the Company protests about the financial 
impact, the Company has stated that the metrics should all return to baseline levels and the credit 
would be further incentive for the Company to avoid the financial impact by providing customers 
with accurate bills based on actual meter reads. Customers have been unhappy with estimated 
and adjusted bills, so granting this credit for each customer who is inconvenienced could help the 
Company restore customers’ faith in the Company.  Staff disagrees with the Company’s claim 
that “adjusted bills and estimated bills are driven by a large number of factors many of which are 
not within the control of the Company.” In fact, the accuracy of bills rendered by the SAP CIS 
system and those requiring manual intervention is within the Company’s control, as is providing 
monthly meter readings.

information system billing transition.  As shown in Table 13 below, this led to an increase in 
adjusted bills starting in 2021 and continuing through 2023:1044

Staff continues to recommend the $20 Adjusted CDG Billing Credit.  While 
issuing adjusted bills is permitted under the Company’s tariff, the volume of adjusted bills 
Central Hudson is issuing to its CDG customers is beyond what can be considered normal and 
expected business operations.  Customers should not have to expect that receiving multiple 
adjusted bills within a single month or dozens of adjusted CDG bills within a single year is a 
standard part of doing business with Central Hudson.  Issuing multiple adjusted bills, especially 
to CDG customers who already have additional billing lines and more complex billing issues, 
causes customer confusion, potential increases call volumes to the Company’s call center, and

Other New York utilities already offer similar credits.  As mentioned in the 
Company’s rebuttal, NYSEG and RG&E each offer a $10 CDG bill credit,1065 which was 
established in their most recent rate proceedings.1066  NYSEG and RG&E customers receive this 
credit when their utility fails to issue an accurate revised or corrected bill with the correct value 
stack credit amounts applied within 45 days of the bill issue date.  NYSEG and RG&E customers 
will receive a $10 credit each month until their utility issues an accurate bill.  Customers also 
received this credit if they had not received accurate CDG credits on their bills for at least four 
months prior to the issuance of the Commission’s order establishing the rate plans for NYSEG 
and RG&E.  Those costs are to be borne by NYSEG’s and RG&E’s shareholders.   
Con Edison also provides customers with a $10 credit, which the Commission 
established in its most recent rate order for that utility.1067  Con Edison issues a monthly $10 
credit to customers who did not receive their CDG and non-CDG VDER bill credits within 60 
days of the initial bill issuance date.  The customer then receives the credit each month until their 
CDG credits are applied completely.  Additionally, the Con Edison rate order established a series 
of reporting requirements providing details of the various CDG programs within Con Edison’s 
service territory, including details on the established credit.1068  
Staff’s recommendation to assess a $20 customer bill credit is reasonable because 
it appropriately balances customer concerns with Central Hudson’s CDG program with 
prospective costs to the Company and encourages customer good will.  Full CDG billing

4. 
Discretionary Customer Credits 
Neither the Customer Experience Panel initial testimony, nor the SCSP’s initial 
testimony provided recommendations on the Company’s use of discretionary customer credits.  
The Company confirmed that it has guidelines for its customer-facing employees 
for offering discretionary credits and it has no plans to make changes to that policy in the rate 
year.1069  The Company also confirmed that it “is not aware of any specific reporting 
requirements associated with discretionary credits.”1070  The Company also stated that the 
discretionary credits show up on a customer’s bill as a miscellaneous adjustment, and the 
customer would be aware of the reason for the credit from their phone or email interaction with 
the Company.1071  The Company confirmed that discretionary credits have “historically been 
included in revenue requirements and are treated as such.”1072 
Staff is concerned with the Company’s recovery of these credits in rates and 
recommends that the Commission require that these costs not be borne by ratepayers.  
Furthermore, the Company could not provide historical data for these expenses beyond April 
2022,1073 which is concerning as Staff could not evaluate trends regarding this expense, nor could 
Staff review the difference on this expense prior to the Company’s SAP CIS transition in 
September 2021.

In its Customer Experience Panel’s initial testimony, the Company stated it 
launched its new SAP-based customer information system, or CIS, on September 1, 2021.1074

E.

The Customer Experience Panel’s initial testimony states “the Company has 
begun a very measured approached to restarting collection activities”1099 and further proposes 
additional collectors, contact center personnel, and billing department personnel to carry out

Company’s proposal for incremental FTEs and call volume overflow due to the Company’s 
inability to produce a detailed plan and firm timeline for resuming normal collections activities

activities.”1102,1103  The SCSP further described its analysis of the Company’s residential and

SCSP stated it is concerned with this level of arrears and the Company’s lack of detail in its 
resumption of collections activity and residential service terminations.  The SCSP also found the 
Company did not yet implement an automated collections process within its SAP CIS so 
collections functions, including the issuance of final termination notices, must be done

F. 
Extreme Heat Protections 
Neither the Customer Experience Panel’s initial testimony, nor the SCSP’s initial 
testimony provided recommendations on extreme cold or extreme heat protections.   
PULP provided a detailed description of the cold weather and extreme heat 
protections instituted at Central Hudson.1116  Although PULP recommended that the Company 
maintain its current cold weather policies,1117 PULP also proposed adjustments to the Company’s 
current hot weather policies.  Specifically, PULP recommended that the Commission lower the 
threshold for halting customer terminations for non-payment during extreme heat events.  In 
areas not classified as “Heat Islands,” PULP recommended lowering the threshold from a 
forecasted temperature and heat index of 93 degrees to a forecasted temperature and heat index 
of 90 degrees.  In areas classified as “Heat Islands,” PULP recommended establishing a new 
threshold of 85.1118   
Additionally, PULP recommended broadening the dates that terminations for non-
payment would be halted during extreme heat events to encompass one calendar day before, the 
day of the extreme heat event, and two calendar days after the heat event.1119  Noting that 
vulnerable customers rely on electricity to run their home air conditioners,1120 PULP contended 
that 90 degrees is the “threshold used to identify extreme heat conditions by federal government 
agencies,”1121and is also “the threshold temperature beyond which NOAA’s National Weather

Staff agrees with the Company’s proposed outreach budget aside from the

and education to be an integral part of a utility’s functions, especially with Central Hudson’s 
proposed resumption of residential service terminations and the implementation of monthly 
meter reading.  Effective customer outreach reduces customer confusion and anxiety about their 
utility.  When customers are informed enough to fully understand what they are being billed for, 
they may be less likely to complain or call with questions.  Additionally, increasing customer 
awareness about a utility’s efforts on various initiatives could have a positive impact on customer 
sentiment and help reduce the levels of customer dissatisfaction potentially caused by the SAP 
CIS transition.

initial testimony but did not propose any modifications.1127

would result in a more comprehensive and transparent report on annual economic development 
activities; and (4) provide the Company with the ability to make minor adjustments to its 
economic development programs on an annual basis by proposing those minor adjustments in its 
Annual Economic Development Report.1128  No intervening parties provided any testimony on 
economic development.

I. 
Credit Card Fees 
In the Central Hudson Customer Experience Panel’s initial testimony, the 
Company proposed to maintain the rate allowance and symmetrical deferral included in the 2021 
Rate Plan.1134  The SCSP’s initial testimony essentially agreed to continue the previous rate 
allowance and symmetric deferral.1135 The SCSP also proposed to continue the reporting 
established in the 2021 Rate Order with certain modifications to provide more information and 
transparency, including labeling the differences between “Vendor A” and “Vendor B” on the 
current quarterly reports.1136  PULP recommended the continuation of this socialization and 
stressed the importance of the no-fee model for low-income customers in particular, who should 
“continue to be afforded the ability to make payments by debit or credit cards without incurring 
transaction fees.”1137 
Staff recommends the Commission continue the current rate allowance and the 
symmetrical deferral mechanism and also adopts Staff’s proposed clarifications to the 
Company’s quarterly credit card reporting be adopted in order to increase transparency of these 
costs.

CDG Credit Reporting 
As stated in the CDG Credit section of this brief and detailed within the SCSP 
Panel’s initial testimony, Central Hudson had high levels of CDG customer bills that were 
delayed1149 or adjusted1150 frequently within a month.  Staff recommended two CDG-focused 
reporting requirements that would improve the monitoring of accurate CDG bills at Central 
Hudson.  The SCSP recommended that the Company file quarterly reports with the Secretary to 
the Commission that would detail the total number and percentage of CDG customer bills that 
were issued within specified timeframes, such as: (1) 30 days or less; (2) greater than 30 days, 
but less than or equal to 60 days; (3) greater than 60 days, but less than or equal to 90 days; (4) 
greater than 90 days, but less than or equal to 120 days; and (5) greater than 120 days, but less 
than or equal to 150 days.1151  The Company could not provide Staff with three years’ worth of 
granular data on the monthly number of CDG customers who were receiving three, four, five, 
six, or more than six adjusted bills within monthly timeframes.  However, the Company did 
provide the number of CDG customer accounts that were receiving multiple CDG adjusted 
bills1152 and the total of CDG adjusted bills1153 that were distributed to these accounts.  The 
second reporting requirement from the Panel recommended the Company begin to track the 
number of residential CDG customer bills that are adjusted within a billing cycle on a granular 
level, such as: (1) CDG customers who received one CDG bill; (2) CDG customers who received 
one CDG bill and one CDG adjusted bill; (3) CDG customers who received one CDG bill and

XI. 
MANAGEMENT AUDIT 
Public Service Law §66(19)(c) requires that “upon the application of a gas or 
electric corporation for a major change in rates…the Commission shall review that corporation’s 
Compliance with the directions and recommendations made previously by the Commission, as a 
result of the most recently completed management and operations audit.”  Since Central 
Hudson’s rates were last reset in the 2021 Rate Order, the Company has participated in the 
implementation of recommendations from two audits.  Accordingly, Staff addressed Central 
Hudson’s status in relation to two recently completed management audits, Case 16-M-0001 
(2016 Management Audit) and Case 21-M-0541 (2021 Management Audit).  Staff also 
addressed one operations audit completed during the time period covered by the 2021 Rate 
Order, Case 13-M-0449 (2013 Staffing Audit). 
A. 
2013 Staffing Audit 
On April 22, 2019, the Director of the Office of Accounting, Audits and Finance 
issued a letter to Central Hudson confirming that the Company had satisfactorily implemented all 
recommendations from Case 13-M-0449.1160  
Central Hudson indicated it had included approximately $2.4 million in the Rate 
Year related to the implementation of recommendations from the 2013 Staffing Audit.1161 
In testimony, Staff recommended removing these costs because the amounts did 
not align with the Company’s approved implementation plan in Case 13-M-0449.  In addition, 
Staff noted these costs were incurred well outside the implementation completion dates specified 
in the Company’s approved implementation plan.  Further, Staff noted that the Company did not

Oversight of Central Hudson (filed April 22, 2019). 
1161  Exhibit 331 (DPS-256).

file a material change request to update the implementation costs or timelines, as required by the 
Commission’s Order Approving Implementation Plans in Case 13-M-0449.1162  
In the rebuttal testimony of its Management Audit Panel, Central Hudson 
disagreed with Staff’s recommendation to remove these costs.  The Company clarified that its 
response to DPS-256 was incorrect.  Central Hudson stated that these follow-on costs were not 
tied to the initial implementation of the 2013 Staffing Audit recommendations and, as such, 
should not be removed.1163  The Company’s Management Audit Panel further clarified that its 
implementation of the 2013 Staffing Audit is deemed to be complete.1164 
Staff acknowledges the Company’s error in its response to DPS-256.  With 
Central Hudson’s latest clarification, Staff now agrees to forgo adjustments to the Rate Year 
revenue requirement related to the 2013 Staffing Audit.  However, after further review, Staff 
notes that its originally recommended adjustment for this item was already inadvertently omitted 
from its revenue requirement models for the Rate Year.  Accordingly, no further adjustments to 
revenue requirement are needed.  
B. 
2016 Management Audit 
On September 20, 2021, the Director of the Office of Accounting, Audits and 
Finance issued a letter to Central Hudson confirming the completion of the implementation of all 
audit recommendations from Case 16-M-0001.1165  The Company did not include costs for 
recovery associated with the 2016 Management in its revenue requirement for the Rate Year.  
C.

On March 16, 2022, the Commission selected Overland Consulting to perform a 
comprehensive management and operations audit of Central Hudson.  Overland conducted audit 
fieldwork from May through September 2022, which included extensive discovery and 
interviews of Company personnel to develop a draft audit report.  Overland provided its draft 
audit report to Central Hudson for fact verification and confidentiality review, and met with Staff 
and the Company to address any concerns identified during the Company’s review.  On April 20,

Central Hudson for fact verification and comment prior to the report’s finalization.  Central 
Hudson did not dispute Overland’s BCAs during its review of the draft report.

not reflected in Staff’s revenue requirements.  Staff is agreeing to forgo the imputation of capital-
related savings, but will reflect the O&M-related savings in its next revenue requirement update.
