In the initial testimony of its EAMs Panel, the Company proposed several EAMs, 
including Distributed Energy Resources (DER) Utilization; Electric Peak Reduction; EV 
Adoption; EV Supply, Equipment, and Infrastructure (EVSE&I); Direct Current (DC) Fast 
Charger; EVSE&I Level 2 Charger; Gas Peak Reduction; Load Factor for Residential Energy 
Intensity; Commercial Energy Intensity; and Load Factor.

In its initial testimony, Dutchess County expressed concern about increasing costs 
and testified that both the Company’s CLCPA proposals and its EAM proposals will only drive 
costs up further.1182  Dutchess County stated that, in order for EAMs to be successful, they must 
reduce direct out-of-pocket customer costs in Dutchess County.  They should not be concerned 
with worldwide macroeconomic costs, and any Company policy should state first and foremost

service possible.1183 
A. 
Disadvantaged Communities Energy Efficiency Benefits 
The Company proposed eliminating its four existing EE and/or BE Earnings 
Adjustment Mechanisms.  These include the Electric Share the Savings EAM, Gas Share the 
Savings EAM, Heat Pump Share the Savings EAM, and a Combined Gas and Electric Energy 
Efficiency Low‐to‐Moderate Income EAM.1184  Staff supported the proposed elimination of 
these EAMs in the Staff EAM Panel’s initial testimony,1185 citing a recent Order in which the 
Commission observed that EE programs have become a standard practice for the large investor-
owned utilities and then directed a pause to the development and institution of new EAMs tied to 
these programs.1186  As there is no dispute regarding the elimination of these EAMs, Staff 
submits that the Commission should approve the Company’s proposal on this issue. 
The Company further proposed two new EE EAMs: an electric Disadvantaged 
Community Energy Efficiency Benefits EAM and a gas Disadvantaged Community Energy 
Efficiency Benefits EAM, both of which would provide a financial incentive based on the 
percentage of spending by the Company’s electric and gas energy efficiency portfolios on 
efficiency projects located in disadvantaged communities as defined by the Climate Justice 
Working Group.1187  Staff opposed the proposal in testimony, citing the aforementioned pause to

B. 
Electric and Gas Peak Reduction 
For the Electric Peak Reduction EAM, the Company stated that the EAM 
incentivizes the Company to reduce its New York Independent System Operator, Inc. (NYISO) 
Zone G-J Locality Peak, which in turn reduces capacity costs that are passed onto customers.1191  
The Company proposed a corrective modification to this EAM that included “[a]dditional text in 
the performance and measurement sections clarifying that the G-J coincident load should be 
weather normalized to be consistent with the existing description of the EAM, as well as adding 
additional clarity on which years’ forecast should be used for each part of the calculation.”1192  
The Company did not propose any changes to its Gas Peak Reduction EAM.1193 
The testimony of the Staff EAMs Panel stated that the Gas Peak Reduction metric 
is measured based on: (a) the firm customer1194 peak demand day, in dekatherms (DTH) less the

The Staff EAMs Panel recommended the discontinuation of the Electric and Gas 
Peak Load Reductions EAMs.1196  The panel found that the attributes of the Gas and Electric 
Peak Reduction metrics can result in financial gains, not due to any of the Company’s actions, 
but rather because of unexpected downturns in the economy or other negative macroeconomic 
impacts.1197  This trend has been observed by Staff with respect to several other utilities with 
similar Electric or Gas Peak Reduction metrics, specifically Con Edison, but also KEDNY, 
KEDLI, NYSEG, and O&R.1198 
In its rebuttal testimony, the Company’s Earnings Adjustment Mechanisms Panel 
disagreed with Staff’s recommendation to eliminate the Electric and Gas Peak Reduction EAMs, 
observing that the REV Order states, “as a general matter, we reject the arguments of parties that 
would confine EAMs to items under direct control or strong influence of the utility, to the extent 
that ‘influence’ is interpreted as the implementation of specific approved program.”1199  The 
Company also stated that the Peak Reduction EAMs should not be discontinued because the 
achievement of these EAMs would result in significant benefits to customers.1200  Additionally, 
the Company testified that the demand response programs it implements, such as the 
Commercial System Relief Program (CSRP) and the Residential EV Managed Charging 
Program, can influence its peak demand by incentivizing customers to reduce their usage during 
peak times.1201 
At the evidentiary hearing, the Staff EAMs panel acknowledged that the CSRP is 
an electric demand response program run by Central Hudson which, if enough customer

demonstrably unsuccessful.1208  As noted above, the Company reports its operationally available 
CSRP load metric annually to the Department in Case 14-E-0423, and Central Hudson’s 
consistent decline in operationally available load demonstrates the diminishing influence of the 
CSR program on peak demand.1209 
For all of these reasons, Staff continues to recommend the elimination of the 
Electric and Gas Peak Reduction EAMs.

C. 
DER Photovoltaic 
In the initial testimony of its EAMs Panel, the Company stated the DER 
Utilization EAM “incentivizes the Company to collaborate with third parties to advance the use 
of DER resources in [its] service territory.”1210  The Company further stated that the DER 
Utilization EAM “provides incentives for increased DER penetration which will play a key role 
in helping New York State reach its clean energy goals as set forth in the CLCPA.”1211  The 
Company proposed updated targets for calendar years 2025, 2026, and 2027.1212 
The Staff EAMs Panel recommended restructuring the DER Utilization EAM by 
separating it into two separate EAMs: one for solar PV and one for Battery Energy Storage 
Systems (BESS).1213  The Staff EAM Panel developed the baseline for the Solar DER Utilization 
metric and the BESS DER Utilization metric on adjusted linear growth and utilized the 
Company’s share of the statewide solar PV and battery storage targets.1214  The Staff EAM Panel 
set minimum targets at 10% above the baseline level, midpoint targets at 25% above the 
baseline, and maximum targets at 50% above the baseline.1215 
In its rebuttal testimony, the Company agreed with Staff’s recommendation to 
separate the DER Utilization EAM into two separate EAMs: one for Solar DER Utilization and 
one for BESS DER utilization.1216  However, concerning the Solar DER Utilization EAM targets, 
the Company argued that all NY-Sun qualifying solar interconnected within its territory should 
count towards the Solar DER Utilization EAM target, and the EAM should not exclude those 
projects that exceed 5 MWs.1217  The Company argued, incorrectly, NYSERDA sets the solar PV 
cap for commercial and industrial NY-Sun qualifying projects at 7.5 MW.1218  In fact, the 
NYSERDA document cited by the Company in its rebuttal testimony sets the system size cap for

Staff’s proposal to separate the DER Utilization EAM into two separate EAMs,

measurement of the Company’s contribution to Commission and State policy goals, as the DER 
Utilization EAM addresses two separate initiatives.  The Commission established the goal of 
achieving 10 GW of solar production statewide by 2030 in Case 22-E-0629,1221 and Governor 
Hochul announced a 6 GW energy storage goal on December 28, 2022.1222  The testimony of 
Staff EAM Panel states that, as these are two separate policy goals, one for solar PV and one for 
BESS, it is more appropriate to have two separate EAM metrics to encourage the attainment of 
both, rather than combining them.1223  Furthermore, Staff’s proposal for the DER EAMs metrics 
ensures the proposed qualifying projects for both PV and BESS are capped at 5MW AC, which 
aligns with NY-Sun and the recent caps used for other utilities.1224  Staff therefore recommends 
that the Commission adopt Staff’s testimonial position regarding DER Utilization EAMs.

E. 
EV Adoption 
In its initial testimony, the Company stated that the EV Adoption EAM 
incentivizes it to reduce GHG emissions by facilitating greater penetration of EVs.1225  The 
Company did not propose any changes to the structure of this EAM but did propose updated 
2025, 2026, and 2027 EV Adoption targets and assumptions about emissions reductions per 
vehicle.1226 
In the testimony of the Staff EAMs Panel, Staff proposed modifications to the 
Company’s development of the EV Adoption EAM.1227  The first proposed modification 
includes an additional 10% to the Company’s proposed minimum incremental battery electric 
EV (BEV) and plug-in hybrid EV (PHEV) targets.1228  Second, the Staff EAM Panel proposed 
new minimum and maximum EV adoption targets by using the New York State Climate Action 
Council Scoping Plan, Appendix G: Integration Analysis Technical Supplement to determine the 
Company’s share of new BEV and PHEV registrations in its territory.1229  Third, the Staff EAM 
Panel proposed lower basis points than previously recommended for the attainment of this EAM.  
Specifically, Staff recommended an EAM of 2 BPs if the Company obtains the minimum target; 
4 BPs if the midpoint target is met; and 8 BPs if the maximum target is achieved.1230 
The rebuttal testimony of the Company’s Earnings Adjustment Panel did not 
agree with Staff’s recommendations, stating the proposed targets are unrealistic.1231  The 
Earnings Adjustment Panel testified that the adoption rates within the Appendix G forecast are 
based on the state’s 2030 Zero Emission Vehicle Memorandum of Understanding targets, which 
are aspirational and do not necessarily account for actual market conditions.1232  Furthermore, the 
Panel stated that the targets for the EV Adoption EAM far exceed what Staff considers superior 
performance and exceptional achievement and that the proposed EV Adoption EAMs targets are

Staff’s proposal to adjust the EV adoption EAM is appropriate and a better option 
specifically because the development of the EAM is derived from the New York State Climate 
Action Council Scoping Plan that was adopted in December 2022 and outlines actions needed for 
New York to achieve 70% renewable energy by 2030; 100% zero-emission electricity by 2040; a 
40% reduction in statewide GHG emissions from 1990 levels by 2030; an 85% reduction from 
1990 level by 2050; and net-zero emissions statewide by 2050.1236  Specifically, Appendix G to 
the Scoping Plan provides the framework, assumptions, details, and pathways required to 
achieve GHG mitigation to meet the goals set out by the Scoping Plan.1237  
The Staff EAM Panel moreover set targets using the principle that EAMs are 
something to be earned, not something that is owed to the Company.  EAMs should be a stretch 
target and should motivate the Company with rewards if they achieve certain outputs that support 
state policy. They are not to reward a business-as-usual scenario.1238  
For these reasons, Staff recommends that the Commission adopt its testimonial 
position concerning the EV Adoption EAM.

XIV. 
REVENUE ALLOCATION AND RATE DESIGN 
A. 
ECOS Studies 
The Company filed three Embedded Cost of Service (ECOS) studies for each of 
its electric and gas businesses.  The three studies consist of: (1) a historical study based on the 
calendar year 2021 (Historical Study), (2) a proforma study based on its Rate Year forecasts 
(Proforma Study), and (3) a hypothetical study based on 2021, that excludes commodity and 
surcharge related expenses (Hypothetical Study).  The Company used the results of both the 
Historical Study and Proforma Study as the basis for their revenue allocation.  According to the 
Company, the use of both studies “allow[s] for a comparison of estimated realized to estimated 
expected rates of return” based on both current and previously authorized rates.1290  While the 
Company did not use the Hypothetical Study for its revenue allocation purposes, the Company 
stated that it does provide a valuable connection between the bundled commodity and delivery 
historical study and its delivery only pro forma study.1291    
For each study, the Company allocated its rate base, expenses, and revenues by 
performing the basic steps of functionalization, classification, and allocation.1292  The ECOS 
studies calculated service class specific rates of return (ROR) which were compared to the 
system average ROR.  The measured deviation from the system ROR was then used to inform 
revenue allocation and rate design, which is discussed in more detail in Section XIV.E (Electric 
Revenue Allocation).1293   
The Company also filed an Allocated Cost of Service (ACOS) for its electric 
business, in compliance with the Commission’s March 16, 2022 Order issued in Case 15-E-0751, 
which directed the Company to produce ACOS studies on a Federal Energy Regulatory 
Commission Account Basis.1294   Additionally, pursuant to the Commission’s October 13, 2023

For purposes of revenue allocation, the Company proposed using two ECOS 
studies, the Historical Study and the Proforma Study, to provide RORs for each service class.1296  
MI recommended disregarding the Historical Study and instead utilizing only the Proforma 
Study.1297  MI argued that the Historical Study reflects the rates in effect during the Historic Test 
Year and not current rates, and that the Company’s requested revenue requirement is based on 
Rate Year circumstances.1298  MI further argued that “any direct comparisons of the class RORs 
between the Historical Study and the Proforma Study may be unduly influenced by the 
circumstances specific to each test year and the degree to which current delivery rates deviate 
from costs on a going-forward basis.”1299    
Staff agrees with the methodology that the Company used in its ECOS studies.  
Staff found the studies to be a reasonable presentation, and performed in a similar manner as in 
the Company’s previous rate proceedings.1300  Staff also agrees with the use of both studies in 
these proceedings, as proposed by the Company.  Historical and proforma ECOS studies each 
have different advantages, and each provide different valuable insights.  In Staff’s initial 
testimony, Staff agreed with the Company’s methodology to use both Historical and Proforma 
Studies as the bases of its electric and gas revenue allocation.1301 While costs, revenues, and

2. 
Property Taxes 
Central Hudson functionalized electric property tax expense in the same 
proportion as land and land rights in its electric ECOS studies, and gas property tax expense on 
net plant in service in its gas ECOS studies.  MI proposed functionalizing electric property tax 
using net plant, in place of the Company’s functionalization approach of using the accounts for 
land and land rights.1303  Additionally, MI noted that the Company used net plant for its 
functionalization of property taxes in its gas ECOS studies.1304  Staff agrees that the treatment of 
property taxes should be functionalized in the same manner for both the electric and gas ECOS 
studies.1305  Since property tax obligations are determined by the Reproduction Cost New less 
Depreciation plus Land, and net plant in service serves as a reasonable proxy for Reproduction 
Cost New less Depreciation plus Land, using net plant to functionalize property taxes within the 
electric and gas ECOS studies is a reasonable and appropriate approach.1306   Accordingly, the 
Commission should adopt Staff’s recommendation. 
3. 
Electric System Control/Load Dispatching 
Central Hudson proposed to functionalize system control, load dispatching, and 
other power supply, which are included in Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) 
Account Nos. 556 and 557, as follows: 30% to the Merchant Supply Function and 70% to the 
production function.  All of the production function costs were then classified entirely to energy.

MI proposed that Accounts 556 and 557 should instead be classified based on the production

Allocation Manual (NARUC Manual), recommends these production accounts be allocated on

Report, the Company’s two gas turbine units have indicated they will be out of service, and the 
NYISO’s generator status report dated December 13, 2023 indicates that the two gas turbine 
units are slated to retire in 2024, leaving only the Company’s hydro units.1309  Because the 
remaining production assets owned by Central Hudson would be mostly hydro-related facilities, 
which produce mostly energy benefits, it is appropriate to keep the allocation of these accounts 
based on energy.  Based on the foregoing, Staff recommends that the Commission reject MI’s 
proposal, and adopt Staff’s position.

B. 
MCOS Studies 
The marginal cost of service study as it relates to the Excelsior Jobs Program rates 
is addressed in Section XIV.J. 
C. 
Electric Loss Factor 
The electric loss factor is determined by the percentage of difference between the 
total electricity received in the system and total metered electricity, divided by total electricity 
received.  From this percentage, the Company will derive a factor of adjustment (FOA).1328  The 
electric system FOA is determined based on the electric loss factor calculated as the most recent 
36-month average.  The current system FOA, based on the 36-months ending September 2021, is 
1.0112.  As of the Company’s testimony, using the 36-months ending May 2023 would produce

D. 
Lost and Unaccounted For Gas 
Lost and unaccounted for gas (LAUF) is measured as the difference between total 
gas received into the system and total metered gas, divided by the total gas received.1330  The 
Company’s current methodology consists of determining an FOA,1331 which is calculated based 
on a five-year average and updated annually every twelve months ending August 31.  This 
methodology has been in place since Central Hudson’s 2017 gas rate case, Case 17-G-0460.1332  
Additionally, the Company measures its performance against the FOA by using dead bands,1333 
which were established in Case 14-G-0319 and will remain fixed until modified by the 
Commission.1334  The Company did not propose any modifications or revisions to how it 
accounts for its LAUF.1335   Staff agrees with continuing the Company’s LAUF methodology 
and FOA calculations in these proceedings.   
E. 
Electric Revenue Allocation and Rate Design 
1. 
Revenue Allocation 
Revenue allocation is the process of apportioning a revenue increase to service 
classes.  The Company’s intent behind its proposed revenue allocation is to move each service 
class within 15% of the system average rate of return.1336  In these proceedings, the Company 
proposed utilizing the same constraints presented in its prior rate case,1337 which limit the 
allocation to classes with RORs that are 15% below or above the system average ROR, to plus or 
minus 25% of the otherwise proscribed increase.1338  Also consistent with prior proceedings, the 
allocation to a service class would be modified only if both the Historical and Proforma Studies

MI recommended changes to revenue allocation, such that classes earning an

factors for those classes performing above the system ROR, sufficiently move classes with 
above-average RORs towards the system average.  Similarly, constraints are in place to allocate 
a higher portion of the revenue requirement to those classes that have been under-earning.  
Conversely, allocating zero revenues to any class would be inequitable and would shift the 
revenue requirement burden to other classes.  For the foregoing reasons, Staff recommends that 
the Company reject MI’s proposed changes to electric revenue allocation.

Service Classification No. 13 Transmission would increase 48%, while volumetric rates would

and/or at too great a magnitude, such as with MI’s proposal, could alter system conditions and 
consequently the cost to serve other customers.  Conversely, implementing a more gradual 
change to balance between customer charges and volumetric rates provides customers time to 
understand how such changes impact customer behavior.  Staff’s recommended customer charge 
adjustments strikes a reasonable balance between properly reflecting the costs for Central 
Hudson to serve individual electric customers, and mitigating against significant bill impacts.  
Accordingly, Staff recommends that the Commission adopt its customer charge adjustments. 
The Company also proposed a new Low Power Attachment Program rate option

ECOS studies by excluding non-applicable costs related to low power attachment devices such

forecasted, any revenues produced by customers taking service under this low power attachment 
offering would contribute to the Rate Year RDM targets for Service Classification No. 2 Non-
Demand.  For those reasons, Staff supports the Low Power Attachment Program rate option 
proposed by the Company.

F. 
Gas Revenue Allocation and Rate Design 
1. 
Revenue Allocation 
For gas revenue allocation, both the Historical and Proforma Studies determined 
Service Classification No. 11 Transmission exceeded the system average ROR by 15%, and as 
such, that customer class received an allocation factor of 75%.1355  All other service classes 
received unmodified allocations of 100%, plus the distributed remainder of the Service 
Classification No. 11 Transmission allocation.1356  Additionally, pursuant to the 2021 Rate 
Order, Central Hudson’s current base delivery rates include a profit imputation of $3.2 million 
estimated to be received from interruptible sales.1357  The Company proposed to continue its 
existing $3.2 million interruptible imputation based on the most recent two year average ending 
March 2023 historic profit levels.1358 This interruptible imputation gets offset in rate design.1359   
Staff recommends a slight modification to the interruptible imputation.  Bundling 
the imputation and revenue requirement together results in an allocation of a portion of the 
imputation to electric generation facility customers taking gas service under Service 
Classification No. 11 who does not receive an offset in rate design.1360  As such, it is appropriate 
to allocate the interruptible imputation separately from the revenue allocation, and exclude these 
electric generation customers from both the imputation allocation and reduction in rate 
design.1361  In its rebuttal testimony, the Company’s Forecasting and Rates Panel modified its 
treatment of interruptible imputations in concurrence with Staff’s position.1362  The Commission 
should adopt this adjustment to the interruptible imputation as both reasonable and appropriate.

2. 
Rate Design 
Gas rates are generally comprised of a minimum monthly charge that includes the 
first few therms consumed, and then a block or multiple blocks with different rates depending on 
usage.  These usage blocks can include a tail block for higher usage with the lowest per therm 
rate.  In its most recent rate case, the Company began the process of flattening its block rate 
structure, with the goal to achieve uniform volumetric rates within five years.1364  The 
Company’s gas Service Classification No. 6 currently offers a high-volume discount rate for 
customers whose annual usage is 50,000 ccf or more.  This is a singular volumetric rate that does 
not utilize a block structure, and was excluded from the Company’s original block rate flattening 
plan.1365   
In these proceedings, the Company proposed changes to the customer charges for 
all customers taking service under Service Classifications Nos. 1, 2, 6, 11, 12 and 13.  The 
Company’s proposed customer charge adjustments all move towards the customer costs 
indicated in the gas ECOS, except for Service Classifications Nos. 11 Transmission and 11 
Distribution, which move directly to the customer costs indicated in the ECOS studies.1366   The 
Company also proposed continuing its five-year block rate phase out plan as initially proposed in 
the Company’s past rate case, with the Rate Year serving as year four out of five.1367  
Additionally,  the Company proposed to incorporate the Service Classification No. 6 high 
volume discount rate, which is applicable to customers whose annual use is 50,000 ccf or more, 
into its flattening plan, and proposing to phase out the discounted high volume use rate over two 
years.1368

No. 6 high volume discount rate in two years, that proposal could produce rate shock and rate

block rate structure, produces more gradual increases to the volumetric rate than under a two-
year phase out.  As the Service Classification No. 6 high volume customers were purposefully 
excluded from the original flattening plan, the slope between the flattening block rates and the

volumetric rates) is the total flattening required to produce a singular unified rate for all 
customers under Service Classification Nos. 2, 6, and 13.  Attempting to close that gap in a 
shorter period than was applied to other block rates would result in a rapid, and inequitable, 
increase in rates.  Further, Staff is concerned that a more abrupt and less gradual change to the 
Service Classification No. 6 discounted rate could result in decreased usage by affected 
customers, or possibly result in customers switching from firm service to interruptible service.  
As the usage and revenues associated with these high-volume customers are forecasted in the 
Rate Year, and are included in the class RDM, it is in the best interest of all other commercial 
customers in Service Classification Nos. 2, 6, and 13 to retain these customers on firm service.

G. 
Danskammer Revenues 
The Company proposed in its initial testimony to include a credit related to 
Danskammer revenues that would be passed back to customers at the end of the Rate Year or 
beginning on July 1, 2025.1389  Based on the concern that completely removing the existing $1.5 
million Danskammer imputation would result in an increase to the revenue requirement, in its 
initial testimony, Staff recommended keeping the $1.5 million imputation of Danskammer 
revenues, as was established in the Company’s 2021 Rate Plan.1390  The Company, in its rebuttal 
testimony, stated that if the Commission were to adopt the continuation of the Danskammer 
imputation, as well as the continuation of its current deferral mechanism, the imputation level 
should be updated to reflect more current levels of Danakmmer revenues, which is around $1.0 
million.1391  The Company’s provided updated Danskammer revenues which reflected a little 
over $900,000 for the 2023 calendar year.1392  Staff agrees that the level of Danskammer 
imputation should be updated, and agrees that lowering the imputation to $1.0 million would 
better reflect the expectations of Danskammer related revenue in the Rate Year.  As such, the 
Commission should adopt a continuation of the Danskammer imputation as recommended by 
Staff, subject to adjusting the imputation level to $1.0 million.

I. 
Battery Storage Rates 
It its direct testimony, KCE voiced its concerns for bulk storage facilities taking 
service within Central Hudson service territory and expressed confusion on which rates would be 
applicable to such facilities.1394  KCE noted a discrepancy between Niagara Mohawk and the 
much higher Central Hudson rates, which assume the applicable standby rates based on each 
Company’s most recently filed ACOS studies and applied to a 200 MW stand-alone bulk storage 
facility.1395  Central Hudson contends that the specific stand-alone storage facility KCE 
references, which is greater than 5 MW and whose intent is to provide wholesale service to the 
NYISO, would be subject to the rates defined in the Company’s Wholesale Distribution Service 
(WDS) tariff which has yet to be filed with FERC.1396 
In Staff’s understanding, FERC Order No. 841 precludes the application of the 
Commission-jurisdictional tariff delivery rates upon standalone bulk energy storage projects 
buying and selling into the wholesale market.1397  At the present time, Central Hudson has not 
yet filed its WDS tariff with FERC.  As such, there is no rate that could be applied to the stand-
alone storage facility as described by KCE.  For large stand-alone storage facilities that take part 
in the wholesale market, the applicable rates applied to these customers would be defined in the 
WDS tariff and such customers would not be subject to delivery rates that fall under the 
Commission-jurisdictional tariff.

Buyback customers is also reasonable and consistent with the directives of the Standby/Buyback 
Order.   The Company further stated that it modified the gas RDM recovery period due to recent 
fluctuations of RDM credits and surcharges over the past 3.5 years under the semi-annual

surcharge that it has historically experienced and should be adopted for the Rate Year.  
Accordingly, Staff recommends that the Commission adopt the Company’s proposed electric and 
gas RDMs.  
EJP Rates 
The Company further proposed to update its rates for electric and gas Excelsior 
Jobs Program (EJP) participants.  The rates would reflect the marginal cost of providing service, 
for which the Company used its results of its filed marginal cost of service (MCOS) study to 
inform the EJP rates.1404   In its direct testimony, the Staff Forecasting Panel stated the Company 
should not update its EJP rates to correspond with the Company’s MCOS study in light of the 
ongoing proceeding in Case 19-E-0283, which the Commission instituted for purposes of 
developing a consistent MCOS study methodology for all New York utilities.  Instead, the Staff 
Forecasting Panel recommended the Company continue to rely on the $14.55 per kW/year 
estimate for marginal costing purposes pursuant to the Commission’s April 18, 2019 Order 
Regarding Value Stack Compensation in Case 15-E-0751, until the Commission adopts a 
consistent MCOS methodology in Case 19-E-0283.1405  In rebuttal, the Company disagreed with 
Staff, stating that while they shared concerns regarding the ongoing proceeding in Case 19-E-
0283, and Commission action in that proceeding could result in a potential change to the MCOS 
and resulting EJP rates, the Company still supported its use of the MCOS as the basis for 
developing its EJP rates, which was the accepted method approved in its current rate plan.1406  
Given that the Company’s proposed EJP rates, based on the relatively higher MCOS study 
estimates, are still lower than current rates, Staff deems it reasonable to utilize the Company’s 
proposed rates in the context of a one-year rate case absent any alternative directive from the 
Commission, since MCOS estimates are used to set the price floor, and the proposed EJP rate 
increase still represents a discount relative to current rates.  However, should the Commission act

Customer Billing 
In its initial filing, the Company proposed several changes to its tariffs regarding 
billing. These changes included redlined tariff amendments to electric tariff leaves 54 and 55 and 
gas tariff leaf 25 that replaced references to customer meter reading submissions through a post 
card to the Company, with language that a customer can submit the reading on its website.  The 
proposed tariff amendments also state, “By December 31, 2024, meters of customers will 
ordinarily be read by the Company on a monthly basis.”1407 
In addition to agreeing with the Company’s proposed tariff modifications, the 
Staff Policy Panel recommended additional tariff language related to estimated bills and adjusted 
bills.1408  The Staff Policy Panel stated that the issues experienced after the Company’s SAP CIS 
transition in September 2021 had demonstrated that the estimated billing procedures previously 
approved by the Commission, in Case 21-M-0045,1409 were ultimately too vague.  The Staff 
Policy Panel then proposed various clarifications to the Company’s electric and gas tariff leaves, 
which are provided in Exhibit__(SPP-3).1410 These changes included (1) setting a cadence for 
actual meter reading and estimated meter readings, aside from certain exceptions; (2) requiring 
the rendering of bills for each regular cycle billing period; (3) adding the SCSP’s proposed 
Estimated Bill Credit of $20 to the customer for each estimated meter read, aside from reads 
where the customer prevents access; (4) including definitions and parameters for adjusted bills; 
(5) adding a provision stating the Company would accept a meter reading electronically 
submitted by the customer; and, (6) adding the SCSP’s proposed Adjusted Bill Credit of $20 for 
each adjusted bill.

As noted in the Staff Policy Panel’s testimony, PSL §39 provides that a utility 
may render an estimated bill to residential customers if the utility’s procedure has been approved 
by the Commission and the bill clearly indicates it is based on an estimated reading.  The Staff 
Policy Panel also stated “Sections 11.13(a)(1) and (h) of the Commission’s regulations, Title 16 
of the New York Codes, Rules and Regulations, which are applicable to residential customers, 
allow a utility to ‘render an estimated bill for any billing period if: the estimated bill is calculated 
in accordance with a procedure approved by the commission and clearly states that it is based on 
an estimated reading’ and ‘may establish other reasonable procedures designed to reduce the 
number of estimated bills.’”1411  While the Commission did approve the Company’s estimated 
billing procedures, the Commission noted several concerns: “(1) lack of studies and analysis; (2) 
a reduction in the number of estimating methods; (3) using estimated bills to create future bill 
estimates; and, (4) lack of a set date in the petition noting when the new CIS will go-live and 
require the procedures to change.”1412  Staff maintains that the Commission’s initial concerns 
may not have been unfounded due to the report issued by the Department’s Office of 
Investigations and Enforcement in Case 22-M-0645 on the Company’s billing following its SAP 
CIS transition.  Staff proposed the tariff modifications as a first step in remedying these 
estimation issues; however, the Staff Policy Panel recommended the Commission require the 
Company to submit tariff amendments that incorporate the conversion to monthly meter reading 
“if and when a resolution is reached” in Case 22-M-0645.1413 
The Company stated in the rebuttal testimony of the Forecasting and Rates Panel 
that the “practice of issuing an estimated or adjusted bill is contemplated and allowed under New 
York State Public Service Law.”1414  The Company also argued that the Staff Policy Panel’s 
proposed tariff modification regarding “staffing failures” is “overly broad” and does not account 
for storms or other abnormal operating conditions that would require meter readers to perform 
other roles.1415  The Company addressed the proposed tariff amendments regarding the Estimated

Bill Credits and Adjusted Bill Credits as a whole with the SCSP’s proposals, and those are 
addressed under those respective sections of this brief.  
Staff recommends the Commission adopt Staff’s proposed tariff amendments, as 
these changes would clarify the Company’s tariff and increase transparency through written 
definitions and clear language.  Staff’s initial intent behind explicitly stating that “computer 
billing system or staffing failure(s)” were not circumstances outside of the Company’s 
control,1416 was to safeguard customers against detrimental impacts should the Company 
experience future issues regarding the Company’s billing system, or from  potential poor 
resource planning on behalf of the Company (e.g., attrition, poorly timed training), as these are 
circumstances within the Company’s control.  For example, while a sudden, extreme weather 
event would not be within the Company’s control, the Company’s planning of resources before, 
during, and after an event is within its control.  In the event the Company needs to divert meter 
reading resources during a weather event, for example, the Company should plan to make up for 
any necessary actual meter readings before or after said event, or increase customer outreach to 
encourage customers to submit meter readings electronically.  Additionally, the Staff Policy 
Panel’s recommended changes do include the following language “unless the existence of 
circumstances beyond the control of the utility made an actual reading of the meter extremely 
difficult consistent with PSL §39.”1417 
For those reasons, the Commission should adopt Staff’s proposed tariff 
amendments.
